After closing: 27 mutations, 27 caught. | test-engineer, sweeping their own work and publishing the survivors | Closed - and the shape is the finding |
| F-70 | **The mutation sweep is deliberately not in CI, and the reasoning is worth keeping.** Its author: *"A mutation harness that runs in CI becomes a thing to satisfy rather than a thing to learn from, and its value is in the round that fails."* It is recorded as a technique and a record in `test-plan.md` and `budget-harness.md` instead. This is the counterpart to F-40 — some properties are best enforced structurally, and some are best left as a practice that must actually be performed. | test-engineer | Standing principle |
| F-71 | **"Well guarded against flattering itself" is not "the numbers are good".** The harness now pins its statistic, refuses a non-canonical vault, tests its callers rather than only its guards, and prints `median of 3 wall-clock runs` rather than a bare figure. None of that is evidence about the architecture. Raised by the harness's own author for the gate report: **the first real figure is still the first real figure.** | test-engineer, unprompted | Standing - carry into the gate report |
| F-72 | **`quick_check` signals corruption two different ways, and an implementation defensive against one is wrong about the other.** It *raised* — "database disk image is malformed" — on a one-page truncation, and *returned non-`ok` rows* on a sub-page cut of a different database. So the natural implementation (read the rows) misses the raising case, and the defensive one (catch the error) misses the row case. §3.4 now specifies treating both as the same outcome. **Neither party's measurement contained this**; it only appeared because two people measured the same property on different fixtures and disagreed. That is the second time on this stack that the disagreement produced the finding — the first being the one-page threshold itself, visible only because one used a percentage and the other a byte count. | architect and code-reviewer, from conflicting results | Closed - the calling convention is in §3.4 |
| F-73 | **Reaching for a second argument that turns out weaker than the one you already had.** Its author, striking their own supporting claim for the second time in this spike: *"In a design document that's a bad habit — extra support makes a conclusion look better while making it easier to overturn."* The `IndexFault` decision always rested on the 4 KB mid-file overwrite plus the 1,150 ms cost, both reproduced independently; the withdrawn `quick_check` generalisation added apparent weight and a new way to be wrong. Recorded beside F-32, which is the same habit caught the same way. | architect, self-caught twice | Standing principle |
| F-74 | **Five unguarded paths in serat-index — the largest cluster outside B2, on the crate that produces the NFR-PRF-04 number.** All 39 tests pass through every one: `EndBulkLoad` no longer draining before rebuilding FTS; `BeginBulkLoad` no longer draining before dropping triggers; `ResolveLinks` no longer draining first; the deferred command dropped instead of held; and `mark_ready` running *before* ANALYZE and link resolution. **None is a live defect** — the code does the right thing in every case. The first three are invisible for one reason worth understanding: `rebuild()` flushes before `end_bulk_load`, so the actor's internal drains are never reached from the only caller — correct defence-in-depth guarding a hazard nothing demonstrates. The deferred slot is entered by no test. And `mark_ready` last, which the code comment calls the thing that makes a crash mid-rebuild recoverable, is pinned for *where* by the structural test but not for *when*. Not blocked, because I4 is correct and the queue is deep — but to be closed **before I5 builds on the actor**, not after. | code-reviewer, by mutation | **Two closed, three corrected — see F-85.** The deferred slot and `mark_ready`'s ordering are now reachable and mutation-verified: dropping the deferred command fails 4 of 5 new tests, and running `mark_ready` early fails the fifth. The deferred slot's test forces the inner drain to always have work waiting — a queue of one, twenty barriers fired into a burst of 400, all of which must be answered rather than return `WriterStopped` from an actor running perfectly well. `mark_ready`'s *when* is pinned by consequence rather than ordering: at the moment a rebuild is least recoverable, `inspect()` must say `RebuildInterrupted`, because if it ran earlier it would say `Ready` and a crash there leaves a database that opens and silently never updates search. |
| F-75 | **The struck 25-minute figure survives in two I4 code comments, and `rebuild.rs` states it as a measurement** (`rebuild.rs:131`, `writer.rs:138`) — so the number its own author withdrew is now asserted **more strongly in code than it ever was in the document**. Same propagation as the four occurrences caught in B4. This is the concrete cost of F-73's habit: a withdrawn figure does not stay withdrawn unless someone chases it into every place it was quoted. | code-reviewer | Open - rust-core-dev, before I4 merges |
| F-76 | **§2.2's flat test now has four exceptions and names one.** I4 adds three actor commands carrying non-`()` reply channels, which as written fails "if a message needs a reply, the channel is a lie and you have a mutex with extra steps". The design is not wrong — these are lifecycle barriers, and §2.2 already carves out `flush()` — but the carve-out names a single function and there are now four. **Architect's sentence to widen, not rust-core-dev's code to change**: the rule should distinguish a barrier from a request, since a barrier legitimately needs a reply and a request does not. | code-reviewer | **Ruled: the rule now names the category.** The widened test turns on one question — **would this message exist if the caller did not need to wait?** A *barrier* exists only because of the wait: its reply means "the state you asked for has been reached", the caller owns the sequence, and the next step is invalid until the previous landed. It may carry a value provided that is a fact about what the barrier finished rather than an answer computed on the caller's behalf, which lets `EndBulkLoad` return a count without becoming a request. A *request* would exist anyway and the reply is the point of sending it — still forbidden, and per-operation completion handles with it, "the same thing wearing a smaller hat". Two limits stop the category swallowing the rule: **no barrier on the save path** or anything a keystroke waits for, and **a barrier is a phase boundary**, so one appearing in the middle of ordinary work is a request that has been renamed. |
| F-77 | **A number must carry its confounds in the same sentence, or not be reported.** Its author, accounting for F-75: the 25-minute figure was published, copied into two code comments and quoted to the owner twice, and then withdrawn a day later — **"withdrawing a number does not un-copy it."** The failure was not the measurement but the timing: it should have been marked as an abandoned run *at the moment it was abandoned*, rather than described in a report and corrected when challenged. **An observation with a caveat two messages later has already propagated.** This is the practical form of F-73 and the reason F-75 exists. | architect, accounting for their own | Standing principle |
| F-78 | **If T2's numbers do not materialise, D4 closes nothing and §1.2's three assumptions stay open — and that is the correct thing to publish**, not something to work around. Sharpened by its author: **the failure mode for the next phase is P1 proceeding as though P0 had validated the three structures it was built to test.** Better to publish "we do not know" with the reasons than leave §1.2 reading as though it had been checked. | architect | Standing - governs the gate report |
| F-79 | **The gates had no opinion about a new dependency, and writing the report is what caught it.** `rayon` was added to serat-index; `cargo test --workspace`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` and `cargo fmt --all --check` all passed. Nothing in the repository had a view. The dependency is correct — §2.2 specifies the parse pool — but **the check that would have said so did not exist, and its author noticed only while writing the hand-over report and listing the dependency.** Recorded because it is evidence about a verification mechanism nobody planned: the narrative report caught what three gates and a reviewer's queue did not, and it caught it because writing down what you did forces you to enumerate it. | rust-core-dev, self-reported precisely | Closed by F-61's forbid-list; the lesson stands |
| F-80 | **F-57 paid off and the size of the payoff is measurable.** I4 deliberately did not couple to the unmerged I2, so when F-29b's richer `SourceEntry` landed on main, the rebase cost **one line in `rebuild.rs` plus a test stub**, and slightly more in I5 where `parse_chunk` takes `&[SourceEntry]`. Coupled to the unmerged branch, the same change would have arrived as a rebase conflict across three branches instead of one line in each. | rust-core-dev | Closed - the practice is vindicated |
| F-81 | **F-69 recurring inside B4's own fix for F-69.** `assert_scale_independent` — the 20% tolerance implementing §3.4's `t_visible` rule — is well tested at three boundaries, but its only caller, `scale_check()` in `prf01-cold-start.rs`, can be short-circuited with nothing going red. The guard is tested; nothing asks it. It is also **the only function still living in a binary rather than the library**, so the fix is the move its author already made three times in the same commit. CI does run the path — there is no `--skip-scale` in the workflow — so it is live rather than theoretical. Not held for it: one function, in a pattern its author has internalised. | code-reviewer, by mutation on the approving pass | Open - test-engineer, one function |
| F-82 | **F-69 is the more useful of the two framings, because it survives a code review.** The reviewer's assessment: unlike F-58, where the setup silently fails to reach the path, F-69's shape leaves **the guard right there with a test beside it** — so a reader sees a tested guard and moves on. | F-83 | **A mutation only tells you something if you did not write the test with that mutation in mind.** The methodological rule behind B4, from the reviewer's read of its author's decision to discard a 16-of-16 result as "proof only that I can hit a target I painted". Belongs in `budget-harness.md` beside the technique, because the technique without this rule produces a number that certifies itself. | code-reviewer, generalising test-engineer's call | Standing principle |
| F-84 | **An exact-string replacement that *succeeds* on a prefix is the one failure mode that instrument cannot catch.** §3.4's `quick_check` paragraph was replaced by matching its opening; the match succeeded, the tail stayed attached, and the section shipped to main at `4f5ed03` ending with a fragment whose antecedent had been deleted — restating an argument two paragraphs earlier in weaker terms. Its author's own diagnosis: exact-string editing is used precisely so a bad match fails loudly, and this one did not fail. **The fix is to match whole paragraphs rather than prefixes, not to add a process.** The reviewer's reason for caring is the part worth keeping: a paragraph ending by restating its own earlier point more weakly is one a reader skims — and skimming is how the "detected by nothing" claim survived as long as it did. | architect, self-diagnosed; found by code-reviewer | Fixed on branch; **the defect is on main** until it merges |
| F-85 | **Three of F-74's five paths are unreachable, and writing the suggested tests would have produced tests that pass for the wrong reason.** Checked rather than assumed: every iteration of the writer loop ends in a `commit`, and the three drain arms are reached either as the first command of an iteration or from `deferred`, so `pending` is empty both ways. A probe asserting `pending.is_empty()` at each **never fired across all 44 tests**, and removing the drains passes everything. So a test there would have been the exact class this review round is about. The substitute: **assert the invariant where it holds** — `debug_assert!` at each arm, so a future loop change that lets work arrive undrained fails loudly — and a comment saying the drains are *currently unreachable* rather than implying they are load-bearing. The drains stay, because they are correct if that ever stops being true. **This is a correction to a review finding rather than a fix of it**, offered as such by the person who received it. | rust-core-dev, correcting code-reviewer with evidence | **Adjudicated: the analysis is upheld, the substitute is not.** The reviewer re-derived the unreachability rather than accepting it and confirmed it — the proposed tests would have entered the arms with nothing to drain and proved nothing. **But the invariant then asserted is false and the assert fires** — see F-104. |
| F-86 | **NFR-PRF-01 IS MEASURED AND IT MISSES - and not because of Serat.** Release build, 20 spawns per figure: **t0 to window visible p95 1088 ms against a 400 ms budget (2.7x over)**, t0 to vault usable p95 1094 ms against 1000 ms. The median split decides what anyone can do about it: 57 ms loader and WebView2 DLLs, 23 ms Tauri runtime, **619 ms inside `WebviewWindowBuilder::build`**, 100 ms to first painted frame, 5 ms to usable. **Serat's own main-thread work is about 28 ms of 810.** The budget is consumed by WebView2 environment and controller creation, before any Serat code that matters, and no design of ours moves it. Section 3.4 budgets that whole phase at 400 ms *including* our own work. The question for Q1 is therefore not "can we make Serat faster" but **"is 400 ms achievable on Tauri v2 at all, and if not what is the right number"** - which bears on C-07. | tauri-app-dev, measured | Open - architect, product-analyst at Q1 |
| F-87 | **Section 3.4's load-bearing property was false, and was proven so by measurement rather than argued.** "Nothing on the critical path is proportional to the size of the vault" - window-visible median was **808 ms at 2 notes and 1410 ms at 20,000**. A third measurement proves cause rather than asserting it: the same binary with the walk delayed returns 821 ms. Fixed - the walk now waits for the first painted frame, *after* the watcher is registered, which is exactly why that ordering rule exists. After: p95 **1088 ms at 2 notes, 1087 ms at 20,000**. The proportionality assertion would now pass; **before the fix it would have failed at 74% growth against its 20% tolerance**, which is the assertion doing its job. | tauri-app-dev, measured | **Magnitude CORRECTED, direction unchanged — see F-153.** The 808/1410/821 ms figures came from three separate blocked runs. | F-89 | **The generated TypeScript is wrong in a way that type-checks and throws at runtime.** `ts-rs` types `mtime_ms`, `bytes` and `since_process_start_ms` as `bigint`, but Tauri's JSON IPC delivers `number` - so `x > 0n` throws against a type the compiler accepted. | F-90 | **Three defects found by running the binary rather than reading it, two of which would have corrupted a budget result.** A benchmark refusal **exited 0**, because `AppHandle::exit`'s code does not survive `Builder::run` returning - to a budget job that reads as a passing run with no trace file. A bad `--vault` **panicked out of Tauri's setup with exit 101** instead of exit 2 and a sentence, found by test-engineer's harness test running against the real binary for the first time. And the buffer-rendered mark could beat the paint mark's round trip and produce no trace at all, now structural with a regression test. | tauri-app-dev | Closed |
| F-91 | **The forbid-list's staleness check is one-directional, and the gap is where it will matter most.** It verifies forbid-list to `POLICY`, so a renamed entry fires; nothing verifies `POLICY` to forbid-list, so **a crate with no entry is silently skipped**. That is `serat-extract`, `serat-ai`, `serat-mcp` and `serat-app` today — four of nine, each with a §2.1 charter and no mechanical counterpart. It bites the day serat-extract arrives, which is the crate where it is worth most: its charter is a **security boundary** (T-03 — the worker links no HTTP client at all, so a network call is a link error). Ten lines, in the shape `POLICY` already uses on itself. | code-reviewer, on the approving pass | Open - rust-core-dev |
| F-92 | **The fifth barrier already exists, and the two limits disagree about it.** `bench/budget/src/subject.rs:155` asks I5 for `apply_and_wait(path, bytes)` — submit one changed file and block until it is visible to a reader. *Would it exist if the caller did not need to wait?* No, so the category permits it. *Is it a phase boundary?* It is called **1,000 times in the NFR-PRF-03 measurement loop**, so the second limit forbids it. The document does not say which wins. **Resolution: `apply_and_wait` should not exist** — the harness can call `submit()` then the already-carved-out `flush()`, which is a barrier by both tests and costs one line. That keeps the command set at four and stops the measurement seam adding a fifth. **The clause that makes limit two usable by someone other than its author: a barrier is called a fixed number of times per phase, not a number of times proportional to the work.** The five rebuild barriers are each called once; `apply_and_wait` would be called once per note — and a reviewer can count that at a call site without knowing what a phase is. | code-reviewer, applying the new rule to the first case that tested it | Open - urgent, I5 implements it as written |
| F-93 | **The register contradicted itself six lines apart.** F-32 struck the 25-minute figure with the words "must not be quoted"; F-38, six lines below in the same file, quoted it as justification. | F-94 | **Two different figures are circulating for Serat's own share of cold start, and both come from the same run.** tauri-app-dev: *"Serat's own main-thread work is about 28 ms of 810."* architect, in §3.4: *"Serat's own contribution is roughly 130 ms of the 400."* They are probably both true under different definitions — 28 ms of main-thread work Serat executes, against ~130 ms of the 400 ms budget window that is not WebView2 environment creation (the 57 ms loader and part of the 100 ms to paint are ours in the sense that we chose a webview shell, but are not code we wrote). **Unreconciled, this is the next 25-minute figure**: two numbers, same run, different bases, and whichever is quoted first wins. Under F-77 a number carries its basis in the same sentence, so the gate report must state which quantity it means. | lead, comparing two reports of one measurement | **Resolved by withdrawal, not reinterpretation.** The five terms are 57 loader, 23 Tauri runtime, 619 WebView2, 100 to first paint, 5 to usable — **130 is not any subset of them.** It was a loose subtraction made while writing a paragraph, and its author deleted it rather than reinterpret it into something it could support. "Serat's own share" is genuinely **three** quantities differing by a factor of six: **28 ms** of code we wrote executing on the main thread (answers *is Serat itself slow?* — no); **185 ms** for everything except WebView2 environment creation (answers *what if the platform term were free?*); **804 ms** for the whole phase (answers *what does the user wait for?*). None is wrong; quoting one without saying which is. **The gate report uses 28 ms, stated as "28 ms of code we wrote, executing on the main thread".** The 185 ms figure is the one to watch — it *looks* like the honest middle and is not, since the 57 ms loader inside it is a function of our binary size and dependency count (ours to influence, not ours to execute) and the 100 ms to paint is our HTML rendered by a webview we did not write. |
| F-95 | **The gate verdict needs three statements, not one.** Architect's structure, which I am adopting: (1) cold start is **measured and missed**, on one platform, best case, with the cause attributed and mostly outside our control; (2) the other three budgets are **unmeasured** — rebuild, save-to-index, and the 50k scale run; (3) §1.2's three structural assumptions **remain untested**, and this number touches none of them, because it says nothing about whether the parse pool or the writer dominates a rebuild. **The failure mode to avoid in the report is the 619 ms being read as "the architecture is too slow".** It is not a finding about the crate graph, the actor or the schema. It is a finding that **the requirement was written without a webview-startup term in it.** | architect | Standing - governs the gate report |
| F-96 | **NFR-PRF-01 was deliberately not re-baselined on this number, and the restraint is correct.** That is product-analyst's call at Q1, and **one platform is not enough** — WebView2 is one of three webviews, and WKWebView and WebKitGTK have their own startup costs that could differ substantially. §3.4 records the *shape* of the answer instead: if all three platforms carry a webview cost of this order, **the requirement is wrong rather than the design**, and the honest fix is a budget separating what we control from what we inherit. It also records what the figure does not establish — it predates F-12's walk fix and was taken with the scanner off. | architect | Open - product-analyst at Q1 |
| F-97 | **Three independent routes reached the walk-contention conclusion, and only the third was evidence.** code-reviewer predicted it analytically from the phase table; architect changed §3.4 on that basis; tauri-app-dev then measured 808 ms at two notes against 1410 ms at twenty thousand, and recovered 821 ms by delaying the walk. **The design was corrected before the measurement existed, which is the good case — and it is also why the measurement was worth taking rather than assumed.** Two correct predictions do not make a third measurement redundant; they make it cheap to interpret. | architect, on the team's own process | Standing principle |
| F-98 | **The lead's resolution to F-92 violated the same limit it was invoked to satisfy, and the real reason `apply_and_wait` is ruled out is better.** `submit()` then `flush()` in the same thousand-iteration loop calls `flush` once per note — **as proportional as `apply_and_wait`**. So counting *flags* the case; it does not settle it. What settles it is limit one, *whose path*: a proportional barrier is either on a production path, where limit one already forbids it, or it is a harness deliberately serialising to measure a latency, which is legitimate because a benchmark is neither the save path nor anything a keystroke waits for. A reader applying the count alone would have deleted a legitimate measurement. **`apply_and_wait` remains ruled out for a different and better reason: a barrier must stay visible where it is called.** `submit(); flush();` shows the wait; `apply_and_wait()` is a verb that reads like a write and hides one, **a single copy-paste from the save path where the wait is forbidden by design goal 2**. That is the hazard that made the original rule worth writing — a mutex is dangerous because it is invisible, not because it is slow. | architect, correcting the lead's ruling | Closed |
| F-99 | **NFR-PRF-03 may be measuring the wrong end, and nobody has the number.** The harness measures submit-to-drained via `flush`. **The UI learns "indexed" from `index.changed`, throttled to ten per second.** Those differ by **up to 100 ms** — twice the entire 50 ms budget — and only the second is what a person experiences. This is F-03 one level deeper: F-03 established which *path* the requirement means (the save path, not the watcher path); this asks which *end* of that path, and the answer decides whether a passing 50 ms figure describes anything a user could notice. A question for the numbers rather than the design, but it must be settled before a green NFR-PRF-03 is reported. | architect, unprompted | Open - test-engineer to measure both, product-analyst at Q1 |
| F-100 | **The reply rule took three passes, and the sequence is the argument for writing rules down at all.** It was too narrow; I4's three replies made it fire on correct code; it was widened to a category; the reviewer applied the widened version to the first fifth case and found the two halves disagreeing; their counting criterion made limit two applicable by someone other than its author; and the countable form then needed limit one to finish. **Three passes for one paragraph — about right for a rule whose entire purpose is to be applied by someone who did not write it.** Each pass was triggered by the rule firing on a case, never by review of the rule itself. | architect | Standing principle |
| F-101 | **"Off the critical path" was being read as "on another thread", and on a cold start those are not the same claim.** §3.4's load-bearing property was false *as implemented* for this reason: the walk was on a background thread and therefore assumed harmless, while canonicalising 20,000 paths contended for the same disk and cores WebView2 was starting on. **Worth recording as a design statement that was wrong rather than as a slow function.** F-12's owner had a 40x on an isolated walk and the word "wasteful"; tauri-app-dev's 1410 ms against 821 ms on the same binary showed it was **600 ms of cold start**. The isolated microbenchmark could not have produced that conclusion. | rust-core-dev, reframing their own finding on tauri-app-dev's evidence | Closed - fix landed |
| F-102 | **A guard that reports the opposite of the truth when the machine is busy is worse than no guard — and it was caught by accident.** F-12's first assertion compared a single pair of timings; during the mutation run, under load from the previous run, it printed **lexical 146 ms against canonicalising 135 ms** — inverted. | F-103 | **Do not normalise the four phases to sum to the wall clock.** If the harness made them add up, it would destroy the one number P0 exists to produce: `parse` as **CPU time summed across threads** against `write` as **producer wait** is exactly what distinguishes a parse-dominated rebuild from a writer-dominated one, and those imply opposite fixes. The four not summing is the information, not an error to be tidied away. | rust-core-dev, warning test-engineer directly | Open - label at the point of output |
| F-104 | **The `debug_assert!` substitute asserted an invariant that does not hold, and fires exactly when the index is already in trouble.** `commit` clears `pending` at its *end*, after `apply::apply`, `sync_note_count` and `tx.commit` — so **any error before those returns through `?` with the batch still in hand**. Demonstrated with an injected one-shot commit failure: `flush` returns `Err(Corrupt)`, the writer thread **panics** at `writer.rs:362` with "work reached ResolveLinks undrained", and the next call gets `WriterStopped` from an actor that was fine a moment earlier. In release there is no assert and the drain does exactly the right thing — **so the code is correct and the assertion about the code is wrong.** The trigger is the `IndexFault` scenario §3.4 exists for, so the writer panics in debug builds precisely when the index is already failing, and debug is where every test runs. **This is the risk the lead named when asking for the adjudication.** The principle survives — asserting an invariant where it holds *is* an adequate substitute for a test that cannot exist — but **the substitute is only as good as the search for error paths, and this search covered only the success path.** The test that "could not exist" now can: a `#[cfg(test)]` seam in `commit`, one `AtomicBool`, buys a guard on a real hazard instead of an assertion of a false invariant. | code-reviewer, by fault injection | Open - three-line deletion plus a comment, optionally the seam |

Neither substitutes for the other, and the balance differs by artifact: code is mostly the first risk, a design document mostly the second. | code-reviewer | Standing principle |

Their own summary: *each was easy to state, useful to the argument, and one question away from not surviving.* The label is now relabelled rather than re-derived — **"main-thread work outside the loader and the webview build"** — with the section saying plainly that how the 28 ms divides between Tauri's runtime and our own code is not established by this run, and that the row's answer does not depend on it. Their reason is the standard: **a table whose entire purpose is that a figure without its basis is the error is the last place to assert a basis nobody has checked.** | architect, on their own pattern | **Now four — see F-123, which names the tell.** |
| F-113 | **An absolute beats a hedged rule only while correcting it is cheap and someone will actually do it.** Both held for §2.2's reply rule — three passes on a paragraph cost a conversation. **Neither will hold in P2**, where widening a shipped API costs a breaking change and the reflex becomes contorting the caller instead. Worth knowing before **ADR-0010's one-statement-per-query rule meets its first legitimate exception.** The companion observation: *a rule with its exceptions pre-installed never fires, which is the same defect as a test whose setup never reaches the path — both look like coverage and neither is.* The tells differ, though: a test that never reaches its path is findable mechanically, which is what mutation does; **a rule that never fires surfaces only when someone applies it to a case its author did not have.** | architect and code-reviewer, jointly | Standing principle - carry into P2 |
| F-114 | **F-91's complement check found a real gap on its first run, in a crate nobody was thinking about.** `serat-contracts` was in `POLICY` and in **neither** capability list — so nobody had decided what a wire contract is not allowed to be. It now has an entry: no database, no parser, no watcher, no HTTP client. The reasoning is the part worth keeping: it is compiled so `contracts/ipc.rs` cannot drift from its bindings, not so it can *do* anything, and **a capability there would be a capability on both sides of the boundary at once.** The reviewer predicted the complement would matter most for `serat-extract`; it mattered immediately for a crate neither of them had in mind. Second guard in two days to catch a real gap the moment it existed (F-79 was the first). | rust-core-dev, on the reviewer's F-91 | Closed |
| F-115 | **ADR-0011's asymmetry now covers three producers and reads as one rule rather than three decisions: the watcher drops, the rebuild waits, the save path drops.** `apply` uses `submit`, not `submit_blocking` — on the save path a full queue means the writer is behind, and waiting there is what design goal 2 forbids, while the refusal is recoverable because a rescan finds the path again. The property is pinned rather than the shape: `a_save_does_not_wait_for_the_writer` fires 64 saves at a one-deep queue and fails if any of them block. | rust-core-dev, applying ADR-0011 to a third case | Closed |
| F-116 | **A destructive command inside a mutation loop is one keystroke from undoing the thing being tested.** An approved file was reverted with a careless `git checkout --` mid-mutation and restored from the approved commit. Self-reported and put in the commit message. Same family as F-51 — the mutation workflow requires repeatedly reverting deliberate damage, which makes an accidental revert indistinguishable from an intended one. | rust-core-dev, self-reported | Standing caution |
| F-117 | **The three rules that fired on real cases did so because design review and code review were happening in the same pass.** The reply rule met I4's three commands; limit two met `apply_and_wait` already sitting in B2's seam; the truncation claim met a fixture two orders of magnitude smaller. **None of those cases was constructed on purpose — they already existed in the tree.** The consequence is narrower than "get a second reviewer": had the reviews been *sequenced*, the reply rule would have been widened under deadline pressure by whoever hit it first, rather than deliberately. **For P2 this argues for spending the awkward case early — ADR-0010's one-statement rule should meet SQ's first genuinely hard query while widening the ADR still costs a conversation rather than a breaking change** (with F-113). | code-reviewer, generalising the session's own process | Standing principle - carry into P2 |
| F-118 | **"The index state is known" means any state that is not `Opening`** — `Rebuilding` and `Unavailable` settle the `t_usable` term exactly as `Ready` does. The requirement is that the state is *known*, not that it is good. Two consequences make it concrete: a shell waiting for `Ready` **hangs for the length of a full rebuild**, on precisely the vault where the window most needs to be up; and **P0 never reaches `Ready` at all**, so it would never report usable at any time. §3.4 assumed this rather than saying it. | tauri-app-dev by building it; architect wrote it down | Closed |
| F-119 | **Throttled events are for invalidation, never for establishing that something has occurred.** The `t_usable` index term takes its signal from an observer on the actor's published state rather than from the event sink, which is throttled to ten per second — a `Rebuilding` whose event lost to the throttle would leave the mark waiting for a transition that **had already happened**. The general form is now in §3.4 because **P1's views face the same choice and will get it wrong by default.** Its author's own account of the cause: they specified preconditions without saying what may *establish* them, which left the throttled channel a perfectly reasonable reading. **The failure mode is the nastiest kind — the mark never fires, the trace never exists, and nothing looks like a bug.** | architect, on their own under-specification; found by tauri-app-dev building it | Closed - and generalised for P1 |
| F-120 | **A constraint pushed to I6 rather than a note: the index actor publishes its classification as soon as the open check produces one, *before* starting any rebuild.** The measured 78–95 ms against 800–970 ms to paint shows the term is not binding — **but it is bounded because the classification is published early, not because the check is fast.** That distinction is what makes it a requirement in §3.4 rather than an observation, and it is what would break if I6 published nothing until a rebuild completed. | tauri-app-dev flagged it; architect made it binding | Open - constrains I6 |
| F-121 | **The lead pointed a reviewer at a WIP snapshot without labelling it as one.** Architect read `c78f6be` — the lead's own protective commit of tauri-app-dev's unfinished work — and reported a finding real against that commit and already closed on the author's finished branch. Their words: *real against what I read, useless to them, the worst combination.* **The cost is the lead's: the commit message said WIP, the message pointing at it did not**, and a reviewer has no reason to check whether a branch tip is the author's work or someone else's snapshot of it. Recorded because the same hazard recurs whenever work is protected on someone's behalf — F-51's tip-moved-underneath problem, from a third direction. | architect, self-critical; lead's cause; corrected by tauri-app-dev | **Closed, and neither party's fault was the lesson.** The subject of the review declined both accounts: **the misread bought a precondition they had genuinely missed** — §3.4's first term, that the index open check has finished — so it was not wasted effort at all. **The real lesson is structural: a WIP snapshot and a finished branch were indistinguishable from outside.** Three people each tried to take the blame; the artifact was the problem. Practical residue, still worth doing: label a snapshot when pointing at it (lead), and name the artifact you reviewed in the first line (F-126). |
| F-122 | **"Tested against" is now a field in the ADR template.** The concrete case a decision was checked against and who supplied it — or honestly *"none yet"*, naming the case that would test it and who should go find it. It records the three P0 instances as the evidence for why, **because a template that asks without saying why gets filled in with nothing.** ADR-0010 carries the first entry: none yet, and the case that would test it is **a real query from the owner's vault that is natural to ask and not sayable in one statement**, to be sought during P2's Dataview hand-translation rather than waited for. Confirming the trade is a result; widening deliberately is a result; **discovering it in P3 and bolting on a second execution path is the failure the field exists to prevent.** This is F-117 turned from prose into structure. | code-reviewer's generalisation, made structural by architect | Closed |
| F-123 | **Four figures from one author failed the same way, and the tell is now nameable: a justified number where only one side of the justification is shown.** The 25-minute run (a duration without its confounds), the 130 ms share (a share-of-total without a basis), the "code we wrote" label (a decomposition without its arithmetic), and now the 20% tolerance — **justified by the size of the *effect* (2.5x, "a factor of ten of headroom") with the *noise* never measured.** After the walk fix removed the effect, two back-to-back batches gave `t_visible` p95 of 1146 ms at two notes against 940 ms at twenty thousand: run-to-run variation of about 20%, **the same size as the threshold. The assertion now fires on nothing** — precisely the flaky job its author had told test-engineer they were protecting them from. **The repair is the instrument, not the threshold:** interleave the two vault sizes within one batch so drift lands on both arms rather than masquerading as a signal, and take enough iterations for the statistic to settle. Widening the tolerance would trade away the detection it was sized for. **Look for the shape before publishing rather than after.** | architect, naming their own repeated failure mode | Standing principle - the most transferable finding of the spike |
| F-124 | **Fifth instance, inside the correction for the fourth — and the tell is now a runnable check.** The commit fixing F-123 said *"the vault-size effect is gone, which is what the fix was for"*. But F-109 shows the instrument **cannot see an effect of that size at this iteration count**, so that sentence claims a result from an absence of evidence — in the paragraph written to stop exactly that. §3.4 now separates what is established from what is not. **Established, narrowly:** the walk *was* proportional at 808 ms against 1410 ms, and delaying it returned the large-vault figure to 821 ms — a known mechanism identified and removed. **Not established:** that no residual proportionality remains. The claim is **unfalsifiable at this iteration count, not confirmed.** The check, stated so it can be run on oneself before publishing: **a claim of the form "X is gone" or "X is fine", resting on a measurement that could not have shown X if it were there.** All five instances fit it. | architect, catching themselves inside their own correction | Standing principle - the check to run before publishing |
| F-125 | **NFR-PRF-01 needs two verdicts, and they differ in kind.** (a) **The miss is measured and unambiguous** — 2.4 to 2.9x over 400 ms, surviving any amount of noise, cause attributed and mostly outside our control. (b) **The design property §3.4 claims — that nothing on the critical path is proportional to vault size — is untested, not validated**, because the instrument that would test it currently cannot. These are separate lines in the gate report. **A report giving only the first would leave P1 believing the second had been checked**, which is precisely the failure F-78 exists to guard against. The same distinction applies to §1.2's three structures: unfalsifiable rather than unmeasured, and worth stating precisely rather than as a blanket "not measured". | architect | Governs the gate report and D4 |
| F-126 | **Name the artifact you reviewed, in the first line.** The other half of F-121, which its subject kept rather than accepted away: the lead failed to label a WIP snapshot, *and* the reviewer reported the finding without saying which commit they had read. Had the report opened with "at `c78f6be`" rather than mentioning it in passing, the author would have spotted the mismatch immediately instead of writing a paragraph to correct it. **Naming the artifact costs nothing and localises exactly this class of error.** | architect, declining to have the whole fault reassigned | Standing practice |
| F-127 | **An assertion is a claim about all paths; a test claims less and is therefore safe to get partly wrong.** The sharpened form of F-104, from the person who wrote the bad assertion: asserting an invariant *is* a sound substitute for an untestable path, **but a search covering only the success path cannot justify a claim that quantifies over every path.** The three asserts are deleted, the drains kept, and the comment now names the one route that reaches them rather than implying there is none — **which is more useful than the assertion was, because it tells the next person where to look.** | rust-core-dev, sharpening the finding against their own work | Closed |
| F-128 | **The new ANALYZE assertion is the only place that defect is visible anywhere in the project — and it stops guarding silently.** F-47's check lives solely in `prf04`, so **if that job is ever skipped, or the benchmark vault shrinks below roughly 10,000 notes, the guard quietly stops guarding** and nothing says so. The defect it catches — statistics describing the index as it was 32 notes ago, because the 5% re-ANALYZE threshold is not crossed by the tail — is invisible at every other scale the project tests at. A single-point guard whose precondition is a property of the fixture. | rust-core-dev, flagging test-engineer's guard | Open - test-engineer, make the precondition explicit |
| F-129 | **`links_resolved` counts asset embeds and the manifest's `edges` does not — structural, not a choice.** Assets are rows in `notes`, so `![[report.pdf]]` resolves like any other link, **which is ADR-0005 working as designed.** The assertion would therefore have fired on a correct rebuild (F-108 confirmed). Three ways to settle it were offered to the oracle's owner, on the principle that **their manifest is the oracle and the implementer's number is the thing under test** — so the choice belongs to the side that is not being measured. | rust-core-dev and test-engineer, settling before it became a red job | Open - test-engineer to pick |
| F-130 | **Serat's main-thread share of cold start is 5.2 ms, not 28 — and the correction came from instrumenting rather than relabelling.** The 28 ms was a *subtraction*: `main → runtime_ready` (23) plus `painted → usable` (5), added because both looked like they would be ours. **The first is almost entirely `tauri::Builder` constructing itself** — Tauri's runtime, not our code — so the figure overstated our share about fivefold. Rather than accept the weaker label architect had settled on, its author bracketed our work with two marks of our own. Measured, 20 spawns, release: `main → state_ready` **4.7 / 5.0 ms**, `runtime_ready → setup_done` **0.5 / 0.5 ms**; **total 5.2 ms at 2 notes and 5.6 ms at 20,000, p95 9.4 / 9.6 — about 0.5% of the ~1000 ms to first paint.** The conclusion gets *stronger*: our contribution is **below the run-to-run noise of every other term in the table**, and the 25.7 ms of Tauri builder previously claimed as ours is **five times our entire contribution**. **5.2 → 5.6 ms across a 10,000x vault-size difference is also §3.4's load-bearing property measured on the one term we actually control** — the only place in the table it can be checked cleanly, since every other term is dominated by platform costs that move with the weather. | tauri-app-dev, correcting themselves by measurement after code-reviewer's arithmetic | Closed |
| F-131 | **"Name the basis" is not enough: an aggregate should be measured, not composed.** The proposed rule after F-32, F-94 and F-106 was that an aggregate names its basis in the same sentence. Its third victim tightened it: **naming the basis would have made the 28 ms look defensible and still been wrong, because the basis itself was the error.** A composed figure inherits every assumption in its composition and shows none of them. And the catch mechanism is worth as much as the rule: **nobody caught it by reading the commit message — it read as a measurement because it was stated as one.** It was caught by doing arithmetic against the *split published beside it*. **Publishing the split is what made it catchable**, which is an argument for publishing decompositions even when only the total is asked for. | tauri-app-dev, tightening architect's rule against their own figure | Standing principle |
| F-132 | **The proportionality claim is partly repaired, and the repair came from a table rather than from reasoning.** F-124 recorded the claim as unfalsifiable because run-to-run noise now exceeds any vault-size effect in wall clock. But **5.2 → 5.6 ms across a ten-thousand-fold vault difference is a direct test on the one term not dominated by a platform cost — and it holds.** §3.4 now states two claims separately: **main-thread work is constant in vault size, measured**; **wall clock is not decidable at this iteration count.** Only the second is unfalsifiable. A materially better position than an hour earlier, reached by instrumenting the term we control rather than by arguing about the aggregate. | tauri-app-dev's instrumentation; architect's separation | Closed for main-thread work; wall clock remains open |
| F-133 | **The same failure appeared independently in two people within a day, on the same quantity — which indicts the quantity, not the people.** Both published versions of "Serat's own share of cold start" were **composed rather than measured**, and both were wrong: one matched no subset of the terms, the other matched two terms that were not ours. The useful conclusion is not whose was worse. **"Serat's own share of cold start" invites composition, because the honest answer needs instrumentation nobody had placed** — and neither author thought to place it until the reviewer's arithmetic made the composition visible. **A quantity that cannot be read off the existing marks will be assembled from them.** The fix is to instrument the quantity you intend to publish, before publishing it. | architect, declining to make it about either author | Standing principle |
| F-134 | **A column written but never read is indistinguishable from a column written correctly.** Adding `VaultMoved` to the open check made I6 the **first reader** of `index_meta.vault_root` — and exposed that `rebuild()` had been recording the **index directory** there, not the vault root. Wrong since it was written, invisible because nothing consumed it. **The general shape is worth more than the bug:** a write-only column has no failing test, no wrong output and no symptom, and only acquires one when something finally reads it. Consequence for the seam: `rebuild` now takes `vault_root` as one new argument before `index_dir`, which changes test-engineer's call site. | rust-core-dev, building I6 | Closed |
| F-135 | **NFR-REL-02's acceptance criterion is exercised for the first time** — truncate the index by one page, start, and it detects the corruption and rebuilds. **That was the last P0 requirement nothing had touched.** Both constraints are mutation-checked: the classification is published **before** any rebuild work, asserted by *ordering* rather than timing (the status must be published while the source has not yet been asked to enumerate — moving the publish after the rebuild fails two tests), and a failed rebuild leaves `Unavailable` with no retry (adding one retry fails the test). **The one-page rule is in the truncation test with its reason attached, so nobody later "improves" it into a percentage.** | rust-core-dev | Closed |
| F-136 | **I6 does not cover the corruption case that matters most, and its author said so rather than let the branch read as covering it.** The tests exercise the six outcomes against a `FakeVault`, so **what is proven is the classification and the ordering, not that a real 124 MB index on a real disk behaves the same.** The case they cannot see is the one already measured: **4 KB of garbage mid-file passes all six stamp steps** — which is precisely why the `IndexFault` classifier reachable from every read and write is **mandatory rather than defence in depth. It is not built.** It belongs in P1 with the read pool. | rust-core-dev, volunteering the gap | Open - P1, and the gate report must not claim otherwise |
| F-137 | **The cold-start figure is two mitigations deep on one defect, and F-12's fix is still not on main.** The 20k cold-start figure is size-independent **because the walk is deferred until after the paint, not because the walk got cheap** — the lexical prefix strip has not merged. So the paint gate is **load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces**, and a green cold-start job must not be read as evidence the walk is cheap. Agreed remedy: remeasure with the deferral removed once the strip lands, and **if the deferral then turns out to be redundant that is a question for architect, since §3.4 now specifies it.** Sharpens F-88, and belongs in the gate report as a caveat rather than a footnote. | tauri-app-dev, flagging their own mitigation for the second time | Open - until F-12 merges and the pair is remeasured |
| F-138 | **The cold-start numbers move substantially between runs on the same machine, so the gate report must carry a range and not a point.** Successive measurements of the same branch: `t0 → paint` about 810 ms, then 940–1146 ms, then 760 ms; `WebviewWindowBuilder::build` 619 ms, then 754–865 ms, then 596 ms; Serat's own share 5.2 ms, then 4.8 ms. **Every version of the conclusion survives — the miss is 1.9x to 2.9x over 400 ms and our share is 0.5–0.6% either way — but no single figure is reproducible enough to quote alone.** This is F-109's noise finding applied to the headline number rather than to the proportionality assertion. | lead, comparing three successive reports of the same measurement | Open - report the range |
| F-139 | **A prohibition is only half a design rule; the other half is what you must provide instead.** F-119 said throttled events may not *establish* that something occurred. The missing half, supplied by its first implementer: **the value must stay readable from a channel that cannot drop** — `index_status()` returns the newest state even when the event announcing it lost to the throttle, which is what makes a late or reloaded subscriber **correct rather than eventually correct**. The one-line form now in §3.4: **the notification may be coalesced freely; the state may not.** Its author on why they would not have written it: *"I had the rule as a prohibition on gating, and a prohibition is only half a design rule."* | tauri-app-dev's addendum to architect's rule | Closed |
| F-140 | **The walk deferral stays, and the question to ask of the remeasurement is not the obvious one.** Not *does the deferral still save anything at 20,000 notes* — it may well not, once F-12's strip lands. The question is **whether removing it puts work proportional to vault size back in front of the paint.** It does: a ~50 ms walk at 20,000 is ~125 ms at 50,000 and half a second at 200,000, **while the budget stays at 400 ms.** So the deferral is what makes §3.4's property **structural rather than contingent on the walk happening to be cheap** — and **a property that holds because a number is currently small breaks silently when the number grows**, which is the same invisible-failure shape as everything else on this project. The instinct against dead mitigations is kept rather than overruled: measure with the deferral out, because **the size of what it prevents is worth knowing, and recording it is what stops the deferral reading as an unjustifiable mitigation.** Delete it only on evidence from the largest vault the budget must hold at. | architect, answering tauri-app-dev's question before it was asked | Open - remeasure at 50k when F-12 lands |
| F-141 | **The wall-clock proportionality claim IS decidable — the limit was the sampling design, not the iteration count.** F-109 and F-124 recorded it as unfalsifiable on the strength of two **blocks** of 20 (all 2-note spawns, then all 20k spawns), so each arm carried whatever the machine was doing during its block. **Interleaving the vaults spawn-by-spawn puts drift on both arms, where it cancels.** 30 interleaved pairs: median **897.3 ms against 891.8 ms** (ratio 0.994), p95 **1337.3 against 1345.0** (ratio 1.006), **paired difference 95% CI [-123.1, +45.4] ms against a +20% tolerance of 179.5 ms — the interval excludes the tolerance, so it is decided.** Within-arm spread is 831 ms, about five times the effect, which is exactly why blocked sampling could not see past it. **The supportable claim is a bound, not an absence:** wall clock does not grow with vault size *beyond the tolerance*; the CI does not exclude a small real effect and nothing needs it to. | tauri-app-dev, correcting their own "not decidable" | Closed - decided |
| F-142 | **A negative result names its design, not just its basis.** Its author's generalisation, and the sixth member of the family: *"This was the 28 ms failure in a different costume. There I composed a number instead of measuring it; here I measured with a design that could not answer the question and reported 'not decidable' as a fact about the system rather than about my method. Both were invisible because the output looked like data."* **"Not decidable" needs "under blocked sampling at n=20" attached, or the next reader takes it as a property of the world.** This extends F-131 (an aggregate should be measured, not composed) to negative results, which are the easier case to state carelessly because they sound like humility. | tauri-app-dev | Standing principle |
| F-143 | **Nobody has measured 50,000 notes at all, and the argument for keeping the walk deferral rests on an extrapolation.** F-140's reasoning — a ~50 ms walk at 20,000 is ~125 ms at 50,000 and half a second at 200,000 — is arithmetic from one measured point, not a second measurement. The conclusion may well be right; **the evidence for it is of a different kind from the evidence for everything else in the gate report**, and the benchmark generator has produced a 50k preset since B1 that nothing has yet been run against. Raised by the person whose instinct architect had just overruled, against the ruling that went their way. | tauri-app-dev | **Measured, and the extrapolation was wrong in direction, not merely unmeasured.** The projection was that a 50 ms walk at 20,000 becomes 125 ms at 50,000. **At 50,000 the effect is *smaller* — median +28 ms, CI including zero.** Walk duration and paint delay do not scale together. Its author's conclusion: *labelling it an extrapolation was the right first correction and it was not enough — an extrapolation that proves wrong needs saying, not just flagging.* |
| F-144 | **§3.4 named interleaving as the fix two paragraphs before mis-attributing the cause to iteration count.** The section already carried the instruction to interleave the two vault sizes within one batch — written when F-123's tolerance error was corrected — and then, two paragraphs later, explained the undecidability by n=20. **The document contained its own correction and was not read against itself.** Its author's observation, offered while accepting the correction. Same family as F-93, where the register struck a figure and quoted it six lines below. | architect, self-observed | Standing caution - read a document against itself, not only forward |
| F-145 | **"We need more iterations" and "we need a different design" are not the same advice.** The undecidability of the wall-clock claim was attributed to sample size when the obstacle was **blocked sampling**: within-arm spread of 830 ms against the effect means **no quantity of blocked samples decides it, while thirty interleaved pairs do.** Its author gave the first advice when the second was true. Recorded because the first is the reflex and it is expensive — it buys runs that cannot answer the question. | architect, correcting their own diagnosis | Standing principle |
| F-146 | **Lead with the largest fact, not the good news, because the good news is what a reader generalises from.** "Design property measured and holds" is true of **one property, on one platform, on one machine**; "three budgets unmeasured" is the larger fact and must not end up as a qualifier under a heading that reads like validation. Applied to this register's own status document, whose verdict was reordered accordingly — and which was simultaneously found to contain **two self-contradictions of exactly F-144's shape**: statement 1 still said the proportionality signal was undecided while statement 3 said it was decided, and a stale point figure sat below the paragraph instructing the reader to use ranges. | architect; applied by the lead to the lead's own document | Closed |
| F-147 | **The interleaving correction was applied to the analysis and never to the instrument — and the broken instrument is on main.** F-141's 30 interleaved pairs are recorded in this register and in the status document, and the reasoning is sound. But `scale_check` in `bench/budget/src/bin/prf01-cold-start.rs` **still runs all 20 spawns at 20k, then all 20 at 50k, and compares two p95s** — exactly the design F-141 proves cannot decide the question, against a within-arm spread of 831 ms and a 179.5 ms tolerance. No branch carries an interleaved version. **So when `subject::cold_start` lands and the job goes live, it becomes the flaky job architect said they were protecting test-engineer from** — inside the harness whose entire purpose is that a number without a sound basis is worse than no number. **F-109's own diagnosis arriving in the place it was diagnosed away from.** Fix: interleave spawn-by-spawn within one batch, and carry F-141's other conclusion into the assertion — **the job should assert the CI excludes the tolerance, not that the ratio is near 1**, because the supportable claim is a bound rather than an absence. | code-reviewer, checking whether a correction reached the code | **Already fixed on a branch, and the register was wrong about it. Verified by the lead:** `scale_check` no longer exists in `prf01-cold-start.rs` at all (the binary is a `main` calling into the library), and the interleaving lives in **`bench/budget/src/lib.rs::measure_scale_independence`**, on `task/b5-close-m37-and-seams` at `88117ca` — unmerged, which is why the reviewer reading main found the old design. **F-149 cost this twice:** the lead described the fix as applied without naming a file, and its author reported it with a branch and a commit but also without a file. Both have adopted the rule. |
| F-148 | **Three guards, each individually deletable, behind one aggregate that does bite.** `serat_main_thread_ms` has three `?` sites; its test calls `complete(&startup)`, which marks **none** of the three brackets, so removing any one still returns `None` from the other two. It proves *"all brackets missing reports None"* while the docstring claims *"either bracket missing"* — which is the useful claim and is unbacked. Not blocking, because the whole-figure mutation bites. But this is the **fourth** version of that figure — and its author's correction to the lead's framing is worth keeping: **the four failed in different ways.** Composed rather than measured (28 ms), then measured, then bracketed, now guarded per term. **Only the first was a defect in the figure; the other three were defects in what would catch a change to it.** | code-reviewer, on the approving pass | Open - tauri-app-dev, three cases or one loop |
| F-149 | **"Resolving from `git log` catches a moved tip; it does not catch a correction that landed in a document and not in code."** The reviewer spent a meaningful part of a review reading the wrong branch before realising the interleaving they had been sent to check **was never in the harness at all**. Their ask, and it is the lead's to act on: **when a fix is described as applied, name the file it landed in.** The lead described F-141's interleaving as a correction without saying it existed only in an analysis and a register entry. | code-reviewer; cause is the lead's | Standing practice |
| F-150 | **The two walk mitigations are different claims, not redundant ones — and only one of them is a property.** *The deferral says no vault-proportional work runs before the paint. The strip says the vault-proportional work is 11x smaller.* **Only the first is a property; the second is a number.** Keeping both means the property survives when the number moves — which is exactly what nobody has measured at 50,000. Its author applied architect's own reasoning back to their own fix: *"50 ms at 20,000 is my number on this machine with a warm cache, and the walk still reads 103 directories and stats 20,205 entries. The strip removes the syscall that dominated; it does not make the walk free."* | rust-core-dev, agreeing with a ruling by strengthening the argument against their own work | Closed |
| F-151 | **The no-index path needs its own budget line, because the two paths have opposite bottlenecks.** With a warm index the tree comes from one query and the walk is pure background cost, so deferring it is free. **With no index the tree comes from the streaming walk — so the paint gate makes that path strictly worse, by delaying the only source of tree data.** One budget covering both will always be satisfied by whichever path is currently easier, which is the failure mode of a budget that averages two regimes. Neither party can verify it today: it needs the S1 binary against a cold vault. | rust-core-dev and tauri-app-dev, agreeing independently | **The premise is CONDITIONAL, not wrong — see F-207, which corrects this entry before it hardened.** It does not hold *while the walk is expensive*; it will likely hold once the strip lands. The 20k result is clean; **the 50k result is not established** — difference-of-arm-medians says the deferral is 509 ms *worse*, median-of-paired-differences says 135 ms *better*, and n=8 against a 6.9–13.1 s spread decides neither. Measured: deferring the walk gives up about a second of head start and buys back the contention the walk would otherwise have with WebView2 starting. **The two cancel, and the sign is not consistent between pairs** (+71 ms at 20k, +135 ms at 50k, with the gate delaying the tree in only 4/10 and 4/8 pairs). **Two people agreed independently, and that agreement was the reason it read as settled.** |
| F-152 | **A guard for a crate that does not exist yet cannot fail against the real workspace — so it must be tested against synthetic graphs.** Four of the new forbid lists describe crates nobody has written (`serat-extract`, `serat-ai`, `serat-mcp`), so against the real workspace they match nothing and are vacuous: **the same shape as everything else this spike keeps finding.** The capability check is now a function over packages, exercised against constructed graphs — an extraction worker linking each of four HTTP clients, an MCP server linking a database, a query compiler linking the database it compiles for. **And the converse is tested, which is what makes the list credible: `reqwest` in `serat-ai` must PASS**, because NFR-SEC-02 puts every outbound call there. A list that blocked a legitimate dependency would be worse than none — which is nearly what happened when I5 added rayon. **Why it could not wait for the crate:** T-03's mechanism is that the extraction worker links no HTTP client *at all*, so a network call is a **link error** rather than a runtime check — and that claim is only as good as something that notices when a client appears. **The guard has to precede the code.** | rust-core-dev | Closed |
| F-153 | **When you invalidate a method, re-run what it produced.** The ~600 ms walk cost — reported here, cited in §3.4, and recorded as a finding — came from **three separate blocked runs**, the exact design its author later proved cannot decide this class of question. **They applied that lesson forward and never went back to check what the flawed design had already banked.** Corrected by 40 interleaved pairs at 20k: **+53.7 ms median, ~271 ms at p95, ratio 1.100 median and 1.200 p95, 27/40 pairs slower (sign test p ≈ 0.04)** — direction and decision unchanged, magnitude about ten times smaller. Their statement of why nobody caught it is the transferable half: **a design flaw affects every conclusion that design has already banked, and those are harder to find because they have stopped looking like open questions — they read as settled.** Being *upstream* of the correction is what made it invisible. Third instance of the family and the worst: F-142 was a method reported as a fact, the 28 ms was a number composed instead of measured, and this was a published finding produced by a discredited design. | tauri-app-dev, auditing their own banked conclusions | Closed - correct the magnitude wherever it is quoted |
| F-154 | **No cold-start figure anyone has taken is a cold-*vault* figure, and this machine cannot produce one.** Every measurement spawns repeatedly against one vault, so after the first iteration the page cache holds it and **the walk is CPU-bound rather than I/O-bound. A user's cold start reads a cold vault.** An attempt to test it failed for a reason worth recording: a freshly generated vault is **write-cached, not cold** — fresh 20k vaults gave +42.9 ms (n=12/arm) against a **null control of +15.9 ms running the same binary on both arms**. Dropping the Windows page cache needs privileges not available here. **So NFR-PRF-01 is validated only against a warm vault, and that is a stated bound on what P0 established**, alongside `gate_eligible: false` for the scanner. It is also the strongest argument for keeping the walk deferral: **the case where the walk hurts most is the one we cannot measure.** | tauri-app-dev, reporting a limit they could not engineer around | Open - stated bound; needs a machine that can drop the cache |
| F-155 | **F-143 is answered: at 50,000 notes the paint property holds, and the deferral earns its place on the tail rather than the median.** 40 interleaved pairs with the deferral in: median ratio 0.981, p95 ratio 1.049, paired difference 95% CI **[-45.8, +61.1] ms against a 199.9 ms tolerance**. Serat's own main-thread work: **4.7 ms at 20k, 4.8 ms at 50k**. §3.4's property is now measured at **2 notes, 20,000 and 50,000**, and the term we control is flat across all three — so the extrapolation F-143 flagged is replaced by a measurement. The deferral's justification also changes shape: **+54 ms median but +271 ms at p95, a ratio of 1.20 sitting exactly on the tolerance. A p95 budget is judged on the part the walk damages most.** Its author withdrew their own delete-it-if-it-saves-nothing instinct — not because they were overruled, but because of F-154. | tauri-app-dev | Closed - the last extrapolation is now measured |
| F-156 | **Writing the interleaved test exposed a second defect: a fake keyed on call order rather than on the vault.** The old `scale_verdict` case returned its fake measurement by *call order*, so **the moment the arms interleaved it passed for the wrong reason** — and it had been green through two full mutation sweeps. F-58's shape rather than F-69's: the assertion was right and the setup never reached the path. Closing M37 also revealed the composition itself was untested — `run_scale_check` composed measure and judge, and a mutation stopping `judge_scale` being called at all went unnoticed. **The pieces were tested; the composition was not.** | test-engineer, while fixing something else | **REOPENED - the sweep is real and the tree it ran against never merged.** The 38 mutations ran only on `task/b5-close-m37-and-seams` (f8312cf). `main` carries none of `run_scale_check`, `judge_scale`, `measure_scale_independence` or `scale_verdict` — only the bare `assert_scale_independent`, which nothing calls. Landing b5 is not a rebase: its `cold_start_once` threads an `open_note` through `VaultUnderTest`, assuming a note-opening cold-start CLI that serat-app never grew — `COLD_START_ENTRY_POINT` still reports "there is no application to start yet, tasks D2 and S1". Forcing it means inventing that contract or stripping the refactor this very sweep covered, so it was not forced. Task #41, F-351 |
| F-157 | **A guard that quietly stops guarding is worse than one that is absent, so it must refuse rather than pass.** `check_analyze_freshness` now **refuses a vault under 10,000 notes** instead of returning green: below that threshold the opportunistic re-ANALYZE fires every batch, so a pass would mean nothing was tested. This closes F-128, where the guard's precondition was a property of the fixture and nothing said so. **The pattern generalises: when a guard has a precondition, the guard should assert the precondition, not assume it.** | test-engineer | Closed |
| F-158 | **Three separate defects this session were found by reasoning about why a test could not fail — none of them needed a machine.** rust-core-dev's ANALYZE arithmetic (the 5% threshold is smaller than one batch at test scale, so the explicit call is redundant and its deletion invisible); tauri-app-dev's blocked-sampling diagnosis (within-arm spread five times the effect, so no quantity of blocked samples decides it); and test-engineer's call-order fake (green through two sweeps, wrong the moment the arms interleaved). **Mutation testing finds these by execution; arithmetic finds them by inspection — and the arithmetic is cheaper and available before the code runs.** | lead, from the session's own record | Standing principle |
| F-159 | **Three measurement calls made with their reasons published beside the number.** (a) **Medians for the comparison, p95 for the budget** — NFR-PRF-01 says p95 and that is what the budget asserts, but *a p95 over twenty samples is a noisier estimator than the quantity it estimates*, so the ratio uses the median. (b) **Tolerance unchanged at 20%** — the instrument was wrong, not the threshold. (c) **The claim is a bound, not an absence**, and the report text says so: *"This bounds the effect; it does not claim there is none."* The CI excludes an effect the size of the tolerance, not a small real one, and the budget is about the former. | test-engineer | Closed |
| F-160 | **A design document that accumulates measured claims needs a discipline for retiring them — and this one arrived at that discipline by breaking it four times in two days.** The four rules §3.4 has had to learn, three of them from other people or from correcting its author: **name the basis** (F-32, F-94); **measure rather than compose** (F-131); **name the design of a negative result** (F-142); and **when you invalidate a method, re-run what it produced** (F-153). The generalisation the report should carry is not about any single number: **a section that only ever adds claims will carry its worst one indefinitely, because a claim stops looking like an open question the moment it is written down.** | architect, on §3.4's own history; **framing corrected by tauri-app-dev** | Standing principle. **The self-congratulatory reading is refused by one of the people who broke the rule:** *"We broke the same rule four times in one section and recovered each time because someone else checked — that is a property of having a reviewer and a register, not of having a method."* **The narrower claim that survives: a design document accumulating measured claims needs an *owner* for retiring them, because all four survived not by being defended but by nobody re-opening them.** |
| F-161 | **A limitation described by the person who hit it reads as a bound; the same limitation described second-hand reads as an excuse.** Applied deliberately: the cold-vault limitation (F-154) goes into D4 **in tauri-app-dev's own words**, because they are the person who tried to engineer around it and failed, and can say what they attempted. Recorded as an editorial rule for the gate report, not only as a courtesy. | architect | Standing practice |
| F-162 | **The fix for a per-term guard needs a control, or all its cases pass against a function that always fails.** F-148 is closed with one case per `?` site, built from a complete `Trace` with exactly one field cleared — **plus a control asserting the complete case is `Some` and equals the value the brackets define.** Without the control, all three cases would pass against a function that returned `None` unconditionally: **the same defect one level up, and, in its author's words, the way they would most likely have got the fix wrong.** Verified by mutation rather than reading: each `?` replaced with `.unwrap_or(0.0)` in turn, all three FAILED, unmutated ok. | tauri-app-dev | Closed |
| F-163 | **Four versions of one figure, failing in four different ways — and only the first was a defect in the figure.** Composed rather than measured (28 ms, wrong); then measured; then bracketed by its own marks; now guarded per term. **The last three were defects in what would catch a change to it, not in the number.** Recorded because "the Nth version of that figure" invites reading a sequence of corrections as escalating carelessness, when three of the four were the guard improving around a number that was already right. | tauri-app-dev, correcting the lead's framing | Standing distinction |
| F-164 | **These distributions are heavy-tailed, so a mean-based test under-detects — the budget job needs a rank statistic or a direct p95 comparison.** In the last run **the mean CI included zero while the sign test gave p ≈ 0.04 and the p95 ratio was 1.20.** A paired-difference CI on the mean is the wrong instrument for a quantity whose whole interest is in the tail, and NFR-PRF-01 is a p95 requirement. This lands on F-147's fix: interleaving alone is not enough if the statistic cannot see a tail effect. | tauri-app-dev, sent to test-engineer with the scripts rather than committed into `bench/` | Open - test-engineer, with F-147 |
| F-165 | **Three editorial rules for the cold-vault limitation in the gate report, from the person who hit it.** (a) **Do not soften "cannot."** It is not hard or noisy — it is **unavailable without privileges**, and a reader who thinks it was skipped will assume it can be added later on the same hardware. (b) **Keep the null control if anything is cut.** Without it, the +42.9 ms is *a number from a design nobody checked* — and this section has now retired four of those. (c) The paragraph goes in **their words**, per F-161, because the person who tried to engineer around a limit can say what they attempted. | tauri-app-dev, writing the D4 paragraph architect asked for | Closed - use verbatim |
| F-166 | **Name a role, not a virtue — a virtue is something a reader agrees with, a role is something someone has to be assigned.** Why "a document accumulating measured claims needs an **owner** for retiring them" is the more useful sentence than any statement about rigour or discipline: the first can be staffed, the second can only be nodded at. Recorded as the reason F-160's narrower framing replaced the flattering one. | architect, explaining why the correction was an improvement | Standing principle |
| F-167 | **Recording that a projection *failed* is different from recording that it was *unverified* — and the difference decides what the next reader distrusts.** Flagging the 50k projection as an extrapolation was the right first correction; saying that it **proved wrong in direction** is what stops the next person distrusting *projections in general* rather than *that projection*. Its author declined credit for the correction on the same principle: the 50k run is what made the direction visible, and what they did was write down what someone else's number showed instead of the weaker thing they had said before it. | architect | Standing distinction |
| F-168 | **Two reasons for one outcome must not be collapsed, when collapsing them teaches the wrong lesson.** The walk deferral survives, but *not* because questioning mitigations is wrong. **The instinct that a mitigation measuring as worthless should go was correct; what changed is that the measurement justifying that turned out to be unobtainable on this hardware** (F-154). Collapsing the two would teach the next reader "do not question mitigations" rather than **"check whether your evidence can see the case that matters"** — which is worse than either party believes, and is why the withdrawal is recorded as its author's own rather than as an overruling. | architect, protecting the lesson rather than the ruling | Standing practice |
| F-169 | **The app falls back to a note remembered in the settings TOML, so a cold-start job without `--open` will pass on a developer machine and fail in clean CI.** Named by the person who created it, before anyone hit it. **This is the flattering-number class in its purest form:** the job succeeds locally because of state the machine happens to carry, and the failure is deferred to the one environment where nobody is watching it interactively. **Lead's ruling: remove the fallback under `--exit-after-ready`** — two lines in serat-app. The benchmark entry point should have no path to success that depends on remembered state, and the ergonomics argument for keeping it does not survive the fact that the *whole purpose* of that binary is to produce a number somebody will believe. | tauri-app-dev, self-reported; ruled by lead | Open - two lines in serat-app |
| F-170 | **A claim made to another teammate was verified before being relied on, not after.** The deterministic-note claim handed to test-engineer for `--open` was checked by byte-order sort across both presets rather than trusted to `ls`. Recorded because the failure it avoids is specific: **a fixture that is deterministic on one filesystem's enumeration order and not another's would make the cold-start figure irreproducible in exactly the way the vault digest exists to prevent** — and it would have looked like noise. | tauri-app-dev | Closed |
| F-171 | **`index_meta.indexer_version` is written once, read nowhere, and its stated purpose does not exist — found by F-134's hunt on its first outing.** The DDL describes it as *"crate version that built it; **a bump rebuilds**"*. It is written at `apply.rs:357` and read by nothing; §3.4's open check reads `user_version`, `state`, `vault_root` and `note_count`, never this. **So an indexer change that alters what gets *extracted* without changing the schema — a parser that starts recognising a new inline field is the obvious case, and I3/I5 are exactly where that happens — leaves every stamp step passing over an index whose content is stale.** Vault and schema agree; only the derivation is out of date, and nothing notices. `built_at` is also write-only but benign, because nothing claims to depend on it.
