Fix is one comparison in the open check plus a `RebuildReason`, **or** a decision that the column is diagnostic and the DDL comment is wrong. **Settle before P1 ships an indexer whose output changes, because after that the wrong answer is silent.** The technique transfers: grep each column, count writes against reads, and look hardest at the ones with a stated behaviour in a comment. | code-reviewer, applying F-134 | **RULED at `895d246` (unmerged): one number, renamed `INDEX_VERSION`** — not a second constant and not a new `RebuildReason`. The stamp covers the schema *and* what the indexer extracts from unchanged input; the DDL says to bump it in the commit that changes either, with the three examples that get missed spelled out — a parser recognising an inline field it used to ignore, a block-kind mapping that stops dropping HTML, a different tokenizer. `indexer_version` is demoted to diagnostic at the column and at the write site. See F-182 for why one number rather than two. |
| F-172 | **Two of I4's three drains are not a gap, and the comment overstates the hazard.** Checked before reporting: without the `BeginBulkLoad` drain the pending rows lose their FTS entries and `'rebuild'` regenerates them; without the `EndBulkLoad` drain the rows commit after the triggers are restored and get their entries that way. **Both repair themselves from opposite ends. Only `ResolveLinks` has a consequence nothing undoes** — and removing that drain fails the new seam test alone. So the fix is a comment naming **which** drain is load-bearing and under what change the others become so, not more tests. | code-reviewer, looking before filing | **Fixed at `44193a7`, which is NOT on main** — the lead merged the earlier tip `fd160d6` as `d9009b4`, so **main currently carries the overstating comment.** The corrected version names `ResolveLinks` as load-bearing, explains that the other two repair themselves from opposite ends of the FTS `'rebuild'`, and says they become load-bearing the moment the sequence changes. One commit to pick up when the hold lifts. |
| F-173 | **When a measurement method is retired, everything it produced is provisional until re-taken — and nothing here records which numbers came from which method.** The walk's ~600 ms was published, cited in §3.4 and reported to the owner before its method was discredited; the discrediting was applied forward only. **There is currently no way to ask "which figures came from blocked sampling?" of this project's own record.** A provenance field on every recorded measurement would answer it in seconds; without one, retiring a method means re-reading everything it might have touched. | code-reviewer, generalising F-153 | Open - the register should carry method provenance |
| F-174 | **The shipped `scale_check` sits exactly on its own boundary, at the one ratio where the answer matters.** With a true p95 ratio of **1.20** against a flat **20%** tolerance, the blocked job **reports a real effect and a sampling artefact identically.** That is sharper than F-147's "it cannot decide": it can decide, and at the decisive value it decides wrong with equal confidence either way. The paired-difference CI from F-141 is the statistic that behaves in both directions — with F-164's caveat that on a heavy-tailed quantity it must be a rank statistic or a direct p95 comparison rather than a mean. | code-reviewer, sharpening their own finding | Open - test-engineer, with F-147 and F-164 |
| F-175 | **Both obvious fixes for F-171 are wrong, and implementing the DDL's stated behaviour would be worse than the gap.** The column holds `CARGO_PKG_VERSION`, so a check implemented as written would **rebuild 20,000 notes after a patch release that changed a log message.** Users would learn that updating means waiting, and *the pressure to disable the check would be immediate and reasonable* — a guard people switch off is worse than none, which is this project's own F-152 argument arriving from the other direction. The gap is nonetheless real and it is a hole in ADR-0002's reasoning: **"derived and rebuildable" makes the schema stamp sufficient only if the schema is the only thing the derivation depends on, and it is not.** Proposal: a `DERIVATION_VERSION` — the same shape as `SCHEMA_VERSION`, one layer down, bumped deliberately by whoever changes extraction, with **its own `RebuildReason`, because "rebuilding because the indexer improved" and "rebuilding because your index is corrupt" are different sentences to a user.** Recorded at the write site with both options so the merged code does not carry a silent gap. | rust-core-dev, rejecting the obvious fix | Open - architect's call |
| F-176 | **Chaining the gate and the commit in one command defeats the point of running the gate.** Self-reported: the run reported `test exit=101` — a stale `cargo.exe` holding the test binaries, LNK1104 rather than a real failure, and clean on re-verification — **but the commit had already happened.** The commit is sound; the sequencing was not. F-51 said *run the gates at the tip you hand over*; this is the same rule failing one step earlier, because a gate whose result cannot stop the commit is not a gate. | rust-core-dev, self-reported | Standing caution |
| F-177 | **Correction to the lead's framing of who found the unfailable tests.** Of the eight found in one teammate's own code, **three were found by the reviewer's mutations first, and two were in *fixes* for earlier instances of the same pattern.** Their summary is the useful part and it is less flattering than the compliment it corrects: **"The technique found them every time; reading found them none of the times, including my own re-reading."** And on the formulation the lead praised — it came from writing the bad assertion and being shown why it fired, which is the route every one of those findings took. *"Be careful about assertions" is not transferable; "an assertion is a claim about all paths, so a search that covers one path cannot justify one" is.* | rust-core-dev, declining a compliment accurately | Standing |
| F-178 | **The judgement is now on p95 — the statistic NFR-PRF-01 itself asserts — with rank statistics beside it as the sensitive detectors.** `judge_scale` compares the two p95s and reports the paired **median** difference and an **exact two-sided sign test**. The reason is F-164 made concrete: in the run behind F-141 the mean's CI included zero while the sign test gave p ≈ 0.04 and the p95 ratio was 1.20 — **a mean would have called a real effect noise.** | test-engineer, on tauri-app-dev's input | Closed |
| F-179 | **Writing the mutation for the p95 change found that the test could not tell p95 from median.** Every arm in it was flat, so the two coincided and swapping the judgement between them went unnoticed. There is now a **tail-only case — four slow spawns in twenty, median untouched, p95 tripled** — which is the shape the real regression has. **Third time this session a test of the same author passed for a reason other than the one intended, and each time the mutation found it rather than the reading.** | test-engineer, self-reported | Closed - 40 mutations, 40 caught |
| F-180 | **The tolerance is a marginal catch for the one regression it was chosen to catch, and that is written into the harness doc rather than left implicit.** Removing the walk deferral costs a **p95 ratio of 1.20** against a flat **20% tolerance** — measured paired, after the same blocked-sampling error had inflated the original estimate about tenfold. So the assertion sits *exactly* on its threshold for that specific defect rather than clearly failing it. Its author recorded that **rather than quietly enjoying the headroom** they no longer have. | test-engineer | Open - the threshold may need revisiting once more regressions are known |
| F-181 | **A measurement the harness does not own is one nobody can re-run at the gate.** Three working sampling scripts were offered and declined in favour of implementing the same designs inside the harness. Recorded because the tempting move — take the working script, get the number sooner — produces a figure whose provenance dies with the person who ran it, which is F-173's problem created deliberately. | test-engineer, declining an offer that would have been faster | Standing principle |
| F-188 | **Three conditions on the corpus-digest test, and the first decides whether it works at all.** (a) **The failure message must demand *both* actions — update the digest AND bump `INDEX_VERSION`.** The natural response to a red golden test is to update the golden file, and **someone who does only that has reintroduced the silent failure inside the mechanism built to prevent it.** (b) The digest must be **canonical**, or it becomes a flaky job that gets disabled — which is F-152's "a guard people switch off is worse than none" arriving again. (c) **The corpus's coverage is its ceiling**, so it must include at least one construct currently *dropped*, since "we stopped dropping X" is precisely the change class the test exists to catch. | architect, ruling on rust-core-dev's F-185 proposal | Open - P1, needs I3 |
| F-189 | **"If you can't write two rules that differ, you don't have two things."** How the deciding argument in F-182 was actually found: its author set out to write the bump rule for the *second* constant and **found themselves writing the same sentence twice.** A test for whether a proposed split is real, available before any code exists and cheaper than the design conversation it replaces. | architect | Standing principle |
| F-190 | **The corpus-digest test enforces *noticing*, not the bump — and I3's description must say so.** The condition that the failure message demand both actions makes the second **stated but not checked**, and there is no way to check it: storing the version beside the digest does not help, because **updating the digest alone leaves a file that is internally consistent and wrong**, and a test cannot read git history. So the honest framing is that **the enforcement is the diff, not the test** — a change touching the digest but not the constant is visibly wrong *to a reviewer*, and the test's real job is to **make that change appear in the diff at all**, which today it does not. Still a large gain, because F-134 existed precisely because the change was invisible. But **I3's description must say "it enforces noticing", or someone will read "this test enforces the bump" and stop.** | rust-core-dev, bounding a mechanism they proposed | Open - wording is part of the I3 deliverable |
| F-191 | **A corpus assembled for convenience passes through exactly the changes it exists to catch.** Its author's own instinct was *"a handful of representative notes"* — which is a corpus that exercises **what the parser already handles**, and is therefore blind to "we stopped dropping HTML". **It has to be chosen adversarially against the change classes**, including at least one construct currently dropped. The same shape as the benchmark vault's 51,600 decoy fields inside code fences: a fixture that only contains what the code already does is a fixture that cannot fail. | architect's condition; rust-core-dev naming the instinct it corrects | Open - P1 |
| F-192 | **Imprecision belongs in the UI, not the contract — a shell can render a human sentence from a precise variant, but cannot recover precision from a vague one.** The reason `Outdated` was rejected in favour of `IndexVersionChanged`, generalised by its recipient to **the whole IPC surface** rather than the one variant. A vague name in a contract costs every consumer the information; a precise one costs one consumer a translation. | architect's ruling; rust-core-dev generalising it | Standing principle |
| F-196 | **I5 BLOCKING — the save path's documented recovery cannot happen, for the same reason the save path exists.** `incremental.rs:147-149` says `QueueFull` is *"the caller's cue to drop this and let a rescan find it, exactly as the watcher path does"*. Traced: `write_atomic` records the hash for echo suppression **before** the rename → `apply` submits → queue full → `QueueFull` returned and the op is gone → **the watcher sees the event, matches the echo entry, and drops it** → nothing ever offers that path again. The rescan it relies on is serat-vault's `WorkQueue` overflow latch, fed by the watcher; **the writer's queue is a different queue with no latch**, and `grep` over `crates/serat-index/` finds only three comments asserting a recovery implemented nowhere. **Echo suppression dropping our own watcher event is precisely why the save path had to exist — and the same mechanism removes the fallback the docstring points at.** The note is missing from the index until the next full rebuild, silently. Reachable whenever the writer is behind — a rebuild, a bulk operation, a sync tool catching up — **which is exactly when someone is also typing and saving.** Same family as I2's original BLOCKING 1: *a recovery named in a comment that does not reach the case it is named for*, found by asking **who actually performs the recovery.** Fix recommended: on a refused submit, **forget the echo entry** — one call, the watcher event survives, and the note indexes one coalescing window later, which is FR-VLT-04's 500 ms and the right budget for a rare backpressure event. | code-reviewer | Open - BLOCKING; needs `forget_echo` on `NoteSource` (architect) |
| F-197 | **I6's no-retry guard does not bite, and the instrument to close it is already in the fixture, unused.** A single silent retry added to the failure arm left every test passing: `a_rebuild_that_fails_leaves_unavailable_rather_than_retrying` uses a fixture that refuses **every** enumeration, so a second attempt fails identically and the end state is unchanged. Its status-count assertion catches a retry that **re-announces itself** and misses one that does not — **which is the likelier shape, since the mistake is a loop, not a second `on_status`.** `FakeVault` already carries an `entries_calls` counter with an accessor. One line. | code-reviewer, by mutation | Open - SHOULD-FIX on I6 |
| F-198 | **The boundary of what NFR-REL-02 has been shown to satisfy, recorded so T1 knows what it still owes.** Every I6 test runs against `FakeVault`, so **classification and ordering are proven and real-disk behaviour is not.** The requirement's stated criterion is a *truncated* index, which the stamp does catch — **so a reader could reasonably conclude the requirement is met.** It is met **for truncation and the five classified reasons**, and **untested against page-level damage**, which passes all six stamp steps and needs the `IndexFault` classifier that does not exist. Volunteered by the branch's author rather than found in review. | code-reviewer, recording rather than raising | Open - T1 scope |
| F-199 | **`cargo test --workspace` fails on Windows with `link.exe exit code 1104` and passes clean on retry — it will look like a red gate to whoever hits it next.** A file lock, not a branch defect; observed twice on I6 and once by rust-core-dev, whose commit-before-confirming (F-176) was triggered by the same thing. **A single retry clears it.** Worth knowing before someone treats a red gate as a real failure or, worse, stops trusting the gate. | code-reviewer, operational | Standing note |
| F-200 | **F-196's fix is not one call — the dependency rule forbids it, which makes it a third addition to a ratified surface.** `serat-index` **cannot reach the `EchoSuppressor`**: it lives on `Vault` in `serat-vault`, and §2.1 forbids that dependency. So forgetting the echo entry has to be a **defaulted `forget_echo` method on the `NoteSource` trait in `serat-core`** — the third widening of a surface architect ratified as fixed, **which needs architect's confirmation rather than the agreement of the two people who found it.** The alternative — returning a *"you must forget this hash"* obligation to `serat-app` — is the **correctness-in-the-wrong-crate outcome**: it makes the shell responsible for an invariant of the indexing pipeline. The trait method is probably right; it is a call to make deliberately on resume, not an assumption to inherit. | code-reviewer and rust-core-dev, independently; needs architect | Open - decide before I5's fix is written |
| F-201 | **The practice that found most of this spike's defects: refuse to accept a sentence as evidence.** Its author's summary, and the one line most worth carrying out of P0 — *"Six of eight mutations invisible in B2, three drains and a no-retry rule that could not fail, a rescan named in a comment that lived in another crate twice over. **In every case the code was reviewable and the claim about the code was the thing that was wrong.**"* The practice that follows: **a guard, a recovery and a measurement each need someone to check that the thing they name can actually happen.** Every named class in this register is a special case of it — F-58 (the setup never reaches the path), F-69 (the guard is tested and its caller is not), F-107 (the guard fires and means the wrong thing), F-127 (an assertion claims all paths from a one-path search), F-147 (the fix reached the analysis and not the instrument), F-196 (the recovery is named in a comment and implemented nowhere). | code-reviewer | **The standing lesson of P0** |
| F-202 | **It was not two people agreeing — it was one intuition held twice.** The second author wrote *"your paint gate makes that path strictly worse"* **after** the first had said it, so it presented as independent corroboration. **"Independent agreement is normally the strongest evidence available, and here it was the weakest, because neither of us had run it."** Sharper than F-194: the failure is not that two people were wrong, it is that **agreement was manufactured by sequence and then counted as evidence.** The check: ask whether the second party reached it before or after hearing the first. | rust-core-dev, on their own agreement | Standing principle |
| F-203 | **An isolated benchmark for a cost that is fundamentally about contention is a category error.** The walk-cost test measures **1.85 s isolated** against **3.1 s in situ** — a 1.7x gap, because one measures the crate on an idle machine and the other measures the walk against the disk and cores the shell is using. **The test stays**, because it does the one job it was written for — failing if `canonicalize`-per-entry returns — **but it must not be read as predicting anything, and that goes in the test's own comment rather than being left to inference.** Its author: *"I made it."* | rust-core-dev, on their own instrument | Open - comment on the test |
| F-204 | **The strip is necessary and probably not sufficient, and the structural answer is a requirements question.** Applying the isolated 77% saving optimistically to the in-situ figures: 20k **might** fit at the median and not at p95; 50k probably does not fit at all, since the non-walk remainder alone is ~1 s and scales at 2.07x. **Neither sentence should be believed until measured with `--exit-after-walk`.** The structural fix is a **streaming walk**: `Vault::walk` collects a `Vec` and returns when the last file is found, so **the first row and the last row become visible at the same moment** — which is exactly what makes the budget scale with vault size. Emitting entries as found would render the first screen in milliseconds and satisfy FR-VLT-01 at 200,000 notes as easily as at 20,000. Same principle architect used for the deferral: **remove the size-proportionality rather than shrink the constant.** It costs something real — sorting needs the whole set, so a streaming tree arrives in directory order and sorts client-side. **And the whole question turns on whether FR-VLT-01's "tree" means *complete* or *first usable*, which is a requirements question, not an implementation one.** | rust-core-dev | Open - flagged as an Open question in prd.md (not ruled on — see F-214's row) |
| F-205 | **Assert the rule, not something the rule usually causes — and this is the likelier mistake once someone has learned the first one.** I6's no-retry guard asserted *exactly one `Rebuilding` status was published*, which catches a retry that re-announces itself and misses one that does not — **and a silent loop is the likelier shape, because someone wraps the call, they do not add a second `on_status`.** The one-line fix (`entries_calls() == 1`) was rejected by its own author as **counting an implementation detail**: a rebuild that legitimately enumerated twice would fail a test about retries for a reason unrelated to retries. **The chosen fix distinguishes the designs by outcome — a source that refuses once and then succeeds must end `Unavailable` under §7.1 and ends `Ready` under any retry** — which cannot be satisfied by a retry that happens to fail again, nor broken by a change that is not a retry. Distinct from F-58 and F-69: **the path is entered correctly and the assertion is on a consequence rather than the rule.** | code-reviewer found it; rust-core-dev sharpened the fix | Open - SHOULD-FIX on I6 |
| F-206 | **"NFR-REL-02 satisfied" and "NFR-REL-02 satisfied for the cases the stamp can see" are different claims, and only the second is true.** The requirement's acceptance criterion is *specifically truncation* — the case the stamp **does** catch — so a reader can reasonably conclude it is fully met. It is met for truncation and the five classified reasons; it is **untested against page-level damage**, which passes all six stamp steps and needs the `IndexFault` classifier that does not exist. | rust-core-dev, correcting their own earlier framing after the reviewer's prompt | Open - the gate report must use the second wording |
| F-210 | **Ruled: not a fourth `NoteSource` method — a separate one-method trait, `EchoGuard`.** `Vault` implements both; the save path takes `&dyn EchoGuard`; `rebuild` takes only `&dyn NoteSource` and never sees it. **The reason is the same one two previous rulings turned on:** `NoteSource` means *"somewhere notes can be enumerated and read from"*, and `forget_echo` is write-path bookkeeping — accepting the fourth method would be the `SCHEMA_VERSION`/`SchemaChanged` mistake again, **in the direction convenient for the ruler, since it costs no new type.** Second reason: every stub implementing `NoteSource` today exists to drive `rebuild`, which cannot produce an echo, so the method would oblige each to say something about a concern it has no relationship to. **And the default question dissolves** — with a separate trait there is nothing to default, the signature requires an `EchoGuard`, and the silent-no-op cannot occur. **The tell that it is a better decomposition rather than a stricter version of the same one: it gets the compile-time property *and* avoids the cost.** | architect, ruling against their own convenience | Closed - decision made, code pending |
| F-211 | **Echo suppression is an optimisation that had acquired the power to lose data.** Without it, a save is merely indexed twice — **wasteful and correct**. The refusal path made it **the only reason a note goes missing.** The clearest statement of why F-196 is a blocker rather than a defect: the mechanism did not fail, it was given an authority its failure mode could not carry. | architect, naming what the bug actually is | Standing |
| F-212 | **Two interacting mitigations measured one at a time will each look fine and the pair will not — so the 2x2 is the minimum, not thoroughness.** The deferral's value **inverts** when the walk gets cheap, and nobody would have predicted that from the design; it appeared only because someone crossed two variables instead of measuring one. Both mitigations were individually justified by measurements that were individually correct. | rust-core-dev, on tauri-app-dev's correction | Open - the 2x2 runs before the walk is touched |
| F-213 | **The strip and the gate have different futures, and the asymmetry decides the order.** **The strip survives streaming** — a streaming walk should not canonicalise every entry either, so it is a **defect fix, not a mitigation**. **The gate probably does not**: it protects a paint from a walk that would no longer threaten it, and under the strip alone it becomes a net cost of ~500 ms. **Its value is already conditional and would go negative in two different futures.** So: land the strip, run the 2x2, and **let the gate's fate be decided by numbers rather than by either party defending it.** | rust-core-dev | Open - sequencing for resume |
| F-214 | **Under streaming, `t_usable`'s "the tree has been delivered" stops having a moment — and that reframes the whole question.** The precondition becomes *the first screen is renderable*, which is **a different claim, satisfiable at 200,000 notes where the current one is not at 50,000.** Hence: **framed as an implementation choice, streaming competes with the strip; framed as a requirements question, it supersedes it** — if the requirement is rewritten that way, streaming is the only design that satisfies it, and the strip and gate become arguments about how long a design that *cannot* satisfy it takes to fail. Architect reads FR-VLT-01 as first-usable and thinks the design should stream, **but declines to rule that the requirement means it**: *"populated and navigable"* can be read either way, so re-baselining is product-analyst's at Q1, and the design position plus the measurements go in front of them **so they decide with the numbers rather than from the wording.** | rust-core-dev's framing; architect's ruling and its limit | Open - flagged as an Open question in prd.md (not ruled on — FR-VLT-01 is P1, outside the P0 gate, and changing its wording is a design call architect/rust-core-dev should weigh in on first) |
| F-215 | **When the stricter version of a decision and the better decomposition come apart, the strictness was standing in for a design nobody had found.** Its author, on arguing about whether `forget_echo` should have a default: *"I was arguing about how to defend a boundary that was in the wrong place."* The `EchoGuard` split gets the compile-time property the strictness was *for*, at no cost — which is the signal that the argument about strictness was a symptom. **The check: if you are debating how strictly to enforce something, ask whether the thing being enforced is in the right place.** | rust-core-dev, on their own position | Standing principle |
| F-216 | **The recovery chain for F-196's fix does not terminate where it appears to, and its author is verifying rather than asserting it.** Forgetting the echo restores the watcher event — **but the watcher's queue may also be full, since the writer being behind is usually a symptom of the same load.** So the chain ends not at *"the watcher enqueues it"* but at **"the watcher enqueues it, *or* drops it and latches an overflow generation that a rescan covers."** Its author believes that terminates correctly because ADR-0011's latch is exactly that mechanism and they built it — **and says that is precisely the shape of belief that produced this bug: a recovery asserted without tracing it to whoever performs it.** So it gets **a test that fills both queues** rather than a claim. F-201 applied to their own fix, before writing it. | rust-core-dev | Open - verify with the fix |
| F-217 | **An optimisation is safe exactly while the thing it removes is genuinely redundant — and the bug is always that some path stopped doing the work it assumed had happened.** The general form of F-211. Echo suppression's job is avoiding a redundant index; the refusal path removed the work it assumed had already happened, and the optimisation silently became the only reason a note goes missing. **Worth applying to every other "we can skip this because X already did it" in the design.** | rust-core-dev, generalising architect's framing | Standing principle |
| F-218 | **A signature that passes the same object twice, chosen deliberately over a struct that would exist only to shorten a call.** `apply(&dyn NoteSource, &dyn NoteParser, &dyn EchoGuard, &VaultPath)` — and since `Vault` implements two of those, `serat-app`'s call site passes it twice. *"Explicit rather than elegant."* Recorded because the tidier alternative would have introduced a type whose only purpose is to hide that two roles are currently filled by one object — which is the thing worth seeing at the call site. | rust-core-dev | Closed - decision made |
| F-219 | **The agreed fix for F-196 is a no-op on the interleaving that matters — there is an earlier broken link than the one named.** `take_echo` **consumes** the entry (`entries.remove(&hash).is_some()`), deliberately, so two identical writes produce two events and one recorded hash suppresses exactly one. Correct as designed. **But the save path is `expect(hash)` → rename → parse → submit, and the watcher event can be handled at any point after the rename — including *before* the submit is refused.** If it wins, the entry is already gone, `forget_echo` finds nothing, and **the note is lost exactly as it is today.** In practice the submit almost always wins, because the watcher coalesces for 150 ms and parse-and-submit takes a few — but **"almost always" on a race whose losing branch silently drops a note is the pattern this spike has found five times.** Found by reading `echo.rs` rather than reasoning about it, one message after the fix was agreed by three people. | architect | Open - changes the fix |
| F-220 | **Record-early fails lossy; record-late fails redundant — and that asymmetry is the argument.** The proposed variant records the echo **after the submit succeeds** rather than before the write: a refused submit means **no entry was ever recorded**, so the watcher event proceeds normally; and if the watcher wins the race, the note is indexed **redundantly, which is wasteful and correct.** §3.2 chose early for a stated and correct reason — an entry recorded too late suppresses nothing — but **"suppresses nothing" costs a duplicate index, and duplicate indexing is idempotent by path.** This **removes** the race rather than narrowing it. `EchoGuard` is unaffected either way: both orderings need serat-index to reach the suppressor, so **only the verb changes — `record_echo` on success rather than `forget_echo` on refusal.** | architect | Open - rust-core-dev to weigh with the code |
| F-221 | **Two questions deliberately left to the person with the code in front of them.** Whether moving `expect` out of `write_atomic` breaks a caller relying on the write being self-suppressing; and whether the redundant-index branch stays harmless at **P2's write-back layer**, where a duplicate parse might interact with the stale-hash guard. If either answers no, `forget_echo` plus a test on the earlier link is the right answer. Recorded because the ruling was offered **with its own limits stated and an explicit invitation not to take the ordering argument on trust** — from the party whose two previous rulings in this thread were both accepted. | architect | Open - rust-core-dev decides |
| F-222 | **It is not a race — the current placement already breaks the only caller that exists, on every call.** `serat-app/src/state.rs:367` is the **sole production caller of `write_atomic`**: it writes, re-reads for mtime, and returns — **and never submits to the index.** So with `expect` inside `write_atomic`: `write_file` → `write_atomic` → `expect(hash)` → the watcher sees the rename → `take_echo` matches → **the event is dropped and nothing ever indexes the file.** Not a corner and not an interleaving: **the steady-state behaviour of S1's only write path.** Architect's ordering argument said record-early fails lossy *on an interleaving*; for this caller **it fails lossy on every call, because there is no submit to race against.** | rust-core-dev, reading the caller rather than the mechanism | Open - fixed by the late variant |
| F-223 | **Widen the write-only sweep from columns to any recorded-then-consumed pair — the trap arms when the consumer is wired, not when the producer is written.** `take_echo` has **no production caller**: four hits across `crates/`, one doc comment and three tests. So the recording is **inert today**, there is **no live data loss**, and **the defect arms the moment I5 wires the consumer.** Same class as F-134/F-171 — something written and never read, correct-looking, wrong the moment someone starts reading it — but **found in a *field* rather than a column, which is why the `index_meta` sweep missed it.** The generalisation: **"the consumer does not exist yet" is exactly the state in which the producer's placement goes unchallenged.** | rust-core-dev, extending the reviewer's own technique | Open - widen the sweep |
| F-224 | **Resolution: the late variant, and `forget_echo` ceases to exist.** `EchoGuard::record_echo(hash)` is called **after a successful submit**; `expect` moves out of `write_atomic` into serat-index's orchestration where F-04 puts it; **`write_atomic` goes back to being a write, which is what its name says.** The race **stops mattering rather than narrowing**: a refused submit records nothing, a watcher-wins means a redundant index, and `upsert_note` is delete-then-insert by path, so that is idempotent. **S1's `write_file` is fixed as a side effect** — its writes become watcher-indexed rather than silently suppressed — **and that is being announced to its owner precisely because it changes their behaviour without changing their code.** On the P2 question, its author separated reasoned from measured: a redundant index is read-only with respect to the vault so **cannot** move a byte range or change a hash under the patcher, **but they cannot rule out that it consumes writer-queue depth when the patcher wants to submit — a backpressure concern, not a stale-guard one.** | rust-core-dev, on architect's variant | Open - to write on resume |
| F-225 | **"A dropped watcher event is recovered by the rescan latch" is load-bearing for the entire backpressure design and is asserted in three comments and tested in none.** The both-queues-full test is being written **even though the chosen fix no longer needs it**, because that sentence is what the whole design rests on — and it is the sentence that started this thread. | rust-core-dev | Open - test on resume |
| F-226 | **§3.2 and §3.1.1 are now wrong, and their author has queued the fix *ahead of D4*.** §3.2 says *"Before writing, the writer records the hash it is about to produce"*; §3.1.1 step 1 says the same. Both are about to be contradicted in code by the late-recording fix. Their author's reason for the ordering: **"the document does not get to be the last thing corrected, which is the discipline I have been asking of everyone else."** | architect | Open - first edit on resume, before D4 |
| F-227 | **A false gate number is waiting to be produced by someone doing exactly the right thing.** After the fix, S1's `write_file` becomes **watcher-indexed**, which is correct — but it means its writes travel **FR-VLT-04's 500 ms path rather than NFR-PRF-03's 50 ms one**, because **S1 has no direct submit; I5 is what adds that.** So anyone benchmarking a save against the current shell will measure several hundred milliseconds and **report NFR-PRF-03 missed by an order of magnitude — having actually measured the watcher path working correctly on a shell with no save path.** The remedy is the same as F-03's: **name which path a write took**, in the task or in the trace, rather than reporting one number. | architect, pre-empting a measurement nobody has taken yet | Open - a line in the task or the trace |
| F-228 | **An unread write is not dead code — it is an unvalidated decision.** Architect's compression of rust-core-dev's F-223, and the form that makes it **searchable rather than a moral: any recorded-then-consumed pair whose consumer is absent.** The existing sweep looks for write-only *columns* in `index_meta` and would not have found a write-only *field* in serat-vault. **Dead code is inert; an unvalidated decision is a commitment nobody has had to defend, waiting for the day something starts depending on it.** | architect compressing rust-core-dev's generalisation | Open - to the reviewer as a widened sweep |
| F-229 | **The remedy for F-227: make the misattribution a red job rather than a plausible number.** A `path` discriminant on `SaveTiming` (`save` vs `watcher`), **refused by the harness unless it reads `save`**, with the failure message naming **FR-VLT-04's 500 ms as what a watcher figure belongs to.** Its author's reason: *"that makes the misattribution a red job rather than a plausible number, which is the only version of this that survives someone in a hurry."* And they were explicit that the location is negotiable and the property is not — a trace line does as well: **"I care that it is recorded at the source, not where."** Note the harness measuring the library directly already protects the number *it* produces; **this protects the number someone else produces**, which is the case F-227 describes. | test-engineer, on architect's and rust-core-dev's warning | Open - on resume |
| F-230 | **A stale seam that fails as a compile error is the better failure, and the paragraph survives the rename.** `SAVE_ENTRY_POINT` is two revisions stale — `apply_and_wait` gone, `apply` gains a third trait object, `rebuild` takes `vault_root`. **It breaks the build rather than producing a wrong number**, which is the outcome to prefer. And the split matters: **the *paragraph* about bypassing the watcher stays correct — only the function name is wrong** — because the bypass is §3.2's requirement rather than a benchmarking convenience, and that sentence survives any rename. | rust-core-dev checking a teammate's file while in it; test-engineer agreeing | Open - trivial fix on resume |
| F-231 | **The whole workspace stopped building because a directory was renamed, and nothing in the source tree was wrong.** The repository folder moved `AIResearch` → `serat`; afterwards every `cargo test --workspace` died in `serat-app`'s build script reading `'\?\D:\Projects\gaws\AIResearch\target\debug\build\tauri-*\out\permissions\app\autogenerated\commands\app_hide.toml'`. Tauri's build script publishes its permissions directory as an **absolute** path in `target/debug/build/tauri-*/output`, and consumers read it back; the cached value survived the rename because the cache is keyed on inputs that did not change. `cargo clean -p tauri` cleared it. **The general form is the one worth keeping: derived state that records absolute paths is not derived state you can move** — C-02 says the index is disposable and rebuildable, and this is the same claim failing for `target/`, which nobody had thought of as state at all. The tell was that the failing path named a directory that no longer existed, which is cheap to check and was not the first thing anyone checked. | lead, on resume | Closed - `cargo clean -p tauri`; each teammate worktree carries its own `target/` and needs the same |
| F-232 | **Six worktrees reported `prunable` and none of them was lost — `prunable` is a claim about a path, not about content.** After the rename `git worktree list` showed all six teammate worktrees at their old `AIResearch` paths marked prunable, which reads as "this is safe to delete" and is the sentence a lead in a hurry acts on. The directories were intact at the new path with every branch committed and clean; `git worktree repair` with the six new paths fixed both the registrations and the broken `.git` files. **Pruning first would have destroyed nothing recoverable — every branch also lives in the primary repository, exactly as coding-standards.md promises — but the promise is what made it safe, not the checking.** Recorded because the recovery order matters: look before pruning, and know *why* deleting would have been survivable rather than discovering it afterwards. | lead, on resume | Closed |
| F-233 | **The TaskCompleted gate rejected every completion, and its message named a cause that was never the cause.** The hook extracted its fields with a `grep -o` pattern requiring no space after the colon. That fails three ways: it matches nothing when the payload is pretty-printed with a space after the colon — which is how it is actually serialised — it truncates at the first embedded escaped quote, and grep is line-based so a description containing a newline is cut short. Any of the three leaves both variables empty, so rule 1 rejected with *cite the FR-/NFR-/US-/ADR- IDs* against a description citing eleven of them. **This is F-107's shape — the guard fires and means the wrong thing — with the extra cost that it teaches the wrong lesson:** its first victim added IDs, was rejected, added IDs to the subject, was rejected again. A teammate who trusts the message edits their task until they conclude the gate is broken, which is the right conclusion reached by the wrong route. **Reproduced independently by two teammates against three payload shapes**, and by the lead against seven. Fixed: the payload is parsed as JSON, and a payload that cannot be parsed **fails open with a notice** rather than rejecting — because an unreadable payload being indistinguishable from a task that cited nothing is precisely the defect. | architect and rust-core-dev, independently; fixed by lead | Closed — seven shapes verified, including the two that must still reject |
| F-234 | **Cleaning tauri was not the whole of F-231, and the second mechanism was worse than the first.** The repo root failed `cargo test --workspace` in serat-core's dependency-rule test with a manifest path under the pre-rename `AIResearch` directory — same class as F-231, derived state holding an absolute path, but a **different mechanism**: `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR` baked into a stale *test binary* that cargo will not rebuild because its inputs did not change either. `cargo clean -p serat-core` fixes it. **Why it is worth more than a build note: the stale binary is the test that mechanically enforces the dependency rule** — CLAUDE.md's central invariant — and it is also rule 3 of the completion gate, which runs the workspace tests **in the repo root rather than in the completing teammate's worktree**. So until that clean was run, every teammate's completion was gated on build state in the lead's copy that none of them could see or fix. **And the sharing is itself a defect:** the gate's test binaries run in the lead's `target/` while the lead builds in it, producing `LNK1104 - cannot open file` on a green tree, because one cargo reaches the run phase and holds a test binary open while another tries to relink it. Observed three times on three different targets. The gate now uses a dedicated `CARGO_TARGET_DIR`, which costs one build cache and buys a result that means what it says. | architect, from the gate's own second failure; mechanism and fix by lead | Closed |
| F-235 | **The record-early sentence was in four places, not the two F-226 named, and one of them was the step the other pointed at.** §3.1.1 step 1 cross-references §6.1 step 5, and §6.1 step 5 said *"recording the new hash for echo suppression first"* — so correcting only the two named passages would have left the contradiction living at the end of a pointer from one of them. The fourth was `contracts/ipc.rs`, where `write_file` documented record-early **as a feature**: *"so the watcher drops the event it is about to see"*, which is a precise description of F-222's data loss written as a guarantee. **The generalisation, and the reason this is not tidying: a rule stated once is corrected once, and a rule stated four times is corrected wherever whoever noticed it happened to be looking.** F-226 was raised from §3.2, and nobody looking at §3.2 has a reason to open the write-back section or the IPC contract. Grep for the *sentence* rather than the section, every time a mechanism changes. | architect, correcting their own finding's scope | Closed at 4014330 |
| F-236 | **F-175 answered, and the half it was right about was not the half it asked for.** F-175 proposed a second derivation constant with its own `RebuildReason`. **No second constant** (F-134 already) and **no second reason**: the check cannot draw the distinction — one number cannot say which of the two things moved — and the user does not need it, because *"this index was built by a different version of Serat"* is the same sentence either way. **But the argument underneath was correct and unaddressed:** *"the indexer improved"* and *"your index is corrupt"* are different sentences, and the enum **already drew that line** — `SchemaChanged` against `Corrupt`, mis-named. Renamed to `IndexVersion`. **The tell that a proposal is half right: it names a real distinction, then asks for a new mechanism to draw a line the existing one already draws under a wrong name.** Free today — nothing constructs the variant and nothing in `app/` reads it, which is exactly F-228's argument for doing it now. | architect, on rust-core-dev's F-175 | Closed at 756e284 |
| F-237 | **`Task::marker_range` has no column, so FR-VIW-06's task-marker half has no recorded range to patch.** Found by the F-228 sweep. `fields` carries `byte_start`/`byte_end` and apply.rs writes them; `tasks` carries no range and writes none — while `serat_core::Task::marker_range` exists and its doc calls itself *"the smallest possible instance of ADR-0008"*. FR-VIW-06 is a **Must** saying a view write replaces the bytes of the target field **or task marker**, and §6.1 step 3 verifies a *recorded* range, so the P2 patcher would have to re-locate the marker by rescanning the block — a second definition of where it is, inside the one mechanism the design says is built by constraint rather than by care. The PRD's data dictionary already leans that way, calling `tasks.block_id` "the write target when ticked from a view". **Ruled: record it**, two integers mirroring `fields`. **Why a P0 item for a P2 requirement: under ADR-0002 there is no migration, so the column added later is a forced full rebuild for every user, and today it is free.** The schema half and the write must land in one commit, or it is a write-only column and we have done F-134 to ourselves. | architect, from the F-228 sweep | Open - task #5 |
| F-238 | **§Traceability's own contract is false for 15 of 86 Musts — and the count over-reads, which is the part to say first.** The table opens *"Every requirement of priority Must in the baseline has a design element here or in the repo-ready files"*, and §10.4 exists so an absence "reads as a judgement rather than an oversight". Between them they account for 71. Missing: FR-BIN-02, FR-BIN-04, FR-BIN-19, FR-NAV-01, FR-STR-04, FR-AIX-02, FR-PLT-01/02/04, NFR-CMP-01, NFR-CMP-03, NFR-PRF-06, NFR-SEC-01, NFR-SEC-02, NFR-SEC-04. **Every one is designed in prose; this is a citation gap, not a design hole**, and reporting it as "15 Musts have no design" would be alarming and wrong. Five have no occurrence of their ID anywhere in docs/03-design or the repo-ready files — FR-AIX-02, FR-BIN-04, FR-NAV-01, FR-STR-04, NFR-CMP-01 — and those are the ones to check by reading, because *described somewhere in prose* is exactly what an untraced requirement looks like right up until it turns out not to be. **NFR-CMP-01 deserves a second look on its own merits: Obsidian vault compatibility is a Must at P1 and the word Obsidian does not appear in architecture.md once.** It is arguably the premise of the whole design rather than a line in it, which is the kind of requirement nobody verifies. | architect, checking §Traceability was current after F-226; **count corrected by its own author** | **Closed, and the number was wrong in the direction that flattered the finding — it is 8, not 15.** §10.1 and §10.2 carry their own requirement tables covering FR-PLT-01..05, NFR-SEC-01..05 and FR-BIN-19, which is proper traceability rather than the "designed in prose" this entry called it; the audit had treated §10.4 as the only supplement and did not check for others before reporting. **The technique found a real gap and the number attached to it was wrong** — and a count is the part of a finding a reader carries away, so an inflated one does damage a wrong sentence would not. The preamble now names all four tables so the next count does not have to rediscover them. Of the true 8: five lacked only a citation and are now cited; **two are real design gaps** (F-248, F-249), neither findable by grep. |
| F-239 | **Four §Traceability rows cite repo-ready files that do not exist, and a filename retires the question a blank cell would have prompted.** `architecture.md` names seven repo-ready design files by path. `schema/001_init.sql` and `contracts/ipc.rs` exist; `grammar/sq.ebnf`, `grammar/lowering.md`, `grammar/conformance.md`, `contracts/extractor.rs` and `contracts/mcp-tools.json` do not, and **there is no `grammar/` directory at all** — confirmed by the lead. The table's preamble permits coverage to live "or in the repo-ready files", so for FR-STR-06..10, FR-STR-12, FR-BIN-05..10, FR-BIN-16, FR-MCP-01/02/04, NFR-PRF-05, NFR-SEC-03, NFR-SEC-05 and NFR-REL-03 that sentence is doing the work of a design element nobody wrote. **This is a worse defect than F-238 and was found the same way:** F-238 is IDs missing from a table, which reads as incomplete; this is a table citing evidence that is not there, which reads as complete. **The conformance-suite row is the sharp case — its Where column names no section, only the file** — so for FR-STR-10 and the P2 gate the entire design element is a path that does not exist. And it is load-bearing past its own row: **C-03 is a non-negotiable and both of its verification methods point at absent files** — FR-STR-08's acceptance criterion is *"Given any query in the conformance suite…"* and FR-STR-09's method is *"Grammar review confirms no production admits arbitrary code"*, needing `grammar/conformance.md` and `grammar/sq.ebnf` respectively. §7's *"The full contract is contracts/extractor.rs"* is present tense about a file that is not there. **The general form, and the reason it belongs in the register rather than only in a task: an artifact cited by name is treated as existing by everyone downstream, and nothing in the repo checks that a path in a design document resolves.** One `test -f` over the paths named in architecture.md would have caught it on day one — now filed as a CI check so the class cannot recur, rather than as a one-time correction. | architect, re-reading their own deliverable on the idle check; confirmed by lead | Open - task #7 (the claim), task #10 (the files), task #11 (the check) |
| F-240 | **A teammate diagnosed the gate that judges their own work, and declined to fix it — which was the right call and is worth recording as a rule.** architect reproduced F-233 precisely, named the grep pattern, and then stopped: *"a teammate quietly rewriting the gate that judges their own work is the wrong shape even when the diagnosis is right."* They raised it to the lead and left task #1 sitting in `in_progress` with the work finished rather than route around the check. **The generalisation: the correctness of a diagnosis does not confer the authority to act on it, when acting on it changes what is allowed to judge you.** Cheap to honour here because the lead was available; the shape matters most when they are not, and the answer then is still to escalate rather than to self-clear. Recorded beside F-233 so the fix does not read as the whole story — the gate was broken, and the more useful fact is that the person it blocked did not quietly unblock themselves. | architect, declining to act on their own correct diagnosis | Standing principle |
| F-241 | **The fix for F-233 was itself unable to read a live payload, and it failed in the direction that looks like success.** The first JSON repair replaced the grep with a real parser but still read a *fixed set of top-level keys*, and the keys are not where it guessed — so on a real event it found nothing, took the fail-open branch, and printed a notice. Fail-open was the right design (an unreadable payload must not be indistinguishable from an uncited task) and it is exactly what hid the second defect: **the gate reported "skipping content rules" rather than "I cannot find the fields", and a notice that names no cause invites nobody to fix it.** Found only because test-engineer forwarded the notice verbatim instead of ignoring it. Now the parser walks the whole payload for the first string under any `subject`/`title`/`name` or `description`/`body`/`content` key at any depth — shape-independent by construction rather than by guessing one more layout — and when it finds nothing it **names the keys it did see**, so the next person does not have to guess either. Verified against flat, nested-under-`task`, nested three deep, unknown keys and non-JSON, plus the two shapes that must still reject. **The general form: a guard that degrades gracefully must say what it degraded to, or the graceful path becomes the silent one.** | lead, on the lead's own fix; surfaced by test-engineer forwarding the notice | Closed |
| F-242 | **`LNK1104` is a link-stage file-contention failure and the gate was reporting it as "your tests are failing".** Reproduced independently by test-engineer and the lead, in three different target directories including a freshly-created isolated one, always clean on immediate retry with no code change — so it is antivirus or the filesystem holding a just-linked `.exe`, not target-directory sharing as first diagnosed, and **the lead's first explanation was wrong**. It matters because the gate turned an environmental flake into a verdict about a teammate's code: the same defect F-233 was just fixed for, a guard firing and meaning the wrong thing, reintroduced one rule further down while fixing the rule above it. The gate now retries a link failure once and, if it survives, reports it as environmental and names the target directory rather than rejecting. **A genuine failure still fails, twice.** | test-engineer and lead, independently; first diagnosis corrected by the isolated run | Closed |
| F-243 | **A test whose name claims the ordering does not test the ordering, and it took a mutation to see it.** `a_successful_submit_records_the_echo_after_not_before` **passed unchanged** when the reviewer moved `record_echo` before `submit` — it asserts only that the hash was recorded, which is true in both orderings. The ordering is covered solely by the *refusal* test. So the suite's coverage of F-224 is real but it lives somewhere other than where the name says. **This is F-58 wearing the opposite disguise: the setup does reach the path, and the assertion is too weak to distinguish the thing the name promises** — and unlike a setup that never runs, this one is invisible to "can it fail", because it can, for the other reason. **The tell is a test name containing a claim the assertions do not mention.** | code-reviewer, by mutation, on rust-core-dev's test | Open - rename or make the ordering observable, before I5 merges |
| F-244 | **Every incremental save nulls all inbound links to the note, and nothing on that path restores them.** `links.dst_note` is `ON DELETE SET NULL` (schema/001_init.sql:201) and `resolve_links` is called from exactly one place — `rebuild.rs:183`. `apply_via` never calls it. Measured with a probe, since reverted: 1 resolved link after a full rebuild, **0 after incrementally saving either end**. That is FR-NAV-04, Backlinks, a **Must at P1**. C-02 survives — a rebuild repairs it and nothing depends on index-only state — so it is P1 work and correctly not blocking a P0 branch. **What makes it worth recording rather than merely filing: it was found while verifying a claim that turned out to be TRUE.** The reviewer set out to check F-220's idempotence argument, confirmed it (cascades are structural, no duplicate rows), and found this next door. **And the silence is the conspicuous part** — `apply.rs:188-201` and `incremental.rs:232-238` both flag comparable gaps in exactly this style, so a reader is entitled to conclude the save path is complete where it says nothing. | code-reviewer, by probe, while confirming F-220 | Open - task #13; doc note before I5 merges |
| F-245 | **`WorkQueue::close()` does not wake a producer blocked in `push_blocking`, and the rescan is where that lands.** `queue.rs:292-297` notifies `ready` only, never `space`; `push_blocking` waits on `space` (`queue.rs:182-185`). Measured: producer still blocked 200 ms after `close()` in **7 of 8 runs**, against a no-`close()` control that hangs as expected — so the occasional wake is a spurious one, not `close` working. Pre-existing on main. **Why it is not a corner case: ADR-0011's rescan uses `push_blocking` as its producer precisely so F-11's loss cannot recur, so at F-11's shape — 20,200 paths against a queue of 8,192 — the rescan spends most of its life blocked in exactly this state, and a shutdown during a rescan hits the window with high probability rather than low.** Same family as F-21, where a channel's lifetime was used as a signal and the result hung a 600 s test run. **The reviewer's first probe passed and they re-derived rather than filing it; only the control run settled it.** | code-reviewer, measured with a control | Open - task #12, one line plus a test that times out rather than hangs |
| F-246 | **The write-only sweep counted a comment as a consumer, so its nine candidates are an undercount reported as a list.** `rescan_completed` (`queue.rs:271`) has **zero production callers** — `rescan_needed()` is consumed once at `watch.rs:265` to raise the event, and nothing ever completes a rescan and clears the latch — yet it did not appear among the sweep's nine, almost certainly because `watch.rs:263` is a *comment* naming `WorkQueue::rescan_completed` and the name-based scan counted it. **The instrument was therefore reporting the opposite of its true state, and "the sweep found nine" is exactly the kind of number that gets cited later as coverage.** Strip comments as well as `mod tests` before counting. **The larger point is the pattern's persistence: recorded-then-unconsumed has now produced F-223 (a field), F-134/F-171 (columns), F-237 (a value with no column at all) and this (a method) — four instances, three mechanisms, and each was invisible to the instrument that found the previous one.** Whatever rule makes such a pair *checkable* is worth more than a longer table. | code-reviewer, finding what the architect's sweep missed and why | Open - folded into task #6 |
| F-247 | **A correct rebuild was about to be rejected by the guard that exists to catch incorrect ones.** `RebuildReport::notes` counts every row in `notes` — notes **and** assets, `kind='asset'` per ADR-0005 — while the harness's own spec assumed markdown notes only, matching the vault manifest's `counts.notes`. The 20k vault is 20,000 notes plus 200 assets, so a correct rebuild reports **20,200 against an oracle of 20,000**, and `check_note_count` and `check_analyze_freshness` would both have rejected **every correct run** on the first real measurement. **It is F-19's failure shape arriving from the opposite direction** — a partial-index-shaped rejection produced by a counting mismatch rather than a real drop — and it would have looked exactly like the defect the guard was built for. Caught when the existing rebuild-loop unit test failed as the real check was wired: **the test's own fixture had never contained assets either**, so the guard and its test were undertested in the same place, which is F-27's pattern rather than a coincidence. | test-engineer, wiring the real check | Closed - `VaultUnderTest` carries `assets`; both call sites check notes + assets |
| F-248 | **FR-NAV-01 has no design, and a near-synonym in the same document is why nobody noticed.** The quick switcher — fuzzy over titles, paths, aliases and headings, ranked by recency and frequency, a **Must at P1** — has no design element anywhere. §9 discusses a *switcher* twice, but it is the **lens** switcher: it changes role, not note. **A reader checking coverage finds the word and stops.** Most of the input exists (`notes.title`, `notes.path`, an `aliases` frontmatter field in `fields`, headings as `blocks` with `kind='heading'`); **the ranking has no home at all** — recency and frequency are recorded nowhere. **C-02 turns that from a missing column into a ruling**: per-note open counts are state, and state living only in `.serat/` is state nothing may depend on. The likely answer — they live there and ranking degrades to lexical after a rebuild — is design goal 5 working exactly as intended, but it has not been ruled, and the alternative writes a file into the user's vault they did not ask for. **The transferable part: a traceability check by keyword is defeated by a synonym in the same document, which is the precise failure mode of checking that a *word* appears rather than that a *thing* is designed.** It is also the limit on test-engineer's #11 and on any grep-shaped audit — those check that a cited path resolves, not that a citation means anything. | architect, on the reading pass task #7 required | Open - P1 ruling, now in §Open design questions |
| F-249 | **NFR-CMP-03's two halves have different answers, and the design contradicts itself on the second.** *"The index directory shall be gitignored by default and shall never be required for correctness."* The second half **is** designed and covered — a fresh clone without `.serat/` rebuilds on first open, §3.4 step 1 and ADR-0002. The first is not: nothing says who writes `.serat/` into a `.gitignore`, or when. **§3.4 *names* NFR-CMP-03, which is what made it look covered, and naming a requirement is not designing it** — the same shape as F-239, where a cited filename retired the question a blank cell would have prompted. Worse, the same paragraph says *"whether the vault is a git repository is the user's business and not ours"*, which is in direct tension with shipping a default that edits the user's `.gitignore`. **A requirement whose two halves are satisfied by opposite postures is one that was written as a single line and needs splitting or narrowing**, and that is a requirements decision rather than a design one. | architect | Open - product-analyst at Q1 |
| F-250 | **The completion gate was fixed twice and read the payload on neither, because both fixes guessed at the key names instead of looking.** The real keys are `task_subject` and `task_description`; the first repair looked for `subject`/`description` at the top level, the second walked the whole payload at any depth for `subject`/`title`/`name` — and still missed, because depth was never the problem and the *names* were. **Two rounds of increasingly general machinery, each built on an unchecked assumption that one printed line would have settled.** What finally produced the answer was the fail-open branch printing the keys it actually saw, which is the one thing neither fix had until the second. **The lesson is not "parse JSON properly" — that was learned at F-233 and did not help. It is that a guess about an interface deserves one observation before it deserves a generalisation**, and that instrumenting the failure to name what it saw was worth more than both attempts at making the reader cleverer. Recorded against the lead, who wrote both. | lead, on the lead's own two fixes; keys supplied by architect from the gate's own output | Closed - rules 1 and 2 verified live against the real payload shape |
| F-251 | **Stripping comments doubled the sweep's output, from 9 candidates to 18 — so the first run's number was not a list, it was an artefact of the instrument.** Two method changes did it. **Comments and doc comments stripped, string literals kept**: a name merely *mentioned* in prose had been reading as a consumer, which is how `rescan_completed` hid behind a comment naming it; literals must survive or every schema column reads as dead, since its only consumer is SQL inside one. **`mod tests` split by brace-matching rather than truncation**: truncating at the first `#[cfg(test)]` would be wrong, because `writer.rs:497` uses it to guard a non-module item and real production code sits after it. A third bucket for `bench/` was kept separate, because vaultgen emits vault *content* whose words collide with identifiers — `owner` and `est` showed 11 and 6 "consumers" that were generated note text. **The transferable point is not the number: it is that a sweep's output is a claim about the sweep, and the first version of the instrument was wrong in the direction that made the codebase look cleaner.** | code-reviewer, re-running the architect's sweep | Closed - 400 items over 38 production / 30 test / 21 bench files |
| F-252 | **Echo suppression will silently stop working the day its consumer is wired, and the number that decides it is 2 seconds.** `take_echo` has no production consumer — `serat-app/src/vault.rs:164-168` drains the `WorkQueue` and throws every path away, honestly labelled as having no consumer yet. The consumer that will call it sits behind the 150 ms coalescing window **plus whatever queue backlog exists**, against `ECHO_LIFETIME` of 2 s (`echo.rs:26`). **At F-11's shape — 20,200 paths against 8,192 capacity — a path waits far longer than 2 s, the entry has expired, and suppression does nothing: every Serat save is reindexed by the watcher, which is precisely what §3.2 exists to prevent.** It fails *safe* rather than lossy, which is why it will not announce itself: the index stays correct and the work merely doubles. **This is F-217 arriving exactly where its author generalised it** — an optimisation is safe only while the thing it removes is genuinely redundant, and here the optimisation quietly stops applying rather than quietly breaking. F-224's late recording moves the record strictly later but does not make this materially worse, because the dominant delay is consumer-side; **so this is not a reason to hold I5, and it is a reason to validate 2 s against measured queue latency before the consumer lands rather than after.** | code-reviewer, from the widened sweep | Open - validate ECHO_LIFETIME before the consumer is wired |
| F-253 | **Three columns in `tasks` name their own source in a comment and are written by nobody, and the data to fill them already exists.** `schema/001_init.sql`'s `CREATE TABLE tasks` declares nine columns and annotates three — `due TEXT -- from due:: (ISO-8601)`, `owner TEXT -- from owner::`, `est REAL -- from est:: (the usual rollup target)`. `insert_task` (`apply.rs:317-336`) writes **five**: `note_id, block_id, status, done, text`. The other three are NULL for **every task row that will ever exist**. `bench/vaultgen/src/note.rs` already generates `due::`, `owner::` and `est::` into the benchmark vault, **so the data is present and only the writer is missing** — meaning the day someone writes the SQ rollup that `est` is annotated as existing for, it returns nothing and **looks like an empty vault rather than a missing writer.** Same table and same shape as F-237's `marker_range`. **The comment is what makes it dangerous rather than merely incomplete: an annotation naming the source reads as a description of behaviour, and retires the question of whether anything implements it** — F-239's mechanism in a DDL instead of a traceability table. | code-reviewer, reading `insert_task` beside the DDL | Open - folded into task #5 |
| F-254 | **The sweep's own stated limit cost it a real hit, and only reading the producer found it.** `due` **never surfaced as a candidate at all**, because `serat-app/src/index.rs:185` has an unrelated local binding `let due = ...` and the scan is name-based. The limit was written down in the task before the run — one name for two things reads as consumed — and it still removed a true positive from the output rather than merely adding noise. **So the instrument's failure mode is not "too many candidates to triage": it is silent omission, in the direction of looking clean.** The finding was recovered by reading `insert_task` next to the DDL, which is what the task said the sweep does not replace. Worth keeping beside F-251: the same run both over-reported (comments as consumers, before the fix) and under-reported (collisions), and neither error announced itself. **Also flagged: `SchemaMismatch` (`serat-core/src/error.rs`) is never constructed in production — an error variant never raised means the condition it names is never detected**, which sits directly beside task #4's rename and should be decided there: dead variant, or missing check. | code-reviewer, on their own instrument | Open - `SchemaMismatch` decided with task #4 |
| F-255 | **Merging the fix destroyed the only fixture that could prove the detector works, and the lead did it while the detector was being written.** Task #11 is a check that every path cited in the design documents resolves; task #7 marked the five missing paths *(planned)*. Both were in flight at once, and **main was the only tree where the check could be shown to fail on real content.** The lead merged #7 without noticing that, removing the fixture from under test-engineer mid-task; architect flagged the timing and the merge had already landed. **The general shape is worth more than the incident: fixing the instance destroys the evidence needed to validate the detector, and the two are routinely worked in parallel by different people because they are obviously related.** Nobody was wrong locally — the fix was ready, the check was not, and neither party could see the other's timing. **The repair is not "merge more carefully": it is that a detector proven against a transient property of the tree was never really proven**, which is F-128 again — a guard whose precondition is a property of the fixture, with nothing saying so. A synthetic fixture is the right answer regardless, and it is strictly better here: it gives three arms rather than one, including **the arm that matters most and that main never offered — that the *(planned)* exemption actually exempts.** If only the failing arm is ever proven, the exemption is the untested half, and the exemption is the part someone later widens to silence a red job. | architect, on the timing; lead, recording their own merge | Closed - synthetic fixture, three arms |
| F-256 | **A teammate stopped asking permission and was right to, and the thing that made it visible was a going-idle message.** architect had twice offered to fold work in "if you would rather", waiting on a lead answer that never came, and then told test-engineer directly about the *(planned)* marker with the note that checking first had been over-deferential. Working agreement 5 already says teammates message each other directly on cross-role decisions and escalate only after one exchange fails — so **the deference was against the written agreement, not merely slower than it.** Recorded because the cost was not the delay: it was that **the one time-sensitive fact in that message (main being the only tree where the path check could fail) was queued behind a permission request**, and by the time it moved, the lead had already merged. **A round-trip for permission is not free when the thing being asked about has a clock on it.** | architect, correcting their own posture | Standing principle |
| F-257 | **A shared gate directory did not remove the false negative, it moved it one directory along — and the payload had carried the right answer the whole time.** F-234 gave the completion gate its own `target/.task-gate` so it would stop colliding with the lead's builds. Several agents complete tasks concurrently, all built into that one directory, and **a passing branch still could not close a task while another agent was compiling**: the same LNK1104, the same wrong verdict, one layer further out. code-reviewer hit it twice identically, then proved their tree green in their own worktree — 0 failures across every suite — so the gate was failing a branch it had no disagreement with. **The fix was in the payload from the beginning: it carries `cwd`, which for a teammate is their own worktree.** Gating there removes the contention structurally rather than by choosing a better shared location, reuses a warm cache instead of a cold one, and — the part that actually matters — **tests the tree the teammate worked in rather than a copy of it.** The general form, and the third time this session: **when a guard is wrong about which environment it is judging, moving the environment is not the fix; asking who the guard is for is.** | code-reviewer, reporting a gate that failed a green tree twice; fix by lead | Closed - gate runs in the caller's worktree |
| F-258 | **Two of this session's gate defects were found only because someone forwarded a message they were entitled to ignore.** F-241 surfaced because test-engineer passed on a fail-open notice addressed to nobody in particular; F-257 because code-reviewer reported a gate failure instead of retrying past it, having already proved their own tree green and being free to conclude the tooling was simply broken. **Both had done the work, both were blocked by infrastructure rather than by their branch, and the cheap move in both cases was to shrug.** The register already says a guard that fires and means the wrong thing teaches the wrong lesson (F-233); this is the other half — **the lesson only gets unlearned if whoever receives it reports the message text rather than the conclusion they drew from it.** "The gate is broken" would have been true, useless, and would have hidden both root causes. | lead, from the session's own record | Standing principle |
| F-259 | **The gate said "this is not necessarily your code" and rejected the task anyway — the same defect, a third time, written by the person fixing the first two.** F-233 was a guard firing and meaning the wrong thing; F-257 was the same verdict from a shared directory; this is the LNK1104 branch printing an accurate diagnosis and then returning failure regardless, so the honest message and the dishonest outcome shipped together. It rejected task #6 on a tree code-reviewer had already proved green — 0 failures across every suite — three times. **The reasoning error is specific and worth naming: `LNK1104` means the link never completed, so NO TESTS RAN.** There is no evidence in either direction, and a guard with no evidence must not produce a verdict. The fix is the same one already made for an unreadable payload one rule earlier, and not carrying it across was the mistake: **fail open, loudly.** The cost of the alternative is not a wrong result, it is that **a teammate who has disproved the gate in their own worktree learns to stop believing it**, which is worth more than the check. code-reviewer had reached exactly that point and stopped re-running rather than add to the contention, which was the right call and is how it surfaced. | code-reviewer, blocked three times; lead, recording their own third instance | Closed |
| F-260 | **The gate was its own cause, and the remedy it printed was the mechanism.** Its advice on failure was "re-run `cargo test --workspace` in your worktree" — so every agent closing a task began a full workspace link while others were building, and the contention the gate reported was partly the contention the gate created. Diagnosed by code-reviewer, who then declined to re-run for that reason: *"another pass from me would add to the contention, not resolve it."* **A guard whose failure path instructs the user to do the thing that causes the failure is self-sustaining**, and it will read as a worsening environment rather than as a loop. Now structurally addressed by gating in the caller's own worktree (F-257), where the cache is warm and the build is not shared. | code-reviewer | Closed |
| F-261 | **Editing a live hook in place is not atomic, and an agent fired it mid-write.** The lead rewrote `.claude/hooks/task-completed-gate.sh` with an in-place `open(...).write(...)`; architect's `TaskUpdate` invoked the script during that window and bash parsed a half-written file — `line 142: is: command not found`, `return: can only 'return' from a function`, `syntax error near unexpected token 'fi'`. `bash -n` passed before and after, and the committed file matches the working file exactly, so **nothing was wrong with the script at any point a human would have looked.** The architect noted it changed between two of their own tool calls and declined to touch it, per F-240. **The general form: a file that other processes execute must be replaced, not edited — write to a temp path and rename, so readers see the old version or the new one and never a prefix of the new one.** Exactly the property `write_atomic` exists to give notes (FR-VLT-03, ADR-0008), applied one layer out to the team's own tooling, and not applied there because nobody thought of a hook as a file with concurrent readers. | architect, hitting a transient; lead, recording their own edit | Closed - replace-and-rename for hook edits |
| F-262 | **The gate's worktree isolation only protects agents that actually entered a worktree, and the read-only role is the one that does not.** F-257 has the gate `cd` to the payload's `cwd`. For a teammate who entered their worktree that is the worktree; **for code-reviewer it is the repo root**, because they never called EnterWorktree — they `cd` into `.worktrees/review` inside individual Bash commands, which does not move the session's cwd. So the one role whose work is entirely read-only, and which therefore has least reason to enter a worktree, is gated in the shared integration copy that F-257 exists to route around. **The fix worked and its coverage is a property of how each agent happens to navigate, which is exactly the shape of a guard whose precondition is a property of the fixture (F-128).** Reported by the person it kept failing, who then declined to change team configuration themselves. | code-reviewer, correcting their own earlier diagnosis | Open - a line in the hook or in the reviewer's role notes |
| F-263 | **`LNK1104` is not contention and the retry does not work for the reason the retry was added — it correlates with whether anything links at all.** The lead recorded it (F-242) as antivirus or file-lock contention, and added a retry on that basis. Architect tested the obvious mitigation and reported the correction rather than the confirmation: **`--jobs 1` does not avoid it** — it failed there too, on `serat_app` — **and it reproduced in a worktree with nothing else running**, so it is neither cargo's own parallel link steps nor another build. What it tracks is cold-link versus warm: a cold-link run failed with **7 occurrences**, and the immediately following run, with artefacts cached and nothing to link, passed with **0**. **So the retry succeeds because the second attempt is no longer linking, not because contention cleared** — practically fine, and it means the failure is *deterministic on cold link* rather than flaky, so **the first gate run after any code change is the one that fails.** An external handle on the freshly written `.exe` fits the evidence and is unconfirmed. **Recorded because the lead's original diagnosis was wrong in a way the working fix concealed:** the mitigation succeeded, which is the circumstance under which a wrong explanation survives longest. | architect, testing the lead's mitigation instead of assuming it | Open - cause unconfirmed; F-242's explanation superseded |
