| F-264 | **A `[P0/design]` ruling merged with no `[P0/impl]` task to carry it into the code, and the ruling's own text explains why nobody would ever notice.** Task #14 ruled the echo set bounded by size rather than time and landed in architecture.md §3.2. `crates/serat-vault/src/echo.rs` is untouched: `ECHO_LIFETIME` is still 2 s at line 26, `with_lifetime` still exists, and all three of `record`, `take_echo` and `pending` still `retain` by elapsed time. Task #7 marked those five *(planned)*, so the assertion went red and **main was broken by a merge the lead made without running the merged crate's own tests** — the binary was run and passed, the suite was not. **The test's own comment had predicted the collision and got the direction backwards:** it said it "starts passing with zero missing the moment the lead merges task #7", when the assertion demanded the five and therefore started *failing*. So the author saw the interaction, wrote it down, and still shipped an assertion that could not survive it — **which is the same gap as the register's, one layer in: naming a hazard is not guarding against it.** The repair is the one F-255 already prescribed: the can-it-fail proof belongs on a synthetic fixture, where it is deterministic and covers all three arms including the one the real tree never offered — that the *(planned)* exemption actually exempts. What the live tree keeps is the property that must hold from here on: every cited path resolves or is marked planned, **plus a floor on the citation count**, because "0 missing" and "checked nothing" are the same one-line PASS unless the count is in it (F-27, one level up). | test-engineer's test; collision caused and repaired by the lead | Closed - 17/17, live-tree assertion now a property rather than a snapshot |
| F-266 | **A "zero problems found" assertion can fail in two opposite directions and a count floor only guards one of them.** doclint's live-tree test asserts no unmarked missing paths plus `report.checked > 10`. The floor catches the extraction heuristic quietly stopping — `checked` collapses and the test goes red — and that half was available cheaply only because `Report.checked` already existed, added by its author against F-27's "a check that ran over nothing is not a passing check". **The half it does not catch is the mirror image: a *(planned)*-marker matcher that becomes too permissive.** Everything then reads as planned, `missing` empties, `checked` stays high, and the test passes over a check protecting nothing. **So the two failure modes of the same assertion are "saw nothing" and "forgave everything", and a volume guard is blind to the second by construction** — volume is unchanged when the classifier is wrong rather than absent. The form that closes it asserts the *positive* set too: `missing` empty **and** `planned` exactly the known five. **The general rule: when a guard passes on an empty result, ask what else produces an empty result, and guard the classification as well as the count.** Raised against the lead's repair by the architect, who ran the merge in a scratch branch rather than reasoning about it. | architect, on the lead's fix to test-engineer's test | Open - needs a `planned` field on `Report`; test-engineer's crate |

| F-324 | **The writer's bottleneck was SQL text being re-parsed a million times, and the phase split named the writer but could not name this.** F-322 established the writer as the dominant phase (45–51% of the wall clock) and the task went looking at batch size, transaction boundaries and index maintenance — the tunables `BATCH_SIZE`'s own doc comment nominates. **None of them was it.** A probe inside `commit()` attributed the writer's ~21 s on the 20k vault as: **`apply::apply` 16.0 s**, ANALYZE 2.8 s, `tx.commit` 2.4 s, `sync_note_count` 19 ms, `SELECT count(*)` 10 ms. So the cost was not committing, batching or counting — it was the eight `INSERT` statements themselves. `apply.rs` issued every one through `tx.execute(sql, params)`, and **rusqlite's `execute` prepares the statement afresh on every call**: it sits in per-row loops over blocks, fields, links and tasks, so a 20,000-note rebuild re-parsed the same eight SQL strings roughly a million times. Moving them to `prepare_cached` took `apply` from **16.0 s to 6.6 s** and the whole rebuild, same build and same minute, from 27.1 s to 17.5 s. **The general form: "which phase" and "which line" are different questions, and a phase split answers only the first.** F-10 built the phase split precisely so the writer could be distinguished from the parser, and it did that job — it just cannot see inside the phase it names, and the guesses about what was inside it were all wrong. Ten minutes of probe beat three plausible hypotheses. | lead, task #34 | Closed - `prepare_cached`, statement cache pinned at 32 |
| F-325 | **The opportunistic ANALYZE fired on nearly every batch of a rebuild, because the rule is written for a table that is not growing.** §4.3's rule re-runs `ANALYZE` when a batch has touched more than `ANALYZE_FRACTION` (5%) of `notes`, so that a stream of saves cannot leave the planner with statistics that no longer describe the index. **During a rebuild `notes` starts EMPTY and grows**, so "this batch touched more than 5% of the table" is true of almost every batch: measured at **35 runs per rebuild, 2.8 s**, every one of them superseded by the next and all of them thrown away by the explicit `ANALYZE` the rebuild runs at the end. The writer's only signal that it is inside a rebuild is that the `blocks_fts` triggers are off, so the fix is a `bulk_load` flag set by `BeginBulkLoad` and cleared by `EndBulkLoad` — which leaves the incremental path, the one the rule was written for, exactly as it was. **The saving is the smaller half of the story; the interesting part is what it repaired.** `the_statistics_left_behind_describe_the_finished_index` carried a comment admitting it could not catch deletion of the rebuild's explicit `analyze()`, because at test scale the opportunistic path fired on the last batch and made the explicit call unobservable. **With the opportunistic path off during a bulk load, the explicit call is the only thing that leaves statistics at any scale, and that test now fails when it is deleted — mutation-checked, 0 notes against 400.** A performance fix closed a test gap that a test change could not. | lead, task #34 | Closed - two-sided test, both halves mutation-checked |

That is a real fix for this test and no fix at all for the next one someone writes in `writer.rs`. **The thing worth naming is the shape of the trap: a process-wide test seam is a guard whose blast radius grows with every test added near it, and it fails by making an unrelated, correct test report a defect that does not exist.** Task #28 stays open and now has a reproduction. | lead, on the lead's own new test | Open - task #28, and now reproducible |
| F-327 | **The NFR-PRF-04 number moves ~35% with machine state, which is larger than the breach F-322 recorded — so its FAIL was real as measured and is not reproducible.** The pre-fix binary measured **36,364 ms and 37,596 ms this morning and FAILED**; the same binary, the same vault, the same pinned digest `35fcb867…`, hours later, measures **27,052 ms and PASSES**. Second, **a budget whose measurement noise exceeds its own margin is not yet a gate** — a PASS/FAIL that flips on machine state will eventually flip in CI and be read as a regression someone must bisect. Third, this is what the harness's own "NOT A GATE NUMBER" banner has been saying about the virus scanner all along, generalised: the reference profile controls one source of variance and does not control this one. **F-26 attests the vault. Nothing yet attests the machine.** | lead, comparing the pre-fix binary against itself | Open - the ENVIRONMENT half. Calibration was tried and rejected (F-332); the harness half is done (F-333). The content is not a fragment. That flag would have deleted a completed P0 deliverable silently, and the task list would still have said done. Only checking `git status` in each worktree **before** removing anything found it. **Two general forms.** First: **"the task is complete" and "the artifact is committed" are different facts, and the definition of done conflates them** — it says "artifact in the right docs/ folder", which was true of a file only that worktree could see. Second: **an uncommitted change is invisible to every tool this project trusts.** The register, the branch triage of task #22, `git branch --merged`, the doclint path checks — all read committed state. F-287 established that "is this merged" needs per-content checks rather than branch ancestry; this is the layer below it, where the content is not in git at all. | F-329 | **`git branch -d` means "merged into its upstream", not "merged into main", and on this repo the two disagree in both directions.** A sweep of the 26 local branches whose tips are ancestors of `main` deleted 23 and refused 3, with a message about the branch not being merged **to its own `origin/` counterpart** — which is a different question from the one being asked. The three split two ways, and only measuring told them apart. **`task/echo-set-bounded-by-size` was a false refusal**: local and remote tips are both in `main`, and the local was merely 5 commits *ahead of its upstream* — all 5 in `main`. Fully dead, and git declined to delete it. **`task/parse-pool-and-symlink-invariant` and `task/traceability-and-repo-ready-files` were true refusals** for a reason git did not state: their *remotes* carry 2 and 1 commits that are **not** in `main`, so the branch name still points at live work even though the local ref does not. **This is F-287's problem one layer over.** F-287 established that "is this branch merged" is not answered by `main...branch` or `main branch`; this adds that it is not answered by `git branch -d` either, because that command silently substitutes the upstream for `main` as the reference point. **The safe predicate is the one actually wanted and it is one command**: `git merge-base --is-ancestor <tip> main`, run against *both* the local and the remote tip, since a name can be dead on one side and alive on the other. Every deletion here was gated on that check — verified afterwards on a sample of ten deleted tips, all still reachable from `main` — which is why `-D` was safe on the false refusal and was not used on the two true ones. **The general form: a safety flag that refuses is only useful if you know which question it answered**, and `-d`'s refusal reads as "this branch has unmerged work" when it means "this branch differs from its upstream". Taken at face value it keeps dead branches; overridden at face value it deletes live ones. | lead, sweeping merged branches | Closed - 24 local and 22 remote deleted, 2 kept for live remote work |
| F-330 | **F-328 IS WRONG, and it is wrong in exactly the way F-320 warns about, written one turn after F-320.** F-328 claimed that task #23's deliverable existed only as uncommitted state in the product-analyst worktree, that `main`'s PRD did not define NFR-SEC-06, and that a `--force` cleanup would have destroyed a completed P0 artifact. **Every part of that is false.** The work landed on `main` on 5 Sept as `bcf76ec` — "Allocate NFR-SEC-06, split NFR-CMP-03, clarify NFR-PRF-03, reconcile the appendix" — followed by `16d0db4` correcting a stale traceability label. `main`'s PRD carries four NFR-SEC-06 references and NFR-CMP-04. **The worktree held a stale older copy of edits that had already landed by another route**, which is also why it was never committed there. **Merging the "recovered" branch would have been a pure regression**: it deletes a paragraph `main` has and the branch does not (the rationale for NFR-SEC-06 being P1 rather than P0), reverts `` `.serat/` `` to unformatted text in NFR-CMP-03, and otherwise only churns whitespace. Caught only because the merge request prompted a check of what `main` already contained — the merge would have succeeded silently and quietly undone two commits. **The mechanism of the error: I inferred "main does not have this" from "this is uncommitted here" and wrote the inference as a measured fact.** One `git grep NFR-SEC-06 main -- docs/02-requirements/prd.md` was all it needed, and I did not run it before writing a register row, a task and a commit message on the strength of it. **That is F-320's lesson — a count derived by hand is not an independent oracle — applied to a claim about repository state instead of a link count, by the same person who had just recorded it.** F-287's shape too: content that landed by a different route is invisible to the check you happen to run. **The durable rule: "X is missing from main" is a claim about main, so it must be measured against main, never deduced from where else X happens to be sitting.** | lead, correcting the lead, prompted by a merge request | Closed - F-328 retracted, task #37 void, branch deleted (local and origin); this row is the evidence, the branch was not |
| F-331 | **F-323 fixed: every argument is now recognised or refused, and `--help` no longer costs 37 seconds.** `parse_args` scanned for `--profile` and `--iterations` by exact string with `position()` and **discarded everything else without comment**. Four shapes it accepted and silently mishandled, each of which produced a real measurement against settings nobody asked for: `--help` ran the full rebuild; **`--profile=ci` is not the string `--profile`**, so it was dropped and the run measured against the reference threshold while the operator had asked for ci; a mistyped `--iteration 1` ran the default count; and a repeated flag silently took the first. Replaced with a sequential scan that accepts both `--flag value` and `--flag=value`, refuses an unknown argument by name with the usage text, refuses a flag given twice rather than picking a winner, and treats `--help`/`-h` as a flag that measures nothing. **The subtlety worth recording is why this was hard to see: the reports were never wrong.** A run given `--profile=ci` printed "(reference profile)" accurately, because it genuinely had used the reference profile. Nothing in the output was false; the only thing missing was the operator's intent, and **a report cannot show the difference between a setting you did not ask for and one you asked for and did not get.** That is why the fix belongs at the parser rather than in a louder report. Refusals print as `budget: ...` and exit 1 rather than as a `FAIL` of the requirement, because nothing was measured and a usage error must not leave a breach in the log that never happened. Verified against `.github/workflows/budgets.yml`: CI passes `--profile ci`, the space form, which the test asserts explicitly. | F-332 | **The obvious fix for F-327 was built, measured and REJECTED: a calibration yardstick is noisier than the drift it would detect.** The plan was the one env.rs already implies — fixed work, a pinned reference, and refuse to call a number a gate number when the machine is off its reference speed, exactly as it already refuses when the virus scanner is off. **Before pinning anything it was tested, and it does not survive this machine.** A fixed CPU round (blake3 over a fixed buffer) and a fixed storage round (file-backed SQLite in the index's own WAL/NORMAL configuration, plus 8,000 small file writes and reads) were measured repeatedly. At ~35 ms rounds two back-to-back invocations disagreed by **41%** — the noise was the measurement. Lengthened to 1 s, then to 3–5 s, the spread did **not** settle: `cpu_spread` 14–37%, `sqlite_spread` 15–44%, `io_spread` 25–**108%**, with the between-invocation minimum still moving 11–16%. **A yardstick that varies by 108% cannot certify a 35% drift; it would pass and fail runs at random.** Two further things were learned by measuring rather than assuming. **The first version of the storage round used `open_in_memory`, so it never touched a disk** — a calibration blind to the axis the rebuild spends most of its time on, and it looked fine until the flaw was noticed rather than detected. And an induced-load trial (8 busy loops on 16 cores) moved **neither** the yardstick nor NFR-PRF-04 — 24,363 ms quiet against 23,792 ms loaded — which is a failed experiment rather than a result, but it does rule out moderate CPU contention as the cause. **The drift is the storage substrate, and nothing inside one process can attest a machine that unsteady.** Recorded as a negative result with its numbers, and the experiment binary deleted rather than left in the harness gating nothing. | lead, task #38 | Closed - calibration rejected on evidence |
| F-333 | **What a run CAN say honestly is whether its own samples agreed, and until now it did not ask.** `passed()` compared the **median** against the limit, so three samples of 28.0 s, 29.0 s and 31.0 s reported `PASS` against a 30 s budget — a run that **watched the budget break** and went green, because the middle sample did not. The verdict is now three-valued: every sample under the limit is `PASS`, every sample over it is `FAIL`, and **samples that straddle the limit are `INDETERMINATE`** and exit non-zero, because that run observed both outcomes and reporting either as the answer is a coin toss dressed as a measurement. **The rule has no tunable constant in it**, which is what makes it defensible where a spread threshold would have been an argument: it does not ask how noisy is too noisy, only whether this run's own evidence points one way. Every result also now prints `spread`, the disagreement between the fastest and slowest sample, because it is the only in-run evidence of how steady the machine was and F-327 is about a number that was tight, confident and nine seconds wrong. **Bounded honestly: this does not fix F-327 and is not claimed to.** A run can now refuse to certify what it did not settle; it still cannot know that the same code measured 35% faster yesterday. The drift is a property of the measurement environment, and the remedies are outside the harness — a storage substrate steady enough to answer a 30 s question, or a budget whose tolerance is set from measured variance rather than from a round number. Both are decisions for test-engineer and the charter, not code. | lead, task #38 | Closed - the harness half; F-327 stays open for the environment half |

The failure is silent at the moment it happens and loud much later, which is this register's most repeated shape. Neither was credited anywhere in the threat model. **Reconciled with an independent read: tauri-app-dev answered #43 in parallel and labelled the finding CONFIRMED, but its own citation of `has_app_acl_manifest` — true when `build.rs` declares an `AppManifest` or ships `permissions/` — IS the opt-in path, so its evidence supports the refutation its label did not draw.** Every mechanical fact agreed across both reads; only the conclusion differed, which is the more useful kind of disagreement to have had. | architect, reading the source #43 asked for; reconciled against tauri-app-dev's parallel read | Closed - merged at 7900814 |

| F-343 | **A blocking objection the author could have answered themselves was left blocking for several cycles.** ADR-0012's Objections section named one question capable of changing the decision rather than its wording — is the `generate_handler!` command list reachable from a test at all, since if not the ADR's own condition is unsatisfiable and "do nothing" becomes the honest ruling — addressed it to the teammate who owns the crate, and then **waited**, through several idle cycles, while that teammate was at a session limit. **Consulting first was correct; waiting was not.** The question was answerable in about ten minutes by building a standalone crate and running it: `generate_handler!` is a proc macro over function *paths*, so the list is not available as data, **but the file can read itself** — a test may `include_str!` its own source and extract the block textually, which is `security_config.rs`'s existing principle (the bytes under test are the bytes that ship) applied one file over. Self-reported by the author who did it, which is the only way this class surfaces at all: from outside, a question correctly addressed to the right owner and a question being used to avoid work are indistinguishable. | architect, on their own #47 | Closed - objection answered, ADR stands, #48 raised |

| F-348 | **Merging an implementation before its document update leaves a window in which the document is wrong, and for a security document the wrong direction is the permissive one.** #48 landed the app ACL manifest at `0c2a980`; the threat model still said "a local webview is not gated, because Serat has not opted in" and "`open_vault` is reachable from the webview today", and ADR-0012 still read `proposed` while its implementation shipped. For four commits `main` **over-claimed an exposure that had been closed** — the mirror of #46, which under-claimed a control that existed, and the more alarming of the two: a reader trusting it would go and re-close something already shut, or worse, conclude the surface is hopeless and stop. **The window was not a mistake in the ordinary sense**, which is what makes it worth a row: the document update did not exist when the implementation was merged, so no sequencing decision at merge time could have avoided it. It is structural. Implementation and the prose describing it are produced by different roles, arrive as separate branches, and are merged in the order they arrive. **The mitigation is not "merge more carefully" but "make them one unit"** — a change that alters what a security document asserts should carry the assertion with it, or wait. Note what did *not* catch this: `doclint` passed throughout, because every cited path still resolved. **A path checker verifies that the document points at things that exist, never that it says true things about them**, which is the same boundary F-346 crossed from the other side. | F-350 | **doclint's third check closes the gap the first two were structurally incapable of seeing, and it was mutation-verified against both shapes rather than watched passing.** F-239's path check asks whether a document points at things that exist; F-303's join check asks whether its IDs agree with the PRD. **Neither can ask whether the document renders**, which is how task #49's STRIDE table spent six passes broken in plain sight while doclint stayed green and was right to. `check-table-structure` adds two rules — a row's unescaped pipe count must match its header's, and no pipe-prefixed line may sit outside a table — and is wired as a third CI job. **Verified by reintroducing both real bugs, not by observing a pass** (F-349, one finding earlier, on exactly this): unterminating T-07's row is caught as `column count 6, header has 7`, and putting a bare `\|` back into an F-342 prose cell is caught as `column count 6, header has 5`, each with the file and line. A guard whose first real run is clean has never been seen to fire on live data, which is why the mutations were the evidence and the clean run was not. One judgement call, invited and taken: an ATX heading immediately after a table is **not** a violation, because a heading is an unambiguous block boundary to every Markdown parser regardless of blank lines — TC-107 pins it. | test-engineer, task #51; mutations re-run by lead | Closed - merged, 38 files, 0 problems |

Not a cold link, not a defect in the branch under test, and not something any number of retries fixes while the collection is still running. **The general form: an error identified by its message is identified by its symptom, and a symptom that has had one cause so far is not a symptom with one cause.** F-263's advice was never wrong; it was incomplete, and the incompleteness was invisible while only one cause had ever occurred. **The discriminator is cheap and should be the first move, not the third: read which targets failed.** Cold-link LNK1104 hits whatever happens to be linking and moves around between runs; lock-contention LNK1104 hits exactly the set of binaries something else is executing, and that set is stable across retries and legible as a group. A secondary consequence worth naming: `cargo test --workspace` is not runnable at all while a background job is executing workspace binaries, so a merge verified during one must either scope to the crates it touches (`-p serat-index`, done here, 75 tests) or wait. | F-368 | **Prose decays against a moving tree faster than code does, and nothing in the toolchain notices.** Three branches landed in one sitting — #66 and #63 from architect, #61's removal from rust-core-dev — and **two of the three carried a claim main had already falsified.** #63 stated that the walk's symlink skip "has no test, on any platform" and that deleting it leaves serat-vault green at 64 passed, with "task #62 adds one" in the future tense; #62 had landed hours earlier, `walk.rs:355` and `:396` hold two mutation-verified ungated tests, and the crate passes 66. #66's closing line routed the code change onto `task/i6-open-or-rebuild`, a branch deleted when I6 landed. **Neither was careless and neither would have been caught by any gate we run:** doclint checks that cited paths resolve, that the PRD join holds and that tables render — all three passed on both branches, correctly, because every one of those checks asks about structure and none asks whether a sentence is still true. **The asymmetry is the finding. A code branch that goes stale usually stops compiling: the signature moved, the variant was renamed, the call site gained a parameter — the tree pushes back.** I6 demonstrated exactly that, breaking on `SchemaVersion`, on `rebuild()`'s new argument and on a call site, and each break was a free notification that the branch had drifted. **A prose branch never stops compiling.** It merges clean, renders clean, and lands a false statement in the document whose whole job is to be true about the tree. So the staleness window for documentation is not just longer than for code, it is *unbounded* — and it is worst precisely where the parallelism is highest, because the document is describing work that other agents are finishing while it is being written. **What follows practically: a design branch's factual claims must be re-checked against main at merge time rather than at authoring time, and the check is manual because no linter can do it.** The claims worth re-reading are the ones with a truth value about the tree — test counts, "nothing asserts this", "task #N will", branch names, present-tense statements about what exists. Both corrections here kept the argument and moved only the tense, and #63's was expanded rather than trimmed: F-364's near-miss (the first directory test passed with the guard deleted, because it asserted on the file *behind* the link) is worth more to a future reader than the closure notice. **A retired finding should be rewritten in the past tense, never deleted — how a gap was missed outlives the gap.** | lead, merging three design branches after I6 and #62 had moved main under them | Closed - all three landed corrected; `41b8f00` and `56c8dc4` |
| F-369 | **The gate ran for the first time and immediately caught a real defect — a cross-process race on the shared 20k vault path, which is task #28's bug one level up.** `ci.yml` produced jobs for the first time in the repository's history (F-365), and on run 34044208361 `cargo test --workspace (windows-latest)` failed while ubuntu, clippy, fmt, location-independence and all nine crate-matrix jobs went green. One test: `a_job_cannot_swallow_the_seam_error_and_post_a_number`, refusing `"generating the vault: bench/vaults/vault-20k is not empty and has no .vaultgen stamp; refusing to delete it"` where it expected the I4 seam error. **The assertion behaved correctly** — it got an `Err`, checked *which* one, and refused the wrong one; a looser test would have passed here and hidden this. **The mechanism, read off the code rather than guessed:** `decide()` calls `ensure_vault` *before* the measurement closure, so a unit test of error propagation that passes a closure returning `Err` still builds a 20,000-note vault first; `ensure_vault` regenerates whenever the `.vaultgen` stamp is absent; and vaultgen refuses a non-empty stampless directory. Those three combine only if the directory is caught **mid-generation** — the stamp is written last, so between the first note and the stamp there is a window seconds wide in which the directory is exactly non-empty and stampless — and `cargo test --workspace` runs test binaries as **parallel processes**, several of which reach `ensure_vault` for the same preset. **Same shape as F-3xx's FAIL_NEXT_COMMIT race and the same remedy standard: shared mutable global state plus concurrency, passing in isolation, failing under load — fixed properly only by making the collision structurally impossible, not rarer.** The state here is a fixed filesystem path rather than a static, and the concurrency is between processes rather than threads, which is why no amount of per-test care inside one binary would have found it. **Two things worth keeping past the fix.** First, the platform split is not "Windows is flaky": it is a timing window, and two runners scheduling I/O differently is exactly what decides a TOCTOU. Reaching for the flaky-platform explanation would have closed the investigation one step before the bug. Second, **this is the argument for the gate existing.** The race predates the run that found it and would have kept sitting there; nothing local surfaces it, because locally `cargo test --workspace` has been unrunnable for an unrelated reason (F-367). A gate whose first act is to fail is doing its job, and the risk now is the ordinary one — that a red check nobody has explained becomes a red check everybody ignores, which is F-354's end state reached by a different road. | lead, diagnosing the first failure ci.yml has ever been able to report; architect declined to diagnose it inside a security document, correctly | Closed - task #70 (`f34edb7`) landed `VaultGenerationLock`, an advisory cross-process lock over `ensure_vault`'s check-then-generate window, with a mutation-verified exclusion test (`the_vault_generation_lock_excludes_a_second_holder_and_releases_on_drop`). Re-run clean by test-engineer at task #93 (`cargo test -p budget --lib`, 57/57). What is NOT closed: an actual repeat of the original CI race under `cargo test --workspace (windows-latest)`, because ci.yml/budgets.yml cannot currently be scheduled at all (F-384) — the diagnosis and the structural fix are sound, but the specific run that would prove the fix against the exact failure mode has not happened |
| F-370 | **The Windows gate failure is intermittent, which is evidence rather than annoyance: it discriminates between the two candidate causes and only one survives.** F-369 diagnosed `cargo test --workspace (windows-latest)` as a cross-process race on `bench/vaults/vault-20k` and named two ways to kill the hypothesis — leftover runner state, or a path-resolution difference. **Both are deterministic, and the run history rules them out.** Run 34044208361 (commit `c2ae8f0`) failed on `a_job_cannot_swallow_the_seam_error_and_post_a_number`; run 34044835359 (commit `2cbdb4e`, the next commit, and one that touches only a doc comment in `tests/analyze.rs`) **passed end to end, windows-latest included.** A stale directory on the runner image or a wrong path would fail every run alike; a fixture directory caught mid-generation fails only when the interleaving lands wrong. **So the intermittency is the discriminator, and it was available for free in the run list without reproducing anything.** Two things follow that are worth more than the diagnosis. First, **a green run after a red one is not evidence the bug is gone**, and the temptation to read it that way is exactly how a race becomes permanent: whoever sees green next will be tempted to close #70, and the fix has to be structural — the standard task #28 met, where no other thread could hold the reference at all — rather than a rerun. Second, **the gate is now the only instrument that can see this at all.** `cargo test --workspace` does not run locally here (F-367, lock contention from #40's collection), the crate-matrix jobs pass because each runs one crate in its own process and never contends, and the ubuntu workspace job passes because its interleaving happens not to lose. **A defect visible only in one job of one workflow, intermittently, is precisely the class that a gate nobody trusts will bury** — which is why F-369 argued the red check must be explained rather than tolerated, and why a rerun-until-green habit would cost more here than the original bug. | lead, checking architect's "it has passed once" claim and finding it settled #70's open question | Closed - strengthened task #70, which is fixed (`f34edb7`); the residual risk this finding warned about — "a green run after a red one is not evidence the bug is gone" — is why test-engineer did not close this on the fix commit's say-so alone, and instead re-ran the exclusion test independently at task #93 before marking it closed |
| F-371 | **Task #13 is correct, well-tested, mutation-verified — and breaches NFR-PRF-04 by more than fortyfold, because the fix landed on a path shared with the rebuild.** `apply::upsert_note` and `upsert_asset` now call `resolve_inbound`/`resolve_outbound` after re-inserting, which is exactly right for the incremental save this task is about (FR-NAV-04, F-244). But `apply::apply` is the **same** code the rebuild drives, so a 20,000-note rebuild now runs ~40,000 extra `UPDATE`s, and each carries a correlated subquery whose `WHERE` is a four-way `OR` over `n.path`, the same with `.md` appended, and two `instr()`-guarded `file_name` comparisons — a shape no index can serve, so each one degenerates toward a scan of `notes`. Measured, not argued: `prf04-full-rebuild --iterations 1` on the standing 20k fixture consumed **1,269 seconds of CPU without producing a result**, against a **30s** budget and the ~21-27s the same job measured after F-324. **That is a lower bound rather than a number** — the process was still burning CPU when it was killed, and a fraction of it is the fixture's tree-digest verification, which was nonetheless inside the total when NFR-PRF-04 last passed. **The mechanism is generic and worth naming past this instance: a per-row cost added to a path that a bulk operation shares is invisible in every test that exercises one row.** serat-index's suite went from 76 to 77 green, the new test is mutation-verified, and nothing in it runs more than a handful of notes — the suite could not have caught this, and did not. Only the budget job sees it, which is what C-06 and the working agreement's "a PR that adds an index read path, a parse step or a query shape adds or updates the benchmark that measures it" are for. **The fix is not to undo the task.** The writer already knows `bulk_load` (task #34), and `rebuild_with_queue_depth` still runs `writer::resolve_links()` — one full pass — at the end, which the author kept deliberately and which makes the per-row work during a rebuild **redundant rather than merely expensive**: their own comment says the safety-net pass "should ordinarily have nothing left to do." Skipping the scoped resolves while `bulk_load` is set keeps FR-NAV-04 exactly where it was fixed and returns NFR-PRF-04 to its pre-#13 cost. **Second-order, and the reason this was nearly missed:** the author could not run `cargo test --workspace` at all (F-367's lock contention) and reported `cargo check --workspace` instead, honestly and with the reason named — but the budget job is a separate binary that neither command runs, so the gap was not in their diligence, it was in what any of the available commands could have told them. | lead, running the budget before merging because the change touched a shared write path | Open - #13 must not merge as-is; returned to rust-core-dev |
| F-372 | **The rebuild budget builds its scratch index INSIDE the shared fixture vault, so any interrupted run silently poisons that vault for every later test — and I did it, twenty minutes after test-engineer independently hit the same shape and fixed it for their own code.** `bench/budget/src/lib.rs:1526` uses `vault.path.join(format!(".serat-bench-{i}"))` and `:1606` uses `vault.path.join(".serat-bench")`. A completed run cleans up; a killed one does not. Killing the `prf04` process that produced F-371's number left a 51MB `.serat-bench-0/` holding `index.db`, `-wal` and `-shm` inside `bench/vaults/vault-20k`, which **moved the vault's tree digest** — so the next thing to call `ensure_vault("20k")` failed its canonicality gate with a manifest mismatch and a "regenerate it" instruction. **Nothing about that message points at the cause.** A reader is told the vault is wrong and told to spend minutes regenerating 20,000 notes; the actual repair was `rm -rf` on one stray directory, after which the digest matched again with no regeneration at all. **The convergence is the finding.** test-engineer hit this class first, from the other direction — their #68 probe originally put its scratch index inside `vault.path`, a panic before cleanup left the shared vault failing its own gate, and they moved to `std::env::temp_dir()` and wrote the reason into the test (`subject.rs:884`). So the correct pattern is already in the tree, written down, one file away from two call sites that still do the wrong thing. **Two independent people walked into the same trap within the hour because the harness's own oldest code models the behaviour its newest code warns against.** The general form is worth more than the fix: **a fixture whose canonicality is checked by digest cannot also be a scratch directory, and any tool that writes into one it does not own converts its own crash into everyone else's failure.** This is also a second, cheaper instance of what task #70 tracks — there the shared path is contended between concurrent processes, here it is contended between a process and its own crash — and both trace to the same root, a fixed shared path used as working space. Fixing #70 without moving these two call sites out of the vault leaves half the hazard standing. | lead, having corrupted the shared 20k vault while measuring F-371 and then hit the failure from the other side while verifying #68 | Open - task #78 |
| F-373 | **#13's second measurement: the `bulk_load` gate works, a residual of ~11.5s remains, and the DDL had predicted that residual in writing before the branch existed.** A controlled A/B minutes apart on the same loaded machine — main at `f34edb7` versus the same tree plus #13's two commits — gives main **49,809ms** with `producer waited` 517ms (1%), and #13 **61,335ms** with `producer waited` 3,265ms (5%). Parse is 30,021 against 30,430ms, i.e. unchanged, so the difference is not the parser; the delta is the writer, and it is **+11.5 seconds on a 20k rebuild**. **The gate itself is correct and did what it was for:** the per-row `resolve_inbound`/`resolve_outbound` calls are skipped during bulk load, which is why the rebuild now finishes at all rather than burning 1,269 CPU-seconds without returning (F-371). What the gate cannot reach is the **other** cost #13 introduces: `ix_links_unresolved` is a real index, and every one of ~220,367 link inserts maintains it. No `bulk_load` check inside `apply` can skip that, because it is SQLite's own work on `INSERT` rather than a statement anybody issues. **And `schema/001_init.sql:268` had already said so, in the P0 tree, before any of this: _"It is not here because P0 does not re-resolve links, and an index P0 never reads would still cost P0's rebuild benchmark (NFR-PRF-04) to build."_** #13 added the index and replaced that sentence with one announcing that P1 adds it — **deleting the prediction in the same commit that fulfilled it.** The lesson generalises past this branch and is not about care: a comment explaining *why something is absent* is load-bearing in a way that a comment explaining what is present is not, because the only person who ever reads it is the person about to remove it, and they are removing it precisely because they have decided the absence should end. **The remedy already exists in this codebase as a pattern.** `begin_bulk_load`/`end_bulk_load` drop and recreate the `blocks_fts` triggers around a rebuild for exactly this reason; `ix_links_unresolved` belongs in the same bracket, created after the load rather than maintained through it. **Separately and not #13's doing: main itself measured 49,809ms against a 30,000ms budget in this same run.** That is a breach on an unattested machine — other agents were compiling throughout, and the harness's own banner says a scanner-off run is a best case rather than a gate number — so it is a flag for #40's variance work and NOT a number to record. The A/B *delta* survives the load because both halves ran under it; the absolute does not. | lead, measuring #13's resubmission rather than accepting `cargo test --workspace` as evidence about a budget | Open - #13 held again; the index, not the resolve calls |
| F-374 | **A sweep done by the right method missed three sites because the string was spelled with a space, and one of the survivors was a generated file whose source nobody had swept.** #68 replaced the pre-I6 open-check figure and swept for it with `grep -n "2.4ms"` — the correct instinct, and #71's lesson applied without being told. Three sites survived, because they write it **`2.4 ms`**: `contracts/ipc.rs`, `docs/05-testing/budget-harness.md`, and the ts-rs bindings generated from that Rust doc comment. **The method was right and whitespace defeated it**, which is a narrower and more useful lesson than "sweep harder": a figure is not a token, and a grep for one spelling of it is a grep for one spelling of it. Searching for the bare number and reading the hits costs seconds more and does not have this failure mode. **The generated-file half is the part worth carrying.** `contracts/ipc.rs:134` is the only writable site; `contracts/bindings/`, `crates/serat-app/bindings/` and `app/src/ipc/generated/` are ts-rs output, two of them gitignored and one committed with CI failing on a diff. | F-376 | **A task assigned to the right person went unstarted through many idle reports because its subject line still carried another role's tag, and the assignee was filtering on the tag rather than the owner field — correctly.** `#64`'s owner was set to rust-core-dev and its status to in_progress, while its subject read `[P0/review]`. The task list renders subjects; the owner is one lookup further in. So the visible signal said code-reviewer and the authoritative one said rust-core-dev, and the assignee trusted what they could see — which is what they had told me they were doing, in writing, several messages earlier: *"remaining pending items #64/#70/#72 are review/test-engineer/architect work."* **They were reading the field the system puts in front of them and I was writing to a field it does not.** Compounded by my own earlier failure on the same task: two messages before that I had written "if code-reviewer stays silent I will hand it to you" and "say which you want", which are invitations, and a teammate declining to claim work owned by another role is correct behaviour rather than passivity. **So the same assignment failed twice for two different reasons, neither of them the assignee's, and the visible symptom both times was an idle teammate reporting nothing to do while real P0 work sat pending.** The general form is the session's recurring shape in a new place: **a record with two fields that can disagree will be read by whichever one is cheapest to see, so the cheap field is the one that has to be true.** F-368 is prose decaying against a moving tree; F-374 is one fact with a canonical home and derived copies; this is one fact with two representations of equal authority and unequal visibility. The fix was to put the owner in the subject — `[P0/impl] RUST-CORE-DEV — ...` — which is redundant with the owner field and exactly for that reason works. **Worth generalising past task lists: any convention where a routing tag and an assignment field can drift will drift, and the reader is not at fault for believing the label.** | lead, after seventeen idle reports that were the correct response to instructions I wrote | Closed - `#64` retitled and started; no other pending task had the mismatch |

| F-382 | **An empty handle slot is indistinguishable from a transient race, and #94 measures that exact path — F-19 one level up, flattering the number in the same direction.** #90's `IndexHandleSlot` is polled rather than waited on, which is correct: `open_vault` must return before the open check finishes, so there is a real window where a save lands before a handle exists, and degrading to the watcher path there is a supported outcome. **But the slot is empty in three situations and they look identical from the call site** — that legitimate startup window; an opener whose `into_handle` was never overridden, which the trait default makes silent by construction (it returns `None`); and an open that failed. In all three `write_file` returns `Ok` quickly having indexed nothing. The remedy is the same shape as F-19's: the degraded path must be **observable** — a counter or trace field distinguishing saves submitted through the handle from saves that found the slot empty — and **#94 must fail when the path degraded rather than reporting whatever it measured.** tauri-app-dev's `rusqlite` dev-dependency already sets up the stronger check, reading `.serat/index.db` with something that is not the crate's own reader. **Second, smaller, explicitly non-blocking:** `IndexHandle` is not `Clone`, so the slot's mutex is held across a file read and a full parse on every save — not a §2.2 breach, since `apply` uses `submit` rather than `submit_blocking` and no barrier appears, but it is lock contention on the save path in a design whose stated claim is that it replaced locking with ownership. | F-386 | **An identity over contiguous intervals constrains data integrity and says nothing about semantics — so ADR-0014's sum identity cannot catch F-377's mutation B, and structurally never could.** The lead proposed that #106's sum identity might close part of #105 for free, on the reasoning that hoisting `mark_first_paint` to script start moves the mark earlier *"without the elapsed time going anywhere"*. **That reasoning is wrong and the correction is the finding.** The time does go somewhere: into the adjacent term. `Startup::vault_usable()` is `painted.max(rendered).max(index).max(watcher)` (`startup.rs:189`), and §3.4 measures `painted → usable` at 4.7–7.5 ms — **so `painted` is not the mark that settles the maximum.** Move it earlier by Δ and `t_usable` does not move at all: `S_shell` shrinks by Δ, `S_settle` grows by exactly Δ, and every sum is invariant. **The general form, which outlives this case:** an identity over contiguous intervals catches a *missing* mark, a clock step, a mark never recorded — and is blind by construction to an **interior boundary moving**, because redistribution between two adjacent terms is exactly what it cannot see. **Consequence for #105:** it does not get simpler, and the post-composite evidence is doing work nothing else in the tree does. **What the partition does buy, stated so it is not oversold: a diagnostic, not a rejection.** Under the mutation `S_settle` goes from 4.7–7.5 ms to roughly Δ — bounded by F-86's ~100 ms — a 13–21× anomaly in the smallest and most stable term in the table, in a quantity the four-mark harness cannot compute at all. **It must not be made a rejection by bounding `S_settle`:** that term grows legitimately in P1 with a real file tree, and on Linux with one inotify watch per directory. A large `S_settle` means the paint fired well before the other three preconditions — suspicious, not provably wrong. | architect, settling a question the lead raised; the lead's reasoning was the thing corrected | Closed — recorded in ADR-0014 §Consequences and §3.4; #105 proceeds on the evidence mechanism |
| F-387 | **doclint resolves the repository root at *compile* time, so a binary built from a copy of the repo silently lints the copy — and the dangerous case reports PASS about a tree nobody is editing.** `bench/doclint/src/lib.rs:340`'s `repo_root()` is `PathBuf::from(env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR"))` walked up two levels. `env!` is expanded when the crate is compiled, not when the binary runs, so the path is **baked into the executable**. Because `cargo run` reuses a cached binary whenever the source is unchanged, running doclint from the repository root can execute an artefact compiled somewhere else entirely. Observed: the lead ran all three jobs from `D:\Projects\gaws\serat` with `pwd` confirming that directory, and got `walking docs/03-design: The system cannot find the path specified` naming a **scratchpad copy** (`scratchpad/rev90/...`) that code-reviewer had built in while reviewing #90 and since deleted. `cargo clean -p doclint` and a rebuild gave PASS on all three. **The failure that was observed is the benign one.** Had the scratch copy still existed, doclint would have scanned *it* — reporting PASS over a stale snapshot while the working tree went unchecked, with nothing in the output naming which tree it read. That is this project's recurring shape: **the instrument flatters the number, and the flattery is invisible because the tool reports success rather than silence.** It also means every prior "doclint green" in this wave was a statement about whichever tree that teammate's binary was compiled in — true for teammates building in their own worktrees, and unverifiable after the fact. **Bounded, and the bound is the warning.** architect had reported "doclint green" on roughly a dozen messages this session and checked rather than assumed: `cargo clean -p doclint` (615 files, 103.9 MiB) then a forced rebuild gave identical results — 94 citations, 40 files, 87 Musts, all PASS — so those greens were about the tree being edited. **What saved it is incidental rather than designed: each worktree has its own `target/`, so `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR` bakes in that worktree.** Anyone who sets a shared `target-dir` to save disk space re-creates the defect across all five worktrees at once, and it would then report PASS about whichever worktree compiled last. Fix: resolve the root at run time (walk up from the current directory to the workspace marker, or take it as an argument), and print the root that was scanned in every report, so a reader can see which tree a PASS is about. | lead, found by a failing run and confirmed by rebuild; scope bounded by architect | Open — no task issued; the fix belongs with whoever next touches `bench/doclint` |
| F-388 | **The proposed fix for F-377's mutation B would have reintroduced mutation B's effect, legitimately and undetectably — caught in consult before it was built.** tauri-app-dev proposed replacing `boot.js`'s double `requestAnimationFrame` with a gate on the browser's `first-contentful-paint` entry, reasoning that a mark which *cannot* fire before a real paint makes the mutations structurally inapplicable rather than merely detected. **That reasoning is right — removing a failure mode beats detecting it — and it has to remove the right one.** `first-contentful-paint` attests that *something* painted, not that **our shell** painted. | F-399 | **The lead merged #105 without waiting for its review, and the BLOCKING finding arrived afterwards — so the wave's own rule that nothing merges with a BLOCKING was broken by the person who wrote it.** #90 and #97 had explicit approvals and #98/#108 had a conditional one; **#105 had none.** It was merged on its author's own report that the mutations went red, which was true and was not the question — the question is whether anything else in the tree makes the guard reachable without the evidence, and that is what a reviewer is for. code-reviewer's call-site mutation (F-397) was sent to the author directly and reached the lead only inside a later report, by which time #105 was on `main`. **The sequencing was an accident; merging without an approval was a decision, and it was the lead's.** What it cost is small and specific: nothing on `main` is wrong, every cold-start figure taken against it is sound, and **the guard the entire F-377 thread exists to produce is deletable in one line with nothing going red** — while #100 is about to take the number that guard protects. Recorded because a rule the lead enforces on teammates and suspends for themselves is not a rule, and because the gate report must not describe #105 as reviewed. | lead, on code-reviewer's report | Open — closes with #113 |
| F-400 | **The merged build never paints — reproduced twice, the first time against a binary predating the merge, so a stale artefact was ruled out before the cause was diagnosed: `prf01-cold-start` times out at 60 s with "the webview never reported a first painted frame", on a freshly built binary — and the cause is F-396(c) firing as a hard failure, by way of my own F-395 ruling.** `app/src/startup.ts`'s `readFirstContentfulPaintMs()` reads `performance.getEntriesByType("paint")` **once, at the moment the second `requestAnimationFrame` fires**, and returns `number \| null`. The `first-contentful-paint` entry is recorded asynchronously and is **not guaranteed to exist at that instant**, so the read races it. On a miss the frontend sends `null` into a command parameter typed `f64` — not `Option` — the call fails to deserialise before any Serat code runs, `schedulePaintMark`'s handler turns it into a status string, **no mark is ever taken, no trace is written, and the harness waits out its full timeout.** F-393 measured the entry landing at 184 ms with the window left open and read *after the fact* over the DevTools Protocol; that measured **that the entry eventually exists**, not that it exists at second-rAF time, and nothing had tested the latter. **The regression entered through F-395, which is mine.** #105's original mechanism gated on `PerformanceObserver({type:"paint"})` — it *waited* for the entry rather than sampling for it. I ruled that half deleted because it lived in `crates/serat-app/webview/boot.js`, which is #98's Vite build output and would be erased by the next `npm run build`. **That ruling was right about the location and wrong about the consequence: nobody moved the observer into `app/src/`, and `grep -rn PerformanceObserver app/src/` now returns nothing.** The waiting mechanism was deleted and the sampling one kept. **The fix is to wait rather than sample** — observe the paint entry and mark when it arrives — and #112's `Option<f64>` remains necessary but is not sufficient, since it converts a silent deserialisation failure into a typed rejection while still producing no mark. | the **original** test-engineer instance reproduced the failure twice on a fresh binary; lead diagnosed the cause. **Attribution corrected:** first recorded as test-engineer-2's, which was wrong — that instance was spawned as a replacement and had not yet run anything | **Closed with the cause named, not "did not reproduce".** The never-paints attempts ran against `522e94c` and later; #100's figure was taken at `5b36c31`, which additionally contains **`878730b` (#112)** — whose commit message names this exact mechanism and replaces the synchronous read with a `PerformanceObserver`. **20 of 20 runs carried paint evidence.** Ancestry measured by code-reviewer; the causal attribution reasoned and marked as such. **So #100's DONE is not in tension with this finding — the fix landed before anyone connected the two**, which is the second time this wave a real-WebView2-only defect was invisible to a green unit suite. |
| F-401 | **A cold-start run poisons the canonical benchmark vault, because the application creates `.serat/` inside it and nothing cleans up.** `cold_start_once` spawns `serat.exe` against the shared canonical vault; the real app creates its index directory there as an ordinary side effect, and the vault's digest then fails `the_canonical_vault_passes_the_gate` for **every other consumer** — the same class as F-372, in the opposite direction. Found and cleared by hand (stray `.serat/` removed, gate passing again) while diagnosing F-400. **The structural fix has a constraint worth recording: cleanup must happen after a `cold_start` batch, never between its iterations**, because the warm-index case NFR-PRF-01 actually specifies needs the index to persist across a batch's own runs. | the **original** test-engineer instance, hit while running #100 — not test-engineer-2, whose first act was to correct this misattribution | Closed — two layers: `CleanIndexOnDrop` (`abfb3d8`, harness-side cleanup) and `tree_digest()` excluding a root-level `.serat/` from the walk entirely (`bdb4ad3`) — the second landed after the vault was poisoned a **third** time, by a different process, while the first fix sat uncommitted-untested; the digest exclusion protects every caller, not just the harness's own |
| F-402 | **Ninth guard-that-cannot-fail, and it is inside #111 — the task written to close F-382, reviewed by the person whose finding F-382 was.** `run_save_benchmark`'s outcome bucketing is what keeps a refused save's near-instant timing out of `save_submitted_ms`, the series NFR-PRF-03's p95 is asserted against. Replace that bucketing with an unconditional push into the asserted series and the result is **94 passed, 0 failed — identical to control.** So a run in which every save was refused would post a **fast, clean p95** for saves that never reached the index, which is F-19's exact shape arriving in the instrument built to prevent it. **The doc comment three lines above the mutation names that failure and cites F-19 and F-382 as the reason the task exists. The reasoning is right and nothing pins it.** The single test that touches the path, `run_save_benchmark_attempts_every_listed_path_and_buckets_it_submitted`, exercises only the submitted path, so it cannot distinguish *"buckets correctly"* from *"buckets everything as submitted"* — F-58's shape. Fix is small and the pattern already exists in the tree: drive a save against a one-deep queue via `IndexHandle::with_queue_depth`, as F-115's `a_save_does_not_wait_for_the_writer` does, and assert the timing lands in `writer_refused_ms` **and is absent from** `submitted_ms`. **The transferable half is the reviewer's own method statement, and it is the strongest argument in the register for why review is not reading:** *"I was deliberately careful on this one because #111 implements a finding of mine, and approving my own suggestion back is the cheapest way for a review to be worth nothing. So I mutated rather than read, and the thing I would have been most tempted to wave through is the thing that turned out unguarded."* | code-reviewer, by mutation, against a task implementing their own finding | **Closed** — #111 merged; both mutations re-run red by code-reviewer (corrected per F-465: this cell read *blocks #94 and #95* long after both closed) |

| F-405 | **Cold start's batch-to-batch spread is ~19% on an attested-quiet machine, and unlike NFR-PRF-04's noise it is not explained by build contention.** Three `n=20` batches, same binary, same vault, same sitting, `concurrent_build_processes: 0` throughout, gave `window_visible` p95 of **1643.0 / 1876.5 / 1575.6 ms** — on top of roughly 97% *within*-batch spread. **#96 established that NFR-PRF-04's historical 35% drift was contention, and that an attested-unloaded machine gives a tight number (3.2% spread).** That explanation does not transfer here: this machine was attested quiet and the cold-start figure still moves ~19% between batches. **So the two budgets have different noise characters and the #96 result must not be generalised into "attestation makes figures stable."** Recorded as unresolved rather than papered over, and it bounds what any single cold-start figure — including #100's — can be said to establish. | test-engineer-3, three batches under attestation | Open — the cause is unidentified; #101 must report the spread beside the number |
| F-406 | **#112's `frame_precedes_paint` check cannot fire on this platform, and it was the author's own pre-committed condition that caught it — the escalation path written in advance, used as designed.** The check rejects when `fcp − raf2 > 200 ms`. | F-407 | **The term ADR-0014 made binding is measurable and the term it declined to budget is not — which defends §Decision 3 better than §Decision 3 defended itself.** #100 reports the aggregate scalars at **~97% within-batch and ~19% cross-batch** spread at zero build contention (F-405), while **`S`, the binding Serat term, moves about 8% on p95.** The split was argued from attribution — a term is ours if a change confined to this repository can move it. **It is now also justified by the instrument: the quantity the ADR budgets can be measured stably and the quantity it excludes cannot.** The consequence reaches past the ADR: **the aggregate scalars cannot serve as a gate in their current form whatever budget is set on them**, because a figure that moves 19% between attested batches cannot decide a pass. That is #101's problem to state and not the ADR's to solve. | architect, from #100's own spreads | Open — #101 must carry it; it bears on whether NFR-PRF-01 can be gated on aggregates at all |
| F-408 | **§1.2's central assumption was misattributed rather than merely untested, and the error is invisible until someone tries to test it: a structure that eliminates a phenomenon cannot also be evidence about it.** §1.2 said *"the single-writer index actor assumes write contention is the bottleneck."* **That claim cannot be tested here, ever** — it is a claim about what happens when writers contend, and the design permits **exactly one writer by construction**, so there is no contention to observe. No P0 measurement could have confirmed or refuted it and none was ever going to. **And the actor never rested on it.** §2.2's own justification is lock ordering, handle poisoning, a single queue, natural backpressure and crash isolation, and it states outright that a shared connection behind a mutex has **the same throughput** — so the actor is justified by *failure modes* rather than throughput, and **would stand unchanged if contention turned out to be irrelevant.** The honest closure is therefore neither "confirmed" nor "contradicted": **§1.2 misstated what its own structure depends on.** That is worth more than a verdict, because the same error — naming a justification a structure does not actually rest on — stays invisible until someone attempts the test and finds there is nothing to measure. **What P0 did establish is narrower and real:** the writer dominated at 45–51% `producer_wait`, and the cost was **internal rather than structural** — roughly a million redundant prepares per rebuild (F-324), fixed by `prepare_cached`, 27.1 s → 17.5 s, `producer_wait` down to 19%. That is evidence the **actor** was not the problem, and no evidence at all about the counterfactual it was nominated to answer. **§1.2's other two:** the parse pool is **contradicted and not built** (14–17% across two attested zero-contention sessions, written as a band rather than either point), and brute-force vector search at 200,000 chunks **stays open explicitly rather than by silence** — a P4 question on a P4 structure, ADR-0009 reversible, untested, and closing it now would be guessing. | architect, closing #103 | Closed — §1.2 closed, and the misattribution recorded rather than smoothed |

| F-410 | **A teammate cannot tell whether a name in the register refers to itself, and one stood down from its own assignment because a ruling named it in the third person.** The lead recorded *"`tauri-app-dev-3` owns `.worktrees/tauri-app` and its HEAD outright"*. The instance reading that commit **is** `tauri-app-dev-3`, and it concluded the ruling granted the worktree to a *different, possibly live* session — so it stood down from #111 and #113 to avoid a HEAD collision with itself, and offered to take a fresh worktree instead. **Every step of that reasoning was correct given what it could observe.** An agent has no reliable way to learn its own registered name: the spawn prompt says the role (`tauri-app-dev`), the register says the instance (`tauri-app-dev-3`), and nothing connects them. **The lead created the ambiguity by numbering instances in a shared document to disambiguate duplicates — a fix for one coordination failure that produced another.** The cheap remedy is to address the reader in the second person in any ruling meant for them (*"you own this worktree"*) and reserve instance numbers for describing sessions that are **not** the reader — or to state the instance name in the spawn prompt so a teammate can recognise itself. **Cost this time: one teammate idle on the wave's critical path while holding the assignment it was idle for.** | tauri-app-dev-3, by standing down from its own task; diagnosed by the lead | Open — remedy is a writing convention, applied from here |
| F-411 | **#106's remaining goal may not be buildable as specified, because `paint_evidence_ms` is on a different clock from everything it would be compared against.** F-389 left #106's third goal — comment in `crates/serat-app/src/startup.rs` says it is on the **browser's navigation-origin clock** and *"never compare this against another field in this struct"* — so `bench/budget/src/trace.rs` cannot diff it against `window_visible_ms` or `window_created_ms` to form a rejection without a clock anchor that does not currently exist. **F-406 is the same hazard one mechanism over**, and it is why this must not be attempted by picking a threshold: architect's `frame_precedes_paint` check turned out unable to fire because the honest gap sat below its own tolerance, and that was discovered by arithmetic after the check was written. **Ruling: scope it with architect before building anything, and if the answer is that no rejection is available without a clock anchor, record that as the closure rather than shipping a check that cannot fire.** A guard that cannot fire reads as coverage — the wave has now recorded ten of them. | test-engineer-3, declining to pick it up unilaterally | **Closed by architect's ruling, and the closure the lead offered was the wrong one.** *"The plumbing does not exist"* is comfortable and **false**: an exact cross-clock anchor is cheap — `performance.timeOrigin` is defined in **Unix-epoch milliseconds** and the harness already passes `SERAT_STARTUP_T0_UNIX_NANOS`, so one extra field reconciles every navigation-origin value to `t0` exactly. **The true closure is that the check could be built and would tell us nothing**, and it is F-406's arithmetic rather than a resemblance to it: the honest gap is **51–65 ms** (rAF fires on the renderer's schedule, Paint Timing measures the compositor's), the attack gap is bounded above by **FCP itself at ~184 ms**, so a separating threshold must sit around **65–130 ms** — against **F-405's ~19% batch-to-batch movement with the cause unidentified**. Three samples cannot bound a tail that narrow, so any threshold either fires on honest runs or never fires, **which is exactly how #112's 200 ms became inert.** **The specific harm of the comfortable version: it invites someone to build the anchor and believe they have solved it** — manufacturing an eleventh unfirable guard with extra plumbing attached. Two riders: **`raf1_ms`/`raf2_ms` do not help** (all four frontend fields are navigation-origin relative, so wiring F-403 moves four nav-clock numbers where there was one), and **the rejection belongs in Rust and already is there** — `frame_precedes_paint` compares FCP against `raf2` with both nav-relative and no anchor needed, so a harness-side version is the same check one process further from the evidence. **What test-engineer builds instead: report `fcp − raf2` as a diagnostic with no assertion on it.** It costs a column, it is the distribution nobody has — three samples is the entire evidence base — and it is the only thing that would ever make this check possible, so a future reader with thirty runs can decide what three could not. |

A mapped drive, a symlink, a UNC form, a differently-cased root on a case-insensitive filesystem, or a trailing separator all present a different `to_string_lossy()` for the same directory — and `open_or_rebuild` treats that as *the vault moved*. **The failure is silent, expensive and self-inflicted**: nothing is wrong, nothing reports an error, the index is simply discarded and rebuilt while the user waits, on a path they will hit again every time they open it that way. **C-02 says the index is disposable, which makes this legal and not free** — disposable means losing it is survivable, not that discarding a valid one on a spelling difference is correct.
