Delete it, or annotate it so the denial is visible at authoring time. | code-reviewer, task #102; **other half found and closed by architect**, applying F-469 to a finding filed against someone else's file | **Half closed, half open.** ADR-0012's own justification had decayed in two places and is corrected: *"`webview/boot.js` is the whole of the P0 front end"* was true of hand-authored source and has been Vite output since #98, and the promised *"revisit when the real front end lands"* came due at #98 and was never recorded as done. **The revisit's answer is still five commands**, verified against the shipped artifact at #102 rather than against source, and the ADR now says which. Event delivery for #117's push needs no addition and is recorded as **observed working in a real window rather than asserted**. Still open: the wrapper itself (vue-frontend-dev), and **`crates/serat-app/capabilities/default.json`'s description string names `boot.js` the same stale way** — tauri-app-dev's, flagged rather than edited. |
| F-468 | **Recorded as *not* a finding, because it read as the worst one available and is not: `tauri.conf.json`'s `frontendDist: "webview"` is correct and the shipped binary does load the Vue/CodeMirror bundle.** Neither #98 nor #108 changes that key, which reads as the binary loading the old static shell — **exactly the flattering cold-start measurement the 2026-09-08 ruling #1 exists to prevent**, and it would have undercut #100's numbers and the 350 ms Serat-term budget #107 gates on. It is not that: `app/vite.config.ts:29` sets `outDir` to `crates/serat-app/webview` with `emptyOutDir`, so `app/` builds **into** that directory and the `boot.js` there **is** the mounted frontend. **Kept on the finder's own reasoning — the configuration reads backwards at a glance and the next reviewer will trip on it too.** A near-miss costing one line to record is cheaper than a second investigation, and this register has spent the day proving the expensive failures are the ones that look already-settled. | code-reviewer, task #102, chased to the shipped artifact rather than stopping at the config | Closed — no defect; recorded so the next reader does not re-derive it |
| F-469 | **Five findings in two days are one pattern, and it has a rule cheap enough to follow: *after fixing a defect, ask what one-line query would have found it, run that query, and fix everything it returns.* Every instance was a correction scoped to the place the defect was REPORTED rather than to the class it belongs to — and in every instance the enumerating query already existed and was one line.** F-452 bounded the phase table and nobody swept the figures computed *from* it (**F-456**, nine sites; query: grep the table's own cell values). F-455's *median* was corrected in ADR-0014 and its twin in §3.4 survived the same session (**F-457**; query: `grep -n '5\.2' docs/03-design`). ADR-0014's basis question was answered in the artefact and stayed open in the tracking (**F-454**; query: grep the task list for the question you just answered). A branch's content was fully on main and its ref read as unmerged (**F-460**; query: grep main for a sentence from the branch's own commit message). A ratification promised that ADR-0002's text moved with it and ADR-0002 was untouched (**F-463**; query: `git show --stat`). **Second clause, and it is what the lead's withdrawal of F-463's ninth instance supplies: enumerate against the CONTENT, never against metadata about the content.** Every metadata channel this project uses has now been recorded stale in one direction or the other — commit ancestry, task-list status, a baseline stub's *"Objections recorded: none"*, and a commit message's own promises. **The lead consulted ancestry and read a merged branch as re-applied; I consulted ancestry one commit later and reported a recurrence that had not happened.** Same channel, opposite errors, neither careless: ancestry answers *which commits* and the question was always *which content*. **Where this does NOT reach, so it is not read as a theory of everything:** F-458 (a fix that was inert, which no query finds — only running it did) and F-462 (a quantity nobody had measured). **Those are the two failure modes a query cannot cover, and naming them is what keeps the rule a rule rather than a slogan.** **Third clause, learned by its own author getting it wrong in the lead's favour:** *check content against a **committed ref** — `git show REV:path` — never against the working file.* They ran the content check against the primary checkout while it was **mid-merge**, `.git/MERGE_HEAD` set and a conflict resolved but unstaged, and read the lead's in-flight working tree as committed state. **A working tree can be mid-operation, and in a shared checkout it is usually mid-*someone else's* operation.** That is the same failure as consulting ancestry, one layer down — *the right thing checked in the wrong place* — and it is the second time in one day that the **checking method** rather than the content was the defect, which is probably the more useful half of this finding. | architect, asked by the lead to find the unifying statement across F-454, F-456, F-457, F-460 and F-463 | Open as a convention — the rule is one line and belongs wherever the definition of done lives |
| F-470 | **A mutation that deletes the entire paint mark produces a clean, complete trace carrying real paint evidence — because the front end is embedded into `serat.exe` at compile time and `cargo build` never invokes vite.** Writing #121's run mechanics, the first thing to establish was the step where a run silently does nothing, and it is the build. `crates/serat-app/tauri.conf.json` sets `"frontendDist": "webview"` and has **no `beforeBuildCommand`**, so the webview assets are compiled *into* the binary rather than read from disk at launch. **Measured both ways rather than inferred from the config.** Replace `crates/serat-app/webview/boot.js` with a 60-byte stub that mounts nothing and never calls `mark_first_paint`, then launch the existing binary with no rebuild: **exit 0, complete trace, `paint_evidence_ms` 352.0, `t0_to_window_visible_ms` 1583.4.** Apply the same stub, run `cargo build --release -p serat-app`, launch again: **exit 2, no trace,** *"no window_visible mark: the webview never reported a first painted frame"*. **So the whole of #121 could have been run, reported clean, and discharged F-392 while testing nothing** — F-461's failure mode arriving on the very mechanism F-461 came from, and this time it would have been invisible rather than merely uninformative, because the trace it produces is indistinguishable from an honest one. **The scope is wider than #121.** Every check this project has run against a real window by changing front-end source has the same trap in front of it: F-392's own `boot.js` mutations, F-417/F-423's CSP work, and anything #121 spawns. A Rust-side mutation needs only `cargo build`; **a front-end mutation needs `npm ci`, `npm run build` and `cargo build --release`, in that order**, and `node_modules` is not checked in. **The cheap remedy is a positive control, and it costs one rebuild:** apply the stub, rebuild, confirm exit 2, restore. A run whose pipeline was never proven to reach the binary is not evidence. Recorded in `docs/05-testing/paint-guard-real-window-runs.md` as the document's first section for that reason. **Second-order note:** `vite.config.ts` sets `emptyOutDir: true` over `crates/serat-app/webview/`, whose three files are **committed build artifacts**, so an innocent rebuild can produce a diff with no source change — check `git status` and do not commit incidental regeneration. **SCOPE ASSESSED — the exposed class is EMPTY; nothing recorded is voided, and nothing reaching the shipped CSP rests on an unrebuilt observation.** Two narrowings, both better than the answer. **(1) The boundary is the observation channel, not the toolchain.** npm/vitest are safe because they read source — but `cargo test` is **not** automatically safe: `crates/serat-app/src/lib.rs:576-588` reads `webview/boot.js` off disk with `read_to_string`, so a bundle mutation *is* visible there with no rebuild. The discriminator is **does the observer read the file that was mutated, or a compiled copy of it?** **(2) F-470 can only produce false *negatives*, and this bounds the whole corpus without reconstructing anything.** Mutate without rebuilding and you observe *unmutated* behaviour — the mutation looks harmless, a false pass. **Fix** without rebuilding and you observe *unfixed* behaviour — the fix looks broken and you investigate. So the exposed class is only *claims whose conclusion rests on the **absence** of an observed effect in the running binary after a front-end edit*; **a claim that observed its expected effect is self-verifying**, because seeing the effect proves the edit reached the binary. **The list:** F-392 observed through `cargo test` against `boot.js` on disk, never the binary — and per F-464 the real-window run was never performed, so there is nothing to void; F-393/F-406/F-430 measured *unmutated* binaries; F-417 is marked *reasoned, not measured* by its own finder; F-423(a)(b) and F-436 are in the class with **the rebuild stated in their own text** (*"Built the unfixed tree"*, *"reproduced twice on fresh builds"*); F-425 caught its defect by observing it. **Nothing unreconstructable, nothing void.** The CSP is safe **by construction** — it compiles from `tauri.conf.json`, so an unrebuilt observation of it is impossible rather than merely unlikely. **And the remedy is not new machinery: F-470 is F-433's sibling by a different mechanism** — there the check watched the wrong observable, here it watches a stale binary — and **F-436 closed the first with a negative control**, which is what #121's runbook now mandates under another name. *Reconstruct the broken state deliberately and watch the check catch it* was proven in this register three findings earlier. | tauri-app-dev, task #121, measured while writing the run mechanics | Open — the remedy is a positive control in the procedure, not a code change |
| F-471 | **F-469 covers claims that go false; this is the class that goes *stale while still reading as pending*, and it is worse because it looks like tracking. Three live instances, found by one query in the design corpus after the lead named the gap -- and the third was found by the rule's own instruction to fix everything the query returns, which is the closest thing here to evidence that the mechanism works rather than merely sounds right.** *(1)* **ADR-0014 §Decision 1** says the sum identity is *"checkable in principle today and in fact only after task #106"* and that the harness *"parses four"* marks so the Serat term *"is not"* computable. **#106 is DONE**: seven marks are required and the identity is asserted. The same ADR's §Objections already records that, so **the document contradicted itself for as long as both paragraphs stood.** *(1b)* **architecture §3.4 said the same thing in the mirrored paragraph** -- *"It cannot assert it yet ... Task #106 closes that"* -- so the defect had a twin one document over, which is **F-457 arriving in a class that did not exist when F-457 was written**. *(2)* **threat-model.md** says Windows coverage of T-07 is *"unavailable until #70 lands"*. **#70 landed at `f34edb7`.** Coverage is still unavailable — for F-384, no CI jobs at all — so **the conclusion survived while its stated reason was discharged**, which is the more dangerous of the two failures: a clause naming a discharged condition reads as *tracked and pending* when it is neither, and nobody re-examines a sentence that appears to be waiting. **Why no existing guard catches these.** They are not false when written and not false-looking afterwards; `doclint` checks that cited paths and IDs resolve, and both of these resolve perfectly. F-469's rule does not reach them either, because there is no defect report to generalise from — nothing was found, a deadline simply passed. **The mechanism is cheap and is the same shape as F-469's:** a revisit-promise must name the discharging event as a **task number**, so that merging task N is paired with `grep -rn '#N' docs/` — one line, at the moment the information exists, by the person who has it. `grep -rn '#106' docs/` on the day #106 merged returns the stale paragraph. **Both instances were in my own documents and neither was found by reading them.** | architect, running F-469 forward from the lead's observation that a promise to revisit is a claim with a date on it | Open — two instances fixed; the convention needs a home in the definition of done |
| F-NEXT/real-binary-run-that-could-not-fail | **A real-binary verification of #119 returned a clean result on a binary that did not have the fix, and only the control revealed it — `wait_for_usable` is gated on a webview paint that lands about a second after the index settles.** Asked to confirm #119 against the compiled shell rather than against a scripted opener, I ran the post-fix binary on a warm 4,000-note scratch vault ten times: **10/10 clean, 2,010 saves, `handle_missing` 0**. Read alone that is confirmation. Run on the **pre-#119 binary it is identical** — 10/10, 2,010 saves, zero — which is the tell, because the pre-fix binary provably has the window. **The cause is in the trace and is general.** `t_usable` is the max of four marks, and on a warm vault `index_settled` lands at **99–387 ms** while `buffer_rendered` — a webview IPC round trip — lands at **923–1,565 ms**, so `buffer_rendered` is the binding term in every run and `--bench-save-list`'s first save happens **764–1,178 ms after the index settled**. The window under test is tens of microseconds wide. **Any harness released by `wait_for_usable` is therefore structurally incapable of observing anything that happens near the index settling**, and will report a clean run whether or not the defect is present. **This does not invalidate #95 or #94.** NFR-PRF-03's words are *a note saved through Serat's own write path*, and a save taken a second after the index is ready satisfies that exactly; what is bounded is not the number but the class of question such a harness can answer. **The transferable half is the method, and it is the mutation rule one level up:** a real-binary run needs a control on the *unfixed* binary for the same reason a test needs a mutation, and this project has thirteen guards-that-cannot-fail on record precisely because a green result was read without one. I had already drafted the 10/10 as confirmation before running the control. **Second consequence, worth its own look:** every save path the shipped application has today — user edit, and AI/MCP write through the review queue (C-05) — is downstream of a painted window, so **no caller that exists can reach F-382's window**; #119 removed a latent ordering hazard rather than a defect anyone was hitting, and that distinction should survive into the gate report. | tauri-app-dev, task #119, real-binary verification; caught by its own control | Open — the method (control on the unfixed binary) is the finding; no change to #94/#95's numbers is implied |
| F-NEXT/f382-window-measured | **F-382's window, measured at last: 20–72 µs warm, 113–191 µs cold — not "instructions", and four orders of magnitude below the multi-second window F-449 attributed to it.** #119 chose to close the window rather than bound it, on the argument that a measured width would be a property of one machine; the real-binary task made a bound cheap to take anyway, so here it is. A throwaway instrumented **release** build of the pre-#119 binary timed, on the actor thread, from just before the terminal state is published to the instant `into_handle()` fills the slot, against a real 4,000-note vault: **warm median 35.4 µs, range 19.8–72.0 µs (n=10); cold 112.8, 156.0, 191.2 µs (n=3).** **Why it is microseconds rather than instructions, which is the part F-382 could not have known by reading:** the waiter is woken *inside* `StatusPublisher::publish`, at the observer, and `sink.emit` — a Tauri IPC emit to the webview — then runs **before `publish` returns and before the slot is filled**. The window is dominated by an IPC emit, not by instruction count, which is why it is ~1000× wider than F-382 assumed and still ~40,000× narrower than the figure F-449 read into it. **F-450's arithmetic argument is now confirmed numerically:** an explanation worth 35 µs cannot produce a 1000/1000 over a multi-second rebuild. **The same instrumentation on the post-#119 binary measures the inverse** — slot fill precedes terminal publish by **0.2–1.4 µs in all 13 runs** (10 warm, 3 cold) — which is #119's ordering claim confirmed by direct observation of the compiled shell rather than of a scripted opener. **Stated bound, per the standing condition:** these are manual runs on one machine with instrumentation that was **never committed**; no automated test asserts the 35 µs, and the committed test asserts only the *ordering*, which it does deterministically. | tauri-app-dev, task #119, instrumented throwaway build | Open — a recorded bound, not a defect; supersedes F-382's "instructions, not milliseconds" characterisation |
| F-473 | **`is_terminal`'s `Unavailable` arm is unpinned: making `Unavailable` publish immediately instead of being deferred leaves all 108 tests green.** No live defect — on that path `into_handle()` returns `None`, so there is no handle to race. **The gap is between the function's doc and its tests:** `is_terminal` asserts uniformity across terminal states (*"its `IndexHandle`, if it produced one, exists"*) that nothing holds it to, and simplifying the function to just `Ready` keeps the suite green. **Any future opener that publishes `Unavailable` while still yielding a handle reopens F-382's window silently** — the fix that closed it would stop covering the case it was written to cover, with no test saying so. Sixteenth in the guards-that-cannot-fail family and a four-row table over `is_terminal` closes it. | code-reviewer, task #119 review, by mutation | Open — SHOULD-FIX; tauri-app-dev, with #119 already merged |
| F-474 | **T1's equivalence snapshots guard three of six series against the empty-versus-empty trap and leave `blocks`, `fields` and `tasks` unguarded.** The file's own comment names the trap — *"an equivalence test over two empty snapshots proves nothing"* — and `notes`, `links` and `fts_hits` are held to non-emptiness. Hollowing each series in turn so both snapshots return empty: **tasks GREEN, fields GREEN, blocks GREEN, notes RED** (control 2/0). Latent rather than live, since `seed()` populates all six today. **The reason it must close is a limit of equivalence testing in general: a defect affecting both builds identically is invisible to it.** If the parser stopped emitting tasks, both snapshots would be empty and T1 would report equivalence over a table nothing was populating — and T1 is the evidence C-02 and NFR-REL-02 rest on. The non-emptiness assertions are what make it the stronger claim. Three lines. | code-reviewer, reviewing T1 **after** it had merged, and finding it worth doing | Open — SHOULD-FIX; rust-core-dev |
| F-475 | **The pre-warm's `?` at `bench/budget/src/subject.rs:610` is unpinned — and it is a SHOULD-FIX only because `handle_missing` is a *hard zero*, which makes the two one decision rather than two.** A failed or skipped pre-warm cannot reach a reported figure: the saves land in the cold-rebuild window, `handle_missing` goes non-zero, and `check_save_completeness` returns `Err` **before any `Outcome` exists** — so there is no figure to report, not merely a failing one. The hard zero is itself mutation-pinned (RED 93/2). **And the experiment has already been run: F-458 *is* this scenario.** The pre-warm was wholly ineffective under the harness's path spelling, and the instrument answered with `handle_missing` 25 of 25 rather than a number — a broken pre-warm observed degrading to a refusal rather than to a fast figure. **The coupling is the finding.** If anyone later relaxes `handle_missing` to a threshold or a warning — *a tempting change the first time a run dies on a residual of three* — this SHOULD-FIX becomes **blocking that same day**, because a swallowed pre-warm failure would then reach a figure. | code-reviewer, task #94, by mutation at `d30aa34` | Open — SHOULD-FIX; **must be re-classified as blocking if the `handle_missing` hard zero is ever softened** |
| F-476 | **F-435 is closed, and the mechanism is not what its own remedy proposed.** F-435 asked that *"the run completed"* and *"the asserted series is the size the criterion requires"* become the same check. No sample-count check exists. What exists is two mutation-pinned links: `measure_save_to_index_with` sets `Measurement::degraded` whenever `trace.writer_refused > 0` (`lib.rs:2018`; mutating the condition to `if false` → **RED 94/1**), and `Outcome::passed()` is `degraded.is_none() && value <= limit` (`lib.rs:574-576`; removing the `degraded` clause → **RED 93/2**). **F-435 was right about everything except the consequence:** 990 refused and 10 submitted does satisfy conservation, `handle_missing` is zero, and the p95 *is* computed over the ten fastest samples. What it cannot do is **pass** — the refusal sets `degraded`, `degraded` fails `passed()` independently of the timing, and the reason travels with the figure in both `to_report` (`DEGRADED`) and `to_json`. **The mechanism is not that the flattering p95 is suppressed; it is that it cannot go green and cannot be quoted without the sentence saying why. Fail the number, not the run.** | code-reviewer, task #94, re-derived by mutation rather than quoted from their own earlier claim — *"a claim I made three hours ago against a different tip is exactly the implicit clearance F-465 is about"* | Closed — both links load-bearing and pinned; F-435's *must close before #95 runs* status was stale |
| F-477 | **Committed build output is where a weakening would hide, and it is the one place this register's main defence stops working.** The standing dirty-worktree rule — *ask of every hunk whether it is a deliberate weakening* — **assumes hunks are legible.** Against 347 KB of minified committed bundle it silently stops working: a stub replacing `crates/serat-app/webview/boot.js` reads as one line against a wall of unreadable text, and in a commit it is **indistinguishable from a legitimate rebuild**. F-383's probe was readable Rust and was seen; this one would not have been. **Demonstrated rather than argued:** a 60-byte stub that mounts nothing and never calls `mark_first_paint` was found live in a worktree by the lead watching diffs — it was a deliberate probe, twice, minutes each, reverted both times, and no commit on either branch touches that directory (`git diff --name-only origin/main...HEAD -- crates/serat-app/webview/` returns zero files on both). **The detection path is the actionable half:** it was caught by someone watching worktree diffs, and there was **no way to signal *probe in flight*** — nothing in the repository would have said so. **Remedy taken:** the #121 runbook gains a mandatory teardown — restore, **rebuild**, `git status --porcelain` over the *whole* worktree, and `git diff --name-only origin/main...HEAD -- crates/serat-app/webview/` **before any commit**, which is the load-bearing one, because *a clean working tree over a bad commit is exactly what "I reverted it" feels like from the inside*. **Remedy named and deliberately not taken:** a CI check that `webview/` matches what `npm run build` reproduces from `app/src/` would make a hand-edited bundle impossible rather than discouraged — left unclaimed as test-engineer's ground, and now live as **#110's unblocked half**, which F-470 promoted from hygiene to load-bearing. | tauri-app-dev, on their own probe, raised rather than quietly reverted | Open — teardown landed at `bba6571`; the structural remedy is #110 |
| F-478 | **A freeze is a message, and a message in flight does not stop a build already being planned — twice in ten minutes, by two different teammates, neither at fault.** The lead declared the build window for #95 and within minutes two roles ran full `cargo test` suites and a release rebuild, because both were mid-task when the declaration arrived and had no reason to re-read a file they had read twenty minutes earlier. **The banner is the authority and the banner is pull, not push.** This is the same gap as F-477's *probe in flight*, from the other side: both are state that exists only in someone's head, with no channel that reaches a working teammate. **What makes it survivable is that the freeze was never the guarantee** — `concurrent_build_processes` in the run's own attestation is, and #83 established it and #96's whole verdict rested on it. **The freeze improves the odds; the attestation is what makes the number defensible, and where the two disagree the attestation wins.** Recorded so that a future reader does not mistake the freeze for the control. | lead, from two crossings in one window | Open — no mechanism proposed; the attestation is the existing control and it held |
| F-479 | **Pre-registered prediction, recorded before #121 can run and therefore before it can be fitted to a result: F-392's stated conclusion may be false, and #101 cites F-392 as a gate-report input.** F-392 concludes that *"the load-bearing mechanism caught both mutations independently of the structural test"*. Reading the code during the freeze, code-reviewer predicts the **sharp** form of mutation B survives the runtime mechanism entirely. **The chain, all established by reading and each citable:** `Startup::mark_first_paint` is **first-wins** (`startup.rs:391-393`) and sets `window_visible` to `self.elapsed_ms()` **at the moment the call arrives**, not to the value passed — so a hoisted call does not add noise, it **claims** the mark and moves `window_visible` earlier by exactly the shell's real paint time, which *improves* the number. The real guard is narrow: `!first_contentful_paint_ms.is_finite() \|\| <= 0.0` (`startup.rs:386-388`), so F-392's **canonical** form (`firstContentfulPaintMs: 0`) is rejected on the zero and goes red. But a hoisted call passing a **plausible positive** — `firstContentfulPaintMs: 1, raf1Ms: 0, raf2Ms: 1` — clears finiteness, clears `> 0.0`, clears the strict `raf2 > raf1` frame gate, and reaches `Ok`; and F-406 demoted `frame_precedes_paint` to a non-rejecting diagnostic, so nothing downstream refuses it. **The Rust side cannot distinguish a hardcoded `1` from a genuine `first-contentful-paint` of `1`** — F-392's load-bearing mechanism is a property of *the browser* (Paint Timing cannot report an entry before the frame is composited), and a mutation that bypasses the API and hardcodes the number is not bound by it. **If the prediction holds, what actually stops mutation B on `main` today is the structural test asserting exactly one `invoke("mark_first_paint"` in `boot.js`** — a textual check at `cargo test` level, not the runtime mechanism — which is the opposite of what F-392 records and of the family of source-shape guards this project has twice ruled against relying on. **The finder states plainly they may be wrong and have not run it**, which is why it is on record *before* the run. | code-reviewer, during the freeze, pre-registering predictions they cannot yet execute | **Open — prediction, not result.** Resolves at #121; if it holds, F-392's conclusion is corrected and #101's citation of it changes |
| F-NEXT/f470-voids-nothing-recorded | **F-470's exposed class is narrower than "frontend mutations" in two ways, and once both are applied it contains no recorded claim that lacks a rebuild — nothing is voided.** Assessed from the record only, under the build freeze; nothing was re-run, and nothing needed to be. *(a) The boundary is the observation channel, not the toolchain.* The lead's reading was that npm/vitest work against source and is therefore unaffected — right, but the reason generalises further and `cargo test` is **not** automatically on the safe side of it. `crates/serat-app/src/lib.rs:576-588` reads `crates/serat-app/webview/boot.js` off disk with `std::fs::read_to_string`, so a `boot.js` mutation **is** visible to `cargo test` with no rebuild. The discriminator is: **does the observer read the file that was mutated, or a compiled copy of it?** vitest reads source (safe), the Rust bundle test reads the mutated file itself (safe), anything that launches `serat.exe` reads the embedded copy (exposed). *(b) F-470 can only produce false negatives, and this is what bounds it.* Mutate without rebuilding and you observe **unmutated** behaviour — the mutation looks harmless, a false pass. **Fix** without rebuilding and you observe **unfixed** behaviour — the fix looks broken, and you investigate. So the exposed class is only *claims whose conclusion rests on the **absence** of an observed effect in the running binary after a front-end edit*; a claim that observed its **expected effect** is self-verifying, because seeing the effect is proof the edit reached the binary. That asymmetry disposes of most of the corpus without reconstructing anything. *(c) The list, and it is empty.* **F-392** — mutations observed through `cargo test` against `boot.js` on disk and the Rust-side mechanism tests, never through the binary; and per F-464 the real-window run was never performed at all, so there is nothing to void. **F-393, F-406, F-430** — measurements of *unmutated* binaries; nothing was mutated. **F-417** — explicitly *"reasoned, not measured"*, marked so by its finder. **F-423(a)(b)** — in the class and **rebuild confirmed in the recorded text**: *"Built the unfixed tree"* and *"Re-measured against the rebuilt binary"*, both presence-of-effect observations besides. **F-425** — caught its defect by observing it, presence-of-effect, self-verifying. **F-436** — in the class and **rebuild confirmed in both arms**: *"rebuilt with the pre-fix CSP"* and *"reproduced twice on fresh builds"*. **No claim is unreconstructable, and none is void.** *(d) The severity question, answered directly: nothing touching the shipped CSP rests on an unrebuilt observation.* F-423 records the rebuild in both arms, F-436 records it in both arms **and** adds a negative control, and the CSP string is compiled from `tauri.conf.json`, so an unrebuilt observation of it is impossible by construction rather than merely unlikely. *(e) The convergence worth keeping.* **F-433 is F-470's sibling by a different mechanism** — there the check could not discriminate because it watched the wrong observable (`--exit-after-ready` reaches `t_usable` through an `invoke` while the broken call was `listen()`); here it cannot discriminate because it watches a stale binary. **F-436 closed the first with a negative control, and F-470's remedy in #121's runbook is the same practice under another name.** So the remedy is not new machinery: *reconstruct the broken state deliberately and watch the check catch it* already exists in this register, proven, three findings earlier. | tauri-app-dev, task F-470 follow-up, from the record alone under the freeze | Open — informational; F-470's remedy stands, its blast radius is nil |
| F-480 | **Pre-registered before #118 can run: the change concentrates every budget's enforcement into one three-line conditional, and neutralising it makes C-06 decorative for all three gated budgets at once while every job still prints `PASS` and exits 0.** `if outcome.gates() { ok &= outcome.passed() && outcome.determinate(); }` (`lib.rs:1727`) is now where NFR-PRF-01's Serat term, NFR-PRF-03 and NFR-PRF-04 all become real. Mutate the condition to `if false` and **every job still runs, still measures, still writes its JSON, still prints a verdict — and nothing in a CI log looks different.** **Before #118 the ungated case did not exist and there was no `gates()` to short-circuit**, so this is a single point of failure the improvement itself created — *and the two facts are not in tension*: it means this line now deserves the scrutiny the five budget constants used to share. **The precedent is exact: F-27 recorded four mutations of this same harness invisible to all nine of its tests**, one of them removing `run_job`'s no-samples branch — same file, same class, same author-blind spot. **If E4 or E5 survives it is BLOCKING regardless of the rest of #118**, because it is the difference between budgets that are binding and budgets that are decorative. **Recorded alongside the praise it belongs with, not instead of it.** The obvious way to demote two figures is to delete their assertions — the version that quietly stops measuring them a refactor later. #118 instead makes `gated` a *field*, `PRF01_REPORTED` a real list so *"reported" is a place rather than an absence*, keeps the full measurement and vault verification and changes only the verdict, and adds a partition test that checks flag against lists **in both directions**, refuses an id in both, and pins the exhaustive set of five — **so a sixth budget added to neither list fails there rather than being silently unmeasured.** That is F-443 closed one layer down rather than reproduced. **E3 is a re-test of a closed finding:** F-81 recorded `run_scale_check`'s call being droppable with nothing going red, and a green E3 is a reopened F-81. | code-reviewer, pre-registering during the freeze what they cannot yet execute | **Open — predictions, not results.** Resolves when #118's five run against a green banner |

The guard is `<= 0.0`, so it rejects F-392's **canonical** mutation B (`firstContentfulPaintMs: 0`), which is exactly why this reads as covered. **A plausible positive is not covered:** `firstContentfulPaintMs: 1, raf1Ms: 0, raf2Ms: 1` clears finiteness, clears `> 0.0`, clears the strict frame gate, and F-406 demoted `frame_precedes_paint` to non-rejecting. Because `mark_first_paint` is **first-wins**, that hoisted call *claims* `window_visible` at `elapsed_ms()` on arrival — script start — and every later honest call returns `Ok(false)` silently, producing a **complete, clean trace with `window_visible` earlier by the shell's entire real paint time.** F-377's original attack, with nothing textual and nothing runtime refusing it. **Status stated honestly by its finder:** the *absence* is verified; the *consequence* is traced through code and **not run**, because builds are frozen. It is mutation B's sharp form, **pre-registered in F-479 before this was found** — so #121 settles it, and a green there means F-392 reopens on its own merits rather than being closed by that run. **The bundle happens to contain exactly one occurrence today** (lead checked), so the property holds and nothing asserts it — which is the definition of the gap. **#101 must not record F-392 as discharged on the strength of a count assertion that is not there.** | code-reviewer, during the freeze, by running F-469's own one-line query — `grep -c 'mark_first_paint' boot.js` | Open — **BLOCKING for #101's citation of F-392**; resolves at #121 |
| F-482 | **F-406's demotion silently reopened F-396(b), and `validate_paint_evidence`'s own doc now affirms the opposite of what it does — in the one place a reader would look before adding a splash screen.** The doc (`startup.rs:870-876`) claims *"checking the frame timestamps against the paint entry makes this guard's soundness independent of what `index.html` happens to contain today"*, and names the consequence if that failed: a loading skeleton *"would silently degrade an FCP-only guard to F-377's mutation B with nothing going red."* **Lead verified the body: the only rejection is `!raf1.is_finite() \|\| !raf2.is_finite() \|\| raf2 <= raf1` — the two frame timestamps compared *to each other*, never to the paint entry.** The sole check that ever compared frames against the paint entry was `frame_precedes_paint`, and **F-406 demoted it to a non-rejecting diagnostic** (`eprintln!` and nothing else). **So the sentence was true when #112 wrote it and became false when F-406 landed.** The guard is FCP-only again, its stated dependency on `index.html` staying contentless is live again, and nothing asserts that property. **This compounds F-481 rather than duplicating it — two mechanisms converging on one uncovered case.** F-481's route: the call-site count assertion is gone, so a *second* `mark_first_paint` call site beside the good one is textually invisible. This route: the runtime guard no longer corroborates the paint entry, so a call carrying a **plausible-positive** FCP is accepted on its own say-so — and `mark_first_paint` being **first-wins**, an early one claims `window_visible` and silently no-ops the honest call. **F-377's mutation B is therefore uncovered textually *and* at runtime, and each gap is invisible because each has a doc comment pointing at the other as the thing that covers it:** `commands.rs:93` points at `validate_paint_evidence`; `validate_paint_evidence` claims an independence it no longer has; `lib.rs:570` says the count check moved somewhere it did not go. **Not an argument that F-406 was wrong** — its reasoning was sound and F-444 records it landing properly pinned. **The gap is that F-406 asked *"can this check ever fire?"* and correctly answered no, and nobody asked *"what cited this check as load-bearing?"*** That is F-469 applied to a **demotion** rather than a fix: *when you demote a check to a diagnostic, re-run the query for everything that depended on it firing.* **Remedies, both small:** correct the sentence under F-442's anchor convention, and add the assertion nothing has ever had — that the shipped `index.html` body carries no rendering content beyond the mount point, a `read_to_string` in the test that already reads `boot.js`. | code-reviewer, during the freeze, by reading the function body against its own doc | Open — **#101 must not record F-396(b) as discharged**; #121's mutation B settles this and F-481 together |
